Harish-Chandra isomorphism for cyclotomic double affine Hecke algebras
Joshua Jeishing Wen

Abstract
. We confirm a conjecture of Braverman–Etingof–Finkelberg that the spherical subalgebra of their cyclotomic double affine Hecke algebra (DAHA) is isomorphic to a quantized multiplicative quiver variety for the cyclic quiver, as defined by Jordan. The isomorphism is constructed as a 𝗊 \mathsf{q} -analogue of Oblomkov’s cyclotomic radial parts map for the rational case. In the appendix, we also prove that the spherical cyclotomic DAHA is isomorphic to the image of a shifted quantum toroidal algebra under Tsymbaliuk’s GKLO homomorphism.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 20C08, 33D52, 53D55; Secondary: 16G20.

1.
Introduction

This paper aims to shine a speck of light on the largely-unexplored world of integrable systems of Ruijsenaars–Schneider type for complex reflection groups.
The Hamiltonians for the quantizations of such systems should be
\mathsf{q}
-difference operators.
For Calogero–Moser systems and their generalizations to
real
reflection groups, the Olshanetsky–Perelomov systems
[
OP76
]
, the quantum Hamiltonians are instead differential operators, and their study can be facilitated by
Dunkl operators

[
Dun89
]
.
The latter are commuting permutation-differential operators with explicit formulas, and one obtains the quantum Calogero–Moser–Olshanetsky–Perelomov Hamiltonians by symmetrizing them.

Thus, one can define Calogero–Moser systems for complex reflection groups by generalizing the Dunkl operators, which was done by Dunkl–Opdam
[
DO03
]
.
Moreover, an entire algebraic structure for studying such systems can be generalized: these are the
rational Cherednik algebras
.
The double affine Hecke algebra (DAHA) was originally defined by Cherednik for Weyl groups, who applied it with great success to solving Macdonald’s conjectures
[
Che95a
,
Che95b
]
.
In type
A
, the DAHA provides the algebraic structure for the trigonometric Ruijsenaars–Schneider system; its rational degenerations, which includes the rational Cherednik algebra, in turn yielded many new insights to Calogero–Moser systems.
Rational Cherednik algebras for complex reflection groups were defined by Etingof–Ginzburg within the larger context of
symplectic reflection algebras

[
EG02
]
, and the cases for certain groups have been studied extensively.

One should be careful in removing the “rational” in “rational Cherednik algebra for complex reflection groups” because the DAHA for such groups has yet to be defined.
While Hecke algebras have been defined in this generality
[
BMR98
]
, the naive definition of an affine Hecke algebra requires the group to preserve a lattice, which is only true in the real case.
Thus, it is not straightforward to define such an object.
For the group
\Sigma_{n}\wr\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}
, Braverman–Etingof–Finkelberg
[
BEF20
]
gave a definition of
cyclotomic DAHA
that sidesteps the issue.
Their cyclotomic DAHA is like the subalgebra obtained from a fictional DAHA for
\Sigma_{n}\wr\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}
by sphericalizing away the cyclic group factors; in fact, they proved that their cyclotomic DAHA degenerates to the analogous such subalgebra for the rational Cherednik algebra.

It is thus sensible to expect results for the spherical subalgebra in the rational case to lift to the cyclotomic DAHA, and this paper confirms one of these expectations, conjectured in
[
BEF20
]
.
Namely, in the rational case, results of Oblomkov
[
Obl07
]
and Gordon
[
Gor06
]
combine to show that the spherical subalgebra of the rational Cherednik algebra for
\Sigma_{n}\wr\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}
is isomorphic to a
quantized Nakajima quiver variety
(cf.
[
Nak98
]
) for the
\widetilde{A}_{\ell-1}
quiver.
This isomorphism opened the door to the geometric representation theory of the cyclotomic rational Cherednik algebra
[
Gor08
]
, which in turn was crucial in the proof of Haiman’s wreath Macdonald positivity conjecture
[
BF14
,
Hai03
]
.

For the cyclotomic DAHA, the candidate replacement for the Nakajima quiver variety is the
multiplicative
quiver variety (cf.
[
CBS06
]
), also for the
\widetilde{A}_{\ell-1}
quiver.
The multiplicative quiver varieties for such quivers were studied by Chalykh–Fairon
[
CF17
]
as phase spaces for generalized Ruijsenaars–Schneider integrable systems.
Their quantizations were defined by Jordan
[
Jor14
]
, although the case
\ell=1
requires a new definition, given in this paper.
The cyclotomic DAHA depends on three parameters
(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
.
Our main result is:

Main Theorem
.

The spherical cyclotomic DAHA for
\Sigma_{n}\wr\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}
is isomorphic to a quantized multiplicative quiver variety for the
\widetilde{A}_{\ell-1}
quiver (
4.1
) when:

•

\mathsf{t}=\mathsf{q}^{k}
for
k>2n\ell
and
\mathsf{q}
is generic

•

or
(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
is generic.

In their work
[
CF17
]
, Chalykh–Fairon identified three families of Poisson-commuting functions, and an analogous three families of commuting elements exist in the spherical cyclotomic DAHA.
Our isomorphism takes manifest quantizations of the former and sends them to the latter.

The proof of the main theorem basically follows that of the rational case; in fact, perhaps the biggest surprise of this paper is that there are no surprises.
Neither algebra in the theorem has a presentation via generators and relations, so the principal difficulty comes from constructing a homomorphism from one to the other.
We do this by constructing a
\mathsf{q}
-analogue of Oblomkov’s cyclotomic radial parts map
[
Obl07
]
.
To do so, it is important to have an analogous isomorphism established between the usual (non-cyclotomic) spherical DAHA of
GL_{n}
and the a quantized multiplicative quiver variety for the Jordan quiver (single vertex with a loop), which was proved in our earlier work
[
Wen23
]
.
The cyclotomic DAHA is a subalgebra of
GL_{n}
-DAHA, and we define an embedding from the quantized
\widetilde{A}_{\ell-1}
quiver variety into the quantized Jordan quiver variety; our radial parts map is then just the restriction of the isomorphism from
[
Wen23
]
to these subalgebras.

Despite the lack of surprises, this paper can get technical.
Oblomkov’s radial parts map is defined using natural constructions on rings of differential operators: localization, restriction, etc.
In our
\mathsf{q}
-deformed setting, there is a shortage of functors and thus we must build these homomorphisms by hand.
Additionally, showing that the sphericalized cyclotomic
\mathsf{q}
-Dunkl elements of
[
BEF20
]
lie in the image of the radial parts map requires some tricks; for this, we found much inspiration from the manipulations in
[
DFK19
]
involving the Gaussian.
We also mention that, as in
[
Wen23
]
, our radial parts map relies on the Etingof–Kirillov realization of Macdonald polynomials via intertwiners for
U_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})

[
EK94
]
.
The beautiful idea to use the Etingof–Kirillov realization comes from
[
VV10
]
.

Finally, we mention that in the setting of
3d mirror symmetry
, multiplicative quiver varieties are multiplicative
Higgs branches
.
The authors of
[
BEF20
]
also conjectured an isomorphism between the spherical cyclotomic DAHA and a quantized
K
-theoretic Coulomb branch

[
BFN18
,
FT19a
,
FT19b
]
.
In the appendix, we show that the spherical cyclotomic DAHA is isomorphic to a
truncated shifted quantum toroidal algebra

[
Tsy23
]
.
The latter algebras are generally expected to be isomorphic to quantized
K
-theoretic Coulomb branches.

Notation

With the intent of providing clarity, we have sought to assign symbols according to the following principles:

•

ground rings are denoted using blackboard bold (
\mathbb{C}
,
\mathbb{L}
,
\mathbb{K}
);

•

more complicated algebras are denoted using calligraphic letters (
\mathcal{R}
,
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
);

•

parameters are denoted using sans-serif fonts: (
\mathsf{q}
,
\mathsf{t}
,
\mathsf{Z}_{i}
);

•

most DAHA elements are denoted using capital Roman letters (
T_{i}
,
X_{i}
,
Y_{i}
);

•

matrices will be denoted using bold capital letters (
\mathbf{X}
,
\mathbf{Y}
,
\mathbf{D}
).

2.
Cyclotomic DAHA

In what follows, we will work over the ring
\mathbb{L}:=\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{q}^{\pm 1},\mathsf{t}^{\pm 1}]
and its field of fractions
\mathbb{K}:=\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
.

2.1.
\mathbf{GL_{n}}
-DAHA

The cyclotomic DAHA sits inside the usual DAHA of
GL_{n}
.
We begin by reviewing the definition and structures of the latter that will be relevant to our study.
Our reference is
[
Che05
]
, although our conventions will be closer to those of
[
Kir97
]
.

2.1.1.
Definition of
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
and PBW

The
GL_{n}
-DAHA
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
is the
\mathbb{K}
-algebra with generators

\{T_{i},X_{j}^{\pm 1},\pi^{\pm 1}\,|\,i=1,\ldots,n-1\hbox{ and }j=1,\ldots,n\}

and relations

\displaystyle(T_{i}-\mathsf{t})(T_{i}+\mathsf{t}^{-1})=0;

\displaystyle X_{i}X_{j}=X_{j}X_{i};

\displaystyle T_{i}T_{i+1}T_{i}=T_{i+1}T_{i}T_{i+1};

\displaystyle T_{i}T_{j}=T_{j}T_{i}\hbox{ for }j\not=i,i+1;

\displaystyle T_{i}X_{j}=X_{j}T_{i}\hbox{ for }j\not=i,i+1;

\displaystyle T_{i}X_{i}T_{i}=X_{i+1};

\displaystyle\pi T_{i}=T_{i+1}\pi;

\displaystyle\pi^{n}T_{i}=T_{i}\pi^{n};

\displaystyle\pi X_{i}=X_{i+1}\pi;

\displaystyle\pi X_{n}=\mathsf{q}^{-2}X_{1}\pi

The extra superscript “
0
” is to place it in a family with the cyclotomic algebras, to be introduced in
2.2
below.
We can also define this as an
\mathbb{L}
-algebra, which we denote by
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}^{0}_{n}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}
.

There is also an alternative presentation via the elements

(2.1)

Y_{i}:=T_{i}\cdots T_{n-1}\pi^{-1}T_{1}^{-1}\cdots T_{i-1}^{-1}

for
i=1,\ldots,n

The elements
\{Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}\}
generate a polynomial subalgebra, and
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
has generators

\left\{T_{i},X_{j}^{\pm 1},Y_{j}^{\pm 1}\,|\,i=1,\ldots,n-1\hbox{ and }j=1,\ldots,n\right\}

and relations

(2.2)

\displaystyle(T_{i}-\mathsf{t})(T_{i}+\mathsf{t}^{-1})=0;

\displaystyle T_{i}T_{i+1}T_{i}=T_{i+1}T_{i}T_{i+1};

\displaystyle T_{i}T_{j}=T_{j}T_{i}\hbox{ for }j\not=i,i+1;

\displaystyle X_{i}X_{j}=X_{j}X_{i};

\displaystyle Y_{i}Y_{j}=Y_{j}Y_{i};

\displaystyle T_{i}X_{i}T_{i}=X_{i+1}\hbox{ for }i\not=n;

\displaystyle T_{i}^{-1}Y_{i}T_{i}^{-1}=Y_{i+1}\hbox{ for }i\not=n;

\displaystyle T_{i}X_{j}=X_{j}T_{i}\hbox{ for }j\not=i,i+1;

\displaystyle T_{i}Y_{j}=Y_{j}T_{i}\hbox{ for }j\not=i,i+1;

\displaystyle Y_{1}\cdots Y_{n}X_{j}=\mathsf{q}^{2}X_{j}Y_{1}\cdots Y_{n};

\displaystyle X_{1}\cdots X_{n}Y_{j}=\mathsf{q}^{-2}Y_{j}X_{1}\cdots X_{n};

\displaystyle X_{1}Y_{2}=Y_{2}T^{2}_{1}X_{1}

We note that this presentation is also valid for
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}
.

The elements
\{T_{1},\ldots,T_{n-1}\}
generate a Hecke algebra for the symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}
with parameter
\mathsf{t}
.
Let
s_{i}\in\Sigma_{n}
be the
i
th adjacent transposition.
For any
w\in\Sigma_{n}
with reduced expression
w=s_{i_{1}}\cdots s_{i_{k}}
, we the element

T_{w}:=T_{i_{1}}\cdots T_{i_{k}}

is well-defined (independent of the reduced expression).

Theorem 2.1

(
[
Che05
]
)
.

\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}
has an
\mathbb{L}
-basis given by elements of the form

M_{X}M_{Y}T_{w}

where
M_{X}
and
M_{Y}
are monomials in
\{X_{i}\}
and
\{Y_{i}\}
, respectively, and
w\in\Sigma_{n}
.

2.1.2.
Spherical subalgebra

For
w\in\Sigma_{n}
, let
l(w)
be its
length
.
The
Hecke symmetrizer
element

\widetilde{S}:=\frac{1}{[n]_{\mathsf{t}^{2}}!}\sum_{w\in\Sigma_{n}}\mathsf{t}^{l(w)}T_{w}

is idempotent.
Here, for a parameter
\mathsf{s}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we denote:

\displaystyle{}_{\mathsf{s}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\mathsf{s}^{n}-\mathsf{s}^{-n}}{\mathsf{s}-\mathsf{s}^{-1}},

\displaystyle[n]_{\mathsf{s}}!

\displaystyle:=\prod_{k=1}^{n}[k]_{\mathsf{s}}

We define the
spherical subalgebra

\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
to be

\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t}):=\widetilde{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})\widetilde{S}\subset\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})

Notice that
\widetilde{S}
is not an element of
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}
.
By abuse of notation, we define
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}
to be the corresponding subalgebra after base change:

\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}:=\widetilde{S}\left(\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}\otimes\mathbb{L}\left[\frac{1}{[n]_{\mathsf{t}^{2}}!}\right]\right)\widetilde{S}

For
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define the specialization
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})
by

\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k}):=\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})\otimes\left(\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}\bigg/(\mathsf{t}-\mathsf{q}^{k})\right)

Let
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})^{+}
,
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})^{+}_{\mathbb{L}}
, and
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})
be the subalgebras generated by positive powers of
\{X_{i}\}
and
\{Y_{i}\}
, and let
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})^{+}
,
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}^{+}
, and
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})^{+}
be their respective spherical subalgebras.
By the DAHA relations, the powers of
\widetilde{S}X_{1}\cdots X_{n}\widetilde{S}
and
\widetilde{S}Y_{1}\cdots Y_{n}\widetilde{S}
generate an Ore set, and we obtain
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}
from
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}^{+}
by inverting them.
We have the following generation lemma:

Lemma 2.2
.

\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
is generated by
\widetilde{S}\mathbb{K}[X^{\pm 1}_{1},\ldots,X^{\pm 1}_{n}]\widetilde{S}
and
\widetilde{S}\mathbb{K}[Y^{\pm 1}_{1},\ldots,Y^{\pm 1}_{n}]\widetilde{S}
.
Similary, for
k>2n
,
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})
is generated by
\widetilde{S}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[X^{\pm 1}_{1},\ldots,X^{\pm 1}_{n}]\widetilde{S}
and
\widetilde{S}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[Y^{\pm 1}_{1},\ldots,Y^{\pm 1}_{n}]\widetilde{S}
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the analogous generation statement for
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})^{+}
and
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})^{+}
.
If we assign

\deg(X_{i})=(1,0),\,\deg(Y_{i})=(0,1),\,\deg(T_{i})=(0,0)

then
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}^{+}
is bigraded with finite rank pieces.
For the case of
(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
, we set
t=1
, and for the case of
(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})
, we set
\mathsf{q}=e^{2\pi i/k}
.
In both cases, the spherical DAHA becomes a symmetrized quantum torus.
The proof of Lemma A.15.2 of
[
VV10
]
proves the result at these specific specializations (
k>2n
is important here).
We obtain the result at generic parameters by applying Nakayama’s Lemma to each (finite-rank) bigraded piece.
∎

2.1.3.
Macdonald polynomials

Let
\mathbb{L}[\mathbf{x}_{n}^{\pm}]:=\mathbb{L}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm 1}]
.
The following is well-known:

Theorem 2.3

(
[
Che95a
]
)
.

There is a faithful representation
\mathfrak{r}
of
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}
on
\mathbb{L}[\mathbf{x}_{n}^{\pm}]
given by the formulas: for
f\in\mathbb{L}[\mathbf{x}_{n}^{\pm}]
,

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}(T_{i})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{t}s_{i}+(\mathsf{t}-\mathsf{t}^{-1})\frac{s_{i}-1}{x_{i}/x_{i+1}-1}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}(X_{i})\cdot f

\displaystyle=x_{i}f

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}(\pi)\cdot f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})

\displaystyle=f(x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,x_{n},\mathsf{q}^{-2}x_{1})

Moreover, this representation remains faithful at any specialization of
(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
where
\mathsf{q}
is not a root of unity.

We call
\mathfrak{r}
the
polynomial representation
.
By abuse of notation, we will also use
\mathfrak{r}
to denote the representations obtained via base-change from
\mathbb{L}
.

Let
\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}
denote the base-changed subring of symmetric polynomials:

\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}:=\mathbb{L}[\mathbf{x}_{n}^{\pm}]^{\Sigma_{n}}\otimes\mathbb{L}\left[\frac{1}{[n]_{\mathsf{t}^{2}}!}\right]

By (
2.3
), it is easily follows that
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}
preserves
\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}
.
Because
\widetilde{S}
is idempotent, we have that this action is also faithful.
Let
\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t}):=\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}\otimes\mathbb{K}
.

Theorem 2.4
.

We have the following:

(1)

For each partition
\lambda
, there exists a unique
P_{\lambda}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})\in\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
satisfying:

(a)

for all
f\in\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
,

\mathfrak{r}\big(f(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n})\big)\cdot P_{\lambda}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})=f(\mathsf{q}^{2\lambda_{1}}\mathsf{t}^{n-1},\mathsf{q}^{2\lambda_{2}}t^{n-3},\ldots,\mathsf{q}^{2\lambda_{n}}t^{1-n})P_{\lambda}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})

(b)

the coefficient of
x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{\lambda_{n}}
is
1
.

The collection
\{P_{\lambda}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})\}
forms a basis of
\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
.

(2)

The set of polynomials
\{P_{\lambda}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})\}
is defined over a localization of
\mathbb{L}
at a multiplicative set
M
not containing
(\mathsf{t}-\mathsf{q}^{k})
for
k\geq 1
.

The functions
\{P_{\lambda}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})\}
are called the
Macdonald polynomials
.
Part (1) is classical, and part (2) follows from the
tableaux sum formula
for
P_{\lambda}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
(see
[
Mac15
]
for both).
From part (2), it follows that we can specialize the Macdonald polynomials at
\mathsf{t}=\mathsf{q}^{k}
for
k\geq 1
.
By part (1), we have that
\{P_{\lambda}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})\}
forms a basis of
\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(\mathsf{q}):=\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]^{\Sigma_{n}}
.

2.2.
Cyclotomic subalgebra

For
\ell\geq 0
, let
\mathsf{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and set
\mathsf{Z}=(\mathsf{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{Z}_{\ell})
.
When
\ell=0
, we treat
\mathsf{Z}
as an empty list.

2.2.1.
Cyclotomic
\mathsf{q}
-Dunkl operators

The
cyclotomic DAHA
of Braverman–Etingof–Finkelberg
[
BEF20
]
is a subalgebra of
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
that can be defined using certain
\mathsf{q}
-Dunkl operators
:

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle:=X_{1}^{-1}\left(\mathsf{q}^{-1}\mathsf{t}^{1-n}Y_{1}^{-1}-\mathsf{Z}_{1}\right)\cdots\left(\mathsf{q}^{-1}\mathsf{t}^{1-n}Y_{1}^{-1}-\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle D_{i}

\displaystyle:=T_{i-1}^{-1}\cdots T_{1}^{-1}D_{1}T_{1}^{-1}\cdots T_{i-1}^{-1}

These elements can be obtained by conjugating
\{X_{1}^{-1},\ldots,X_{n}^{-1}\}
by certain elements lying in a completing of
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
.
Specifically, let

(2.4)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}_{a}}=\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}_{a}}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n};\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})

\displaystyle:=\exp\left(\sum_{m>0}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{(\mathsf{q}^{-1}\mathsf{t}^{1-n}\mathsf{Z}_{a}Y_{i})^{-m}-\mathsf{t}^{m(n-2i)}}{(1-\mathsf{q}^{2m})m}\right)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}}

\displaystyle:=\prod_{a=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}_{a}}

We denote the
\mathsf{q}
-Pochhammer symbols
by

\displaystyle(x;\mathsf{q})_{\infty}

\displaystyle:=\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}(1-\mathsf{q}^{i}x)

\displaystyle(x;\mathsf{q})_{m}

\displaystyle:=\frac{(x;\mathsf{q})_{\infty}}{(\mathsf{q}^{m}x;\mathsf{q})_{\infty}}\hbox{ for }m\in\mathbb{Z}

Proposition 2.5
.

The following properties hold for
\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}}
:

(1)

\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}}X_{i}^{-1}\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}}^{-1}=(-1)^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z}_{1}\cdots\mathsf{Z}_{\ell})^{-1}D_{i}
;

(2)

the action on the Macdonald polynomial
P_{\lambda}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
is given by

(2.5)

\mathfrak{r}(\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}})\cdot P_{\lambda}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})=\left(\prod_{a=1}^{\ell}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\mathsf{q}^{-1}\mathsf{t}^{-2(n-i)}\mathsf{Z}_{a}^{-1};\mathsf{q}^{2}\right)_{-\lambda_{i}}\right)P_{\lambda}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})

Proof.

Part (1) follows from the proof of Lemma 3.21 in
[
BEF20
]
.
Namely, one can check that

\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}}(X_{1}^{-1},\ldots,\mathsf{q}^{-1}X_{i}^{-1},\ldots,X_{n};\mathsf{q}^{-1},\mathsf{t}^{-1})=(1-\mathsf{q}\mathsf{t}^{n-1}\mathsf{Z}_{i}^{-1}X_{i})\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}}(X_{1}^{-1},\ldots,X_{n}^{-1};\mathsf{q}^{-1},\mathsf{t}^{-1})

Part (2) is by direct calculation using Theorem
2.4
(1).
∎

The corollary below was proved in
[
BEF20
]
:

Corollary 2.6
.

The elements
\{D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}\}
pairwise commute.

2.2.2.
Definition of
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
and PBW

The cyclotomic DAHA
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
is defined to be the subalgebra of
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
generated by

\{T_{i},Y_{j}^{\pm 1},X_{j},D_{j}\,|\,i=1,\ldots n-1\hbox{ and }j=1,\ldots n\}

We emphasize that only
X_{j}
is included, not
X_{j}^{-1}
.
The Dunkl elements
\{D_{j}\}
can be defined in
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}
, and we define
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}
and
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})
analogously.
We likewise define
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})^{-}
,
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}^{-}
, and
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})^{-}
to be the subalgebras generated by

\{T_{i},Y_{j}^{-1},X_{j},D_{j}\,|\,i=1,\ldots n-1\hbox{ and }j=1,\ldots n\}

i.e. without
\{Y_{j}\}
.

Remark 2.7
.

In
[
BEF20
]
, their Dunkl operators are instead

\displaystyle D_{1}^{\mathrm{BEF}}

\displaystyle:=X_{1}^{-1}(Y_{1}-\mathsf{Z}_{1})\cdots(Y_{1}-\mathsf{Z}_{\ell})

\displaystyle D_{i}^{\mathrm{BEF}}

\displaystyle=T_{i-1}^{-1}\cdots T_{1}^{-1}D_{1}^{\mathrm{BEF}}T_{1}^{-1}\cdots T_{i-1}^{-1}

It follows that our
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
coincides with theirs with rescaled and inverted
\mathsf{Z}
-parameters.

Proposition 2.8
.

We have the following:

(1)

There exists a filtration on
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}^{-}
where

\deg(X_{i})=\ell,\,\deg(Y_{i}^{-1})=2,\,\deg(D_{i})=\ell,\,\deg(T_{i})=0

(2)

\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})^{-}_{\mathbb{L}}
has an
\mathbb{L}
-basis given by elements of the form

M_{D}M_{X}M_{Y}T_{w}

where:

•

M_{D}
is a monomial in the
\{D_{i}\}
;

•

M_{X}
is a monomial in the
\{X_{i}\}
;

•

M_{Y}
is a monomial in the
\{Y_{i}\}
where every variable has exponent
\leq 0
and
>-\ell
;

•

w\in\Sigma_{n}
and
T_{w}
is defined as in
2.1.1
.

Proof.

Linear independence follows from the analogous statement over
\mathbb{K}
, which is proved in
[
BEF20
]
.
The relations used in
loc. cit.
to show the spanning property are defined over
\mathbb{L}
.
This yields a presentation for
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}^{-}
; the relations in
loc. cit.
shows the existence of the filtration.
∎

Next, let us consider the action of
\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}
on
\mathbb{C}[z,w]
where the generator
\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}
acts by

\displaystyle\sigma\cdot z

\displaystyle=e^{2\pi\sqrt{-1}/\ell}z,

\displaystyle\sigma\cdot w

\displaystyle=e^{-2\pi\sqrt{-1}/\ell}w

Taking the tensor product, we obtain an action of
(\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z})^{n}
on
\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n},w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}]
.
We set

(2.6)

\mathcal{R}_{\ell}:=\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n},w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}]^{(\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z})^{n}}

Corollary 2.9
.

\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}^{-}
is a flat deformation of a filtered algebra whose associated graded is isomorphic to
\mathcal{R}_{\ell}\rtimes\Sigma_{n}
.

Proof.

Setting
\mathsf{q}=\mathsf{t}=1
, the Hecke algebra generated by
\{T_{i}\}
becomes the group algebra
\mathbb{C}[\Sigma_{n}]
while the elements
\{X_{i}\}\cup\{D_{i}\}\cup\{Y_{i}\}
commute and satisfy

(2.7)

X_{i}D_{i}=(Y_{i}^{-1}-\mathsf{Z}_{1})\cdots(Y_{i}^{-1}-\mathsf{Z}_{\ell})

In the associated graded of
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},1,1)
for the filtration from Proposition
2.8
, (
2.7
) becomes
X_{i}D_{i}=Y_{i}^{\ell}
.

Now consider
\mathcal{R}_{\ell}
.
If we denote the variables in the
i
th tensorand by
\{z_{i},w_{i}\}
, then the map

(2.8)

\displaystyle X_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto z_{i}^{\ell},

\displaystyle t^{1-n}Y_{i}^{-1}

\displaystyle\mapsto z_{i}w_{i},

\displaystyle D_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto w_{i}^{\ell},

\displaystyle T_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto s_{i}

defines a ring homomorphism
\mathrm{gr}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},1,1)\rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{\ell}\rtimes\Sigma_{n}
.
It follows from Proposition
2.8
, this homomorphism is an isomorphism.
∎

2.2.3.
Spherical generators

The spherical subalgebra is defined to be
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t}):=\widetilde{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})\widetilde{S}
.
We similarly define
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}
and
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})
as well as the ‘minus’ versions
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})^{-}
,
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}^{-}
, and
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})^{-}
.
These minus subalgebras inherit the filtration from Proposition
2.8
because
\deg(T_{i})=0
.

Lemma 2.10
.

\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
is generated by:

\widetilde{S}\mathbb{K}[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]\widetilde{S}\cup\widetilde{S}\mathbb{K}[Y_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,Y_{n}^{\pm 1}]\widetilde{S}\cup\widetilde{S}\mathbb{K}[D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}]\widetilde{S}

For
\ell>0
and
k>2n\ell
,
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})
is generated by:

\widetilde{S}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]\widetilde{S}\cup\widetilde{S}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[Y_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,Y_{n}^{\pm 1}]\widetilde{S}\cup\widetilde{S}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}]\widetilde{S}

Proof.

Because
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})^{-}_{\mathbb{L}}
is a direct summand of
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}^{-}
, it follows from Corollary
2.9
that
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}^{-}
is a flat deformation of a filtered algebra whose associated graded is isomorphic to

\mathcal{R}_{\ell}^{\Sigma_{n}}=\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n},w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}]^{\Sigma_{n}\wr\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}}

By a theorem of Weyl
[
Wey39
]
(cf.
[
Gan06
]
),
\mathcal{R}_{\ell}^{\Sigma_{n}}
is generated by power sums

(2.9)

p_{a,k,b}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}^{k+a\ell}w_{i}^{k+b\ell}

where
a,k,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
0\leq k<\ell
.
Under the
(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})\mapsto(1,1)
degeneration followed by taking the associated graded and applying the map (
2.8
), the element

(2.10)

P_{a,k,b}:=\widetilde{S}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{a}Y_{i}^{-k}D_{i}^{b}\right)\widetilde{S}

is sent to
p_{a,k,b}
.
Applying Nakayama’s lemma to each piece of the filtration (which has finite-rank), it follows that
\{P_{a,k,b}\}
generates
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})^{-}
and
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})^{-}
.

To prove the lemma, the steps are similar to the proof of Lemma
2.2
.
Namely,

(1)

for
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})^{-}
, we consider
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},1)_{\mathbb{L}}^{-}
;

(2)

for
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})^{-}
, we consider
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},e^{2\pi i/k},1)^{-}
.

After these specializations, we take the associated graded, wherein we have the relations

\displaystyle[Y_{i}^{-1},X_{i}]

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{q}^{-2}-1)X_{i}Y_{i}^{-1}

\displaystyle[D_{i},Y_{i}^{-1}]

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{q}^{-2}-1)Y_{i}^{-1}D_{i}

\displaystyle[D_{i},X_{i}]

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{q}^{-2\ell}-1)X_{i}D_{i}

The same steps as in the proof of Lemma A.15.2 in
[
VV10
]
allow us to produce every
P_{a,k,b}
at these specializations, and the lemma follows from applying Nakayama’s lemma to each filtered piece.
∎

3.
Quantum groups

The following is an abbreviated recap of Section 3 from our previous work
[
Wen23
]
.

3.1.
Quantized enveloping algebra

In this subsection, we review the algebra
\mathcal{U}:=U_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
and properties of its category of finite-dimensional representations.

3.1.1.
Definition

First, let us establish some basic notation for roots and weights of
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
:

•

Let
\{\epsilon_{i}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the coordinate basis and let
P\cong\mathbb{Z}^{n}
be the lattice spanned by
\{\epsilon_{i}\}
.

•

The set
R:=\{\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{j}\}
is the set of
roots
,
R^{+}:=\{\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon\}_{i<j}\subset R
is the set of
positive
roots, and the adjacent difference
\alpha_{i}:=\epsilon_{i}-\epsilon_{i+1}\subset R^{+}
is the
i
th simple root
.
The sublattice
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
spanned by
R
is the
root lattice
.

•

Under the standard pairing
\langle-,-\rangle
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
whereby
\{\epsilon_{i}\}
forms an orthonormal basis, we identify
P
with the
weight lattice
(for
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
).

•

Note that
P
has another basis
\{\omega_{i}\}
, where:

\omega_{i}=\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+\epsilon_{i}

We call
\omega_{i}
the
i
th fundamental weight
.

•

We call
\lambda\in P

dominant
if it pairs nonnegatively with all elements of
R_{+}
.
Let
P^{+}\subset P
be the subset of dominant weights.

•

We denote by
\delta
the
staircase partition
:

\delta:=(n-1,n-2,\ldots,0)\in Q^{+}

A somewhat similar element is the
Weyl vector

\rho:=\left(\frac{n-1}{2},\frac{n-3}{2},\ldots,-\frac{n-1}{2}\right)=\delta-\frac{n-1}{2}\left(1,\ldots,1\right)

Both satisfy
\langle\delta,\alpha_{i}\rangle=\langle\rho,\alpha_{i}\rangle=1
for all
i
.

The
quantized enveloping algebra

\mathcal{U}=U_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathfrak{gl}_{n})
is the
\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})
-algebra with generators

\{E_{i},F_{i},\mathsf{q}^{h}\,|\,i=1,\ldots,n-1\hbox{ and }h\in P\}

and relations

\displaystyle\mathsf{q}^{\vec{0}}=1,\,\mathsf{q}^{h_{1}}\mathsf{q}^{h_{2}}=\mathsf{q}^{h_{1}+h_{2}},

\displaystyle\mathsf{q}^{h}E_{i}\mathsf{q}^{-h}=\mathsf{q}^{\langle\alpha_{i},h\rangle}E_{i},\,\mathsf{q}^{h}F_{i}\mathsf{q}^{-h}=\mathsf{q}^{-\langle\alpha_{i},h\rangle}F_{i},

\displaystyle E_{i}F_{j}-F_{j}E_{i}=\delta_{ij}\dfrac{\mathsf{q}^{\alpha_{i}}-\mathsf{q}^{-\alpha_{i}}}{\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1}},

\displaystyle E_{i}^{2}E_{i+1}-(\mathsf{q}+\mathsf{q}^{-1})E_{i}E_{i+1}E_{i}+E_{i}E_{i+1}^{2}=0,

\displaystyle F_{i}^{2}F_{i+1}-(\mathsf{q}+\mathsf{q}^{-1})F_{i}F_{i+1}F_{i}+F_{i}F_{i+1}^{2}=0,

where
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker delta.

\mathcal{U}
is a Hopf algebra with the coproduct
\Delta_{\mathcal{U}}
, counit
\epsilon_{\mathcal{U}}
, and antipode
\iota_{\mathcal{U}}
given by

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{U}}(E_{i})=E_{i}\otimes\mathsf{q}^{\alpha_{i}}+1\otimes E_{i},\hskip 14.22636pt\Delta_{\mathcal{U}}(F_{i})=F_{i}\otimes 1+\mathsf{q}^{-\alpha_{i}}\otimes F_{i},\hskip 14.22636pt\Delta_{\mathcal{U}}(\mathsf{q}^{h})=\mathsf{q}^{h}\otimes\mathsf{q}^{h};

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\mathcal{U}}(E_{i})=\epsilon_{\mathcal{U}}(F_{i})=0,\hskip 14.22636pt\epsilon_{\mathcal{U}}(\mathsf{q}^{h})=1;

\displaystyle\iota_{\mathcal{U}}(E_{i})=-E_{i}\mathsf{q}^{-\alpha_{i}},\hskip 14.22636pt\iota_{\mathcal{U}}(F_{i})=-\mathsf{q}^{\alpha_{i}}F_{i},\hskip 14.22636pt\iota_{\mathcal{U}}(\mathsf{q}^{h})=\mathsf{q}^{-h}.

We will use
Sweedler notation
to denote coproducts:

\Delta_{\mathcal{U}}(x)=x_{(1)}\otimes x_{(2)}

With this notation, the
adjoint action
of
\mathcal{U}
on itself can be written as:

\mathrm{ad}_{x}(y)=x_{(1)}y\iota_{\mathcal{U}}(x_{(2)})

Finally, we note here that for
x\in\mathcal{U}
,

(3.1)

\iota_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}(x)=\mathsf{q}^{2\rho}x\mathsf{q}^{-2\rho}

3.1.2.
R-matrix

The
universal
R
-matrix

\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}
is an invertible element of a suitable completion of
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes 2}
that satisfies the following properties:

(3.2)

\displaystyle(\Delta_{\mathcal{U}}\otimes 1)\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{13}\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{23}

(3.3)

\displaystyle(1\otimes\Delta_{\mathcal{U}})\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{13}\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{12}

(3.4)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}(x_{(1)}\otimes x_{(2)})

\displaystyle=(x_{(2)}\otimes x_{(1)})\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}\hbox{ for }x\in\mathcal{U}

Here,
\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{ab}
means that the first tensorand of
\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}
is placed in the
a
th position and the second tensorand in the
b
th position.
Some consequences of these properties are:

\displaystyle(\epsilon_{\mathcal{U}}\otimes 1)\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}=(1\otimes\epsilon_{\mathcal{U}})\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}=1

\displaystyle(\iota_{\mathcal{U}}\otimes 1)\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}=(1\otimes\iota_{\mathcal{U}})\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}=\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}^{-1}

(3.5)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{12}\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{13}\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{23}=\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{23}\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{13}\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{12}

The final equation (
3.5
) is known as the
quantum Yang-Baxter Equation
.
Finally,
\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}
factors in the following manner:

(3.6)

\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}=\mathsf{q}^{-\sum\epsilon_{i}\otimes\epsilon_{i}}\left(1\otimes 1+\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}^{*}\right)

where, if we let
\mathcal{U}^{+}
and
\mathcal{U}^{-}
be the subspaces of
\mathcal{U}
spanned by products of
\{E_{i}\}
and
\{F_{i}\}
respectively, then
\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}^{*}
is an element of a suitable completion of
\mathcal{U}^{+}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{-}
.

The action of
\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}
is well-defined on highest weight representations of
\mathcal{U}
.
We will be particularly interested in its action on the
vector representation

\mathbb{V}\cong\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})^{n}
.
Let
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\}
be a basis of
\mathbb{V}
.
If we let
E_{ij}
be the matrix unit

E_{ij}e_{k}=\delta_{jk}e_{i}

then the specialized
R
-matrix has the form

\mathbf{R}^{\prime}:=\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}\big|_{\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{V}}=\mathsf{q}\sum_{i}E_{ii}\otimes E_{ii}+\sum_{i\not=j}E_{ii}\otimes E_{jj}+(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\sum_{i<j}E_{ji}\otimes E_{ij}

We will use more often the transpose of this matrix:

\mathbf{R}=\mathsf{q}\sum_{i}E_{ii}\otimes E_{ii}+\sum_{i\not=j}E_{ii}\otimes E_{jj}+(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\sum_{i>j}E_{ji}\otimes E_{ij}

Let
\tau:\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{V}
be the tensor swap.

\mathbf{R}
then satisfies the
Hecke condition
:

(3.7)

\tau\mathbf{R}-\mathbf{R}^{-1}\tau=\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1}

Finally, we will also make use of the
Drinfeld element

u
and the
ribbon element

\nu
.
To define
u
, it is helpful to introduce Einstein notation for
\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}
:

\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}=\sum_{s}{}_{s}r\otimes r_{s}=:{}_{s}r\otimes r_{s}

We then set
u:=\iota_{\mathcal{U}}(r_{s}){}_{s}r
.
In
[
Dd89
]
, it was shown that

\iota_{\mathcal{U}}^{2}(x)=uxu^{-1}

As a consequence of (
3.1
), we have that

\nu:=(\mathsf{q}^{-2\rho}u)^{-1}=u^{-1}\mathsf{q}^{2\rho}

is central.

3.1.3.
Representations

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be the category of finite-dimensional
\mathcal{U}
-modules, and let
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mathfrak{C}
be the subcategory generated by irreducible modules with highest weight in
P^{+}
.
For
\lambda\in P^{+}
, we denote the corresponding irreducible module by
V_{\lambda}\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.
By
[
RT90
]
,
\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}
both have the structure of a
ribbon category
, and this gives us a graphical calculus for working with matrix elements of representations.
Here, we review the structures needed in this paper—more details can be found in
[
BK01
]
.

(1)

Tensor and dual
:
A morphism
f:V\rightarrow W
will be denoted by an upward pointing arrow from
V
to
W
with a coupon labeled by
f
.
We will drop the coupon when denoting the identity morphism, and the identity morphism of
V^{*}
will be denoted using a
downward
point arrow from
V
to
V
.
Tensor product of objects and morphisms will be denoted using horizontal juxtaposition.
For instance, the diagram below denotes
f\otimes g\otimes\mathrm{id}:U_{1}\otimes V_{1}\otimes W^{*}\rightarrow U_{2}\otimes V_{2}\otimes W^{*}
:

By (
3.1
), the action of
\mathsf{q}^{2\rho}
identifies
V\cong V^{**}
, and since
\Delta_{\mathcal{U}}(\mathsf{q}^{2\rho})=\mathsf{q}^{2\rho}\otimes\mathsf{q}^{2\rho}
, this identification respects tensor products.

(2)

Characters
:
For
\mathsf{C}\in\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})^{\times}
,
\mathcal{U}
has a character
\chi_{\mathsf{C}}
where

\displaystyle\chi_{\mathsf{C}}(E_{i})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\chi_{\mathsf{C}}(F_{i})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\chi_{\mathsf{C}}(\mathsf{q}^{h})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{C}^{\langle h,\omega_{n}\rangle}

We denote by
\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{C}}
the corresponding representation.
Observe that
\mathbb{1}:=\mathbb{1}_{1}
is the trivial representation while
\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}}
is the
quantum determinant
representation.

(3)

(Quantum) Co/evaluations
:
For
V\in\mathfrak{C}
, let
\left\{v_{i}\right\}
be a basis of
V
with corresponding dual basis
\left\{v^{i}\right\}
.
The canonical maps

\displaystyle\mathbb{1}\ni a

\displaystyle\mapsto av_{i}\otimes v^{i}\in V\otimes V^{*}

\displaystyle V^{*}\otimes V\ni v^{i}\otimes v_{j}

\displaystyle\mapsto\delta_{ij}\in\mathbb{1}

are homomorphisms
\mathrm{coev}_{V}:\mathbb{1}\rightarrow V\otimes V^{*}
and
\mathrm{ev}_{V}:V^{*}\otimes V\rightarrow\mathbb{1}
, respectively.
Graphically, we will omit depicting
\mathbb{1}
;
\mathrm{ev}_{V}
and
\mathrm{coev}_{V}
appear as caps and cups oriented towards the left:

Using
\mathrm{ev}_{W}
and
\mathrm{coev_{V}}
, we can define for
f:V\rightarrow W
an adjoint
f^{*}:W^{*}\rightarrow V^{*}
.

To define maps with the opposite ordering, we will use
\mathsf{q}^{-2\rho}:V^{**}\rightarrow V
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{1}\ni a

\displaystyle\mapsto av^{i}\otimes\mathsf{q}^{-2\rho}v_{i}\in V^{*}\otimes V

\displaystyle V\otimes V^{*}\ni\mathsf{q}^{-2\rho}v_{i}\otimes v^{j}

\displaystyle\mapsto\delta_{ij}\in\mathbb{1}

We denote these maps by
\mathrm{qcoev}_{V}
and
\mathrm{qev}_{V}
, respectively.
Graphically, they will be depicted as cups and caps with orientations opposite from before:

(4)

Braiding
: Recall the universal
R
-matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}
from
3.1.2
.
Let
\tau_{V,W}:V\otimes W\rightarrow W\otimes V
be the tensor swap.
By (
3.4
),
\beta_{V,W}:=\tau_{V,W}\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}:V\otimes W\rightarrow W\otimes V
is a
\mathcal{U}
-module isomorphism, and in fact it endows
\mathfrak{\mathfrak{C}}
with the structure of a
braided monoidal category
.
We depict
\beta_{V,W}
and
\beta_{W,V}
by braid crossings:

(5)

Ribbon structure
: The action of the ribbon element
\nu
gives a
\mathcal{U}
-endomorphism of
V
, which can be drawn in two ways:

In
[
Dd89
]
, it was computed by Drinfeld that

(3.8)

\nu\big|_{V_{\lambda}}=\mathsf{q}^{\langle\lambda,\lambda+2\rho\rangle}

Drawing these decorated strands in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
as emanating upwards from a fixed horizontal axis, we can enhance them into ribbons with a front and back side.
We require the front side to always face the reader at the start and end of the ribbon—the back side may appear in the middle due to the loop given by
\nu
, in which case the ribbon is twisted twice.
The graphical calculus assigns to each morphism in
\mathfrak{C}
such a
\mathfrak{C}
-colored ribbon tangle
.

Theorem 3.1

(
[
RT90
]
)
.

A morphism in
\mathfrak{C}
only depends on the isotopy type of its associated
\mathfrak{C}
-colored ribbon tangle.

In practice, we will work with strands instead of ribbons but keep track of loops coming from
\nu
.

3.2.
Reflection equation algebra

Whereas
\mathcal{U}
deforms the universal enveloping algebra, we will also make use of a
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant quantization
\mathcal{O}_{G}
of the ring of functions on
G=GL_{n}
.
This algebra has both a categorical description as well as one by generators and relations.

3.2.1.
Braided Tannakian reconstruction

We review here the construction of
\mathcal{O}_{G}
via a braided analogue of Tannakian reconstruction, defined by Majid
[
Maj93
]
.
As a
\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})
-vector space,
\mathcal{O}_{G}
is defined as the
coend
of
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}
:

(3.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{G}

\displaystyle:=\left(\bigoplus_{V\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}}V^{*}\otimes V\right)\bigg/\left\langle f^{*}(v^{*})\otimes w-v^{*}\otimes f(w)\,\middle|\,\begin{array}[]{l}v^{*}\in V^{*},\,w\in W,\\
f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{U}}(W,V)\end{array}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{\lambda\in P^{+}}V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\lambda}

\mathcal{O}_{G}
is evidently a
\mathcal{U}
-module.
Using the categorical structures of
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}
reviewed in
3.1.3
, we can endow
\mathcal{O}_{G}
with a
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant Hopf algebra structure:

•

Algebra structure:

For
v^{*}\otimes v\in V^{*}\otimes V
and
w^{*}\otimes w\in W^{*}\otimes W
,

m_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(v^{*}\otimes v\otimes w^{*}\otimes w)=r_{t}r_{s}w^{*}\otimes{}_{t}rv^{*}\otimes{}_{s}rv\otimes w

The inclusion
\mathbb{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{1}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{1}\in\mathcal{O}_{G}
provides the unit.

•

Coalgebra structure:

For
v^{*}\otimes v\in V^{*}\otimes V
, we define the coproduct
\Delta_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(v^{*}\otimes v)
as:

\Delta_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(v^{*}\otimes v)=v^{*}\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{V}(1)\otimes v

The evaluation map
\mathrm{ev}_{V}
on
V^{*}\otimes V
yields the counit
\epsilon_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}
.

•

Antipode:
The antipode
\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}
is given by:

\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(v^{*}\otimes v)=\nu r_{s}v\otimes{}_{s}rv^{*}

3.2.2.
Presentation

\mathcal{O}_{G}
is also a localization of the
reflection equation algebra
.
To define the latter, recall the matrix units
\{E_{ij}\}
, the vector representation
\mathbb{V}
, and the specialized
R
-matrix
\mathbf{R}
from
3.1.2
.
Let
\{m_{ij}\}_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
be a set of generators.
We place them in a matrix
\mathbf{M}
as follows:

\mathbf{M}=\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}\otimes m_{ij}

For relations involving such matrices, we will only use subscripts to denote the tensorand wherein we place the matrix units—all remaining factors are assumed to be placed in the final tensorand.

Theorem 3.2
.

[
DM03
]

Let
\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}
be the
\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})
-algebra generated by the entries of
\mathbf{M}
subject to the relations:

\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{M}_{1}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{M}_{2}=\mathbf{M}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{M}_{1}\mathbf{R}

We have the following:

(1)

Let
\{e_{i}\}
be a basis of
\mathbb{V}
with dual basis
\{e^{i}\}
.
The map
\varphi:\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{G}
induced by
m_{ij}\mapsto e^{i}\otimes e_{j}
is an injective algebra homomorphism.

(2)

There exists a central element
\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{M})\in\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}
such that

\varphi(\textstyle\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{M}))=\mathrm{qcoev}_{\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}}}(1)

The map
\varphi
becomes an isomorphism upon inverting
\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{M})
.

\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}
is called the
reflection equation algebra
.
We will abuse notation and view
\mathbf{M}
as a matrix of elements in
\mathcal{O}_{G}
.
By the antipode condition, we have that
\mathbf{M}^{-1}:=(1\otimes\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{G}})(\mathbf{M})
does indeed satisfy
\mathbf{M}\mathbf{M}^{-1}=\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{I}
.
The element
\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{M})
is called the
quantum determinant
.
An explicit formula for it is given in
[
JW20
]
.
We will be content with its categorical interpretation in terms of
\mathrm{qcoev}_{\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}}}
.
For instance, from this interpretation, it is easy to see that

\textstyle\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{M}))=\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{M})^{-1}

We will denote by
\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{M}^{-1}):=\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{M}))
.
Note that this is not necessarily the Jordan–White formula applied to
\mathbf{M}^{-1}
.

3.2.3.
Joseph–Letzter–Rosso homomorphism

To
v^{*}\otimes v\in V^{*}\otimes V
, we can associate a functional on
\mathcal{U}
by

(v^{*}\otimes v)(x)=v^{*}(xv)\hbox{ for }x\in\mathcal{U}

The following map can be viewed as assigning a dual element via a quantum Killing form:

Theorem 3.3
.

[
JL92
,
Ros90
]

The maps
\kappa,\bar{\kappa}:\mathcal{O}_{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{U}
given by

\displaystyle\kappa(v^{*}\otimes v)

\displaystyle=((v^{*}\otimes v)(-)\otimes 1)\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}_{21}\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}

\displaystyle\bar{\kappa}(v^{*}\otimes v)

\displaystyle=((v^{*}\otimes v)(-)\otimes 1)\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}^{-1}\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}^{-1}_{21}

are
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant algebra embeddings (where
\mathcal{U}
is endowed with the adjoint action).
Moreover, the center of
\mathcal{U}
is
\kappa(\mathcal{O}_{G}^{\mathcal{U}})=\bar{\kappa}(\mathcal{O}_{G}^{\mathcal{U}})
.

The map
\kappa
does not respect coproducts, but the discrepancy can be pinned down precisely:

Proposition 3.4

(
[
VV10
]
)
.

Let
\left\{v_{i}\right\}\subset V
and
\left\{v^{i}\right\}\subset V^{*}
be dual bases.
For
v^{*}\otimes v\in V^{*}\otimes V
, we have

(3.10)

(\Delta_{\mathcal{U}}\circ\kappa)(v^{*}\otimes v)=\kappa(v^{*}\otimes v_{i})r_{s}r_{t}\otimes\kappa({}_{s}rv^{i}\otimes{}_{t}rv)

In particular,
\kappa(\mathcal{O}_{G})
is a left coideal sublagebra of
\mathcal{U}
, i.e.

\displaystyle(\Delta_{\mathcal{U}}\circ\kappa)(\mathcal{O}_{G})

\displaystyle\subset\mathcal{U}\otimes\kappa(\mathcal{O}_{G})

We end by making a few observations:

(1)

Note that the map
V^{*}\otimes V\otimes W\rightarrow W
given by

v^{*}\otimes v\otimes w\mapsto\kappa(v^{*}\otimes v)(w)

is a
\mathcal{U}
-morphism depicted by the following diagram:

(3.11)

(2)

The
\Delta_{\mathcal{U}}
formula (
3.10
) is the result of taking (
3.11
) with two strands (
\mathcal{U}
-modules) passing through and making bunny ears to force the appearance of
\Delta_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}
:

(3)

From (
3.6
), it is easy to deduce that

\textstyle\kappa(\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{M}))=\kappa(\mathrm{qcoev}_{\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}}}(1))=\mathsf{q}^{-2\omega_{n}}

4.
Quantum differential operators

In this section, we review and study the ring of quantum differential operators on the matrix space corresponding to the following framed cyclic quiver:

(4.1)

Here, the numbers within the squares and circle denote the dimensions of the vector spaces we will assign to them, and the subscripts
1,\ldots,\ell
are the labelings of the vertices, which we will view as elements of
\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}
for
\ell\geq 1
.
In the case of
\ell=1
, we will have a loop from vertex
1
back to itself.
We will define this algebra piecemeal, addressing the round vertices first before the square one.
The latter corresponds to the
quantum Weyl algebra
.
Unfortunately, we will need to split the former into the cases
\ell>1
and
\ell=1
.

4.1.
The case
\mathbf{\ell>1}

Here, we will work with the algebras defined by Jordan
[
Jor14
]
for the subquiver of (
4.1
) consisting of round vertices.

4.1.1.
Braided coordinate ring
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}

The quantized function algebra
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}
has generators

\left\{x_{ij}^{(a)}\,\middle|\,\begin{array}[]{l}1\leq i,j\leq n\\
a\in\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}\end{array}\right\}

We put the generators with superscript index
a
into the matrix

\mathbf{X}^{(a)}:=\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}\otimes x_{ij}^{(a)}

For
\ell\geq 3
, its relations are as follows (recall that subscripts only denote where we place the matrix units):

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\mathbf{R};

(4.3)

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a-1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a-1)}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)};

(4.4)

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(b)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(b)}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\hbox{ for }b\not=a,a\pm 1

For
\ell=2
, we replace (
4.3
) with:

(4.5)

\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(2)}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(1)}=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(1)}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(2)}

4.1.2.
Algebra of quantum differential operators
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}

The algebra
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
of quantum differential operators on
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}
is obtained by adjoining additional generators

\left\{\partial_{ij}^{(a)}\,\middle|\begin{array}[]{l}1\leq i,j\leq n\\
a\in\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}\end{array}\right\}

to
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}
.
As before, we put them into the matrices

\mathbf{D}^{(a)}:=\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}\otimes\partial_{ij}^{(a)}

For
\ell\geq 3
, these new generators satisfy the relations:

(4.6)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(a)}\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(a)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(a)}\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(a)}\mathbf{R};

(4.7)

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(a)}\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(a+1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(a+1)}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(a)};

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(a)}\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(b)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(b)}\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(a)}\hbox{ for }b\not=a,a\pm 1;

(4.8)

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(a)}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(a)}+(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\mathbf{\Omega};

(4.9)

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(a)}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a+1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a+1)}\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(a)};

(4.10)

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(a)}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a-1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a-1)}\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(a)}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1};

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(a)}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(b)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(b)}\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(a)}\hbox{ for }b\not=a,a\pm 1;

where in (
4.8
),

\mathbf{\Omega}=\sum_{ij}E_{ij}\otimes E_{ji}\otimes 1

For
\ell=2
, we replace (
4.7
), (
4.9
), and (
4.10
) with

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(1)}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(2)}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(1)};

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(a)}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a+1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a+1)}\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(a)}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1};

Remark 4.1
.

In comparison with
[
Jor14
]
, we have scaled the
\partial
-generators of
loc. cit.
by
(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})
.
This will simplify the formulas for the moment map in
5.1.1
.

4.1.3.
Equivariance

The algebras
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
are modules over
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
.
Here, we will describe these module structures in more detail.
For a fixed
a\in\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}
, let
\mathcal{U}^{(a)}
denote the
a
th tensorand of
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
(we index the tensorands using
\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}
), and let
\mathbb{V}_{(a)}
denote the vector representation of
\mathcal{U}^{(a)}
with basis
\{e_{i}^{(a)}\}
and dual basis
\{e^{i}_{(a)}\}
.
One should view
\mathcal{U}^{(a)}
as the copy of
\mathcal{U}
acting on the round vertex in (
4.1
) labeled by
a
.
Finally, let

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\ell}^{(a)}

\displaystyle:=\left\langle x_{ij}^{(a)}\,\middle|\,1\leq i,j\leq n\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{(a)}

\displaystyle:=\left\langle x_{ij}^{(a)},\partial_{ij}^{(a)}\,\middle|\,1\leq i,j\leq n\right\rangle

We will view
n\times n
matrices as elements of the
exterior
tensor product of vector representations
\mathbb{V}\boxtimes\mathbb{V}^{*}
.
The quantized ring of functions on matrices would then be a suitable quantization of the polynomial ring generated by
\mathbb{V}^{*}\boxtimes\mathbb{V}
, and likewise, functions on the dual space would be generated by
\mathbb{V}\boxtimes\mathbb{V}^{*}
.
In this way, we will coordinates of
\mathbf{X}^{(a)}
as functions on matrices from vertex
a+1
to vertex
a
and coordinates of
\mathbf{D}^{(a)}
as analogous fuctions from vertex
a
to vertex
a+1
.
To be precise, consider the following algebra homomorphisms out of the tensor algebras:

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathcal{O}}^{(a)}:\mathcal{T}\left(\mathbb{V}^{*}_{(a)}\boxtimes\mathbb{V}_{(a+1)}\right)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\ell}^{(a)}

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathcal{D}}^{(a)}:\mathcal{T}\left(\mathbb{V}^{*}_{(a)}\boxtimes\mathbb{V}_{(a+1)}\oplus\mathbb{V}_{(a)}\boxtimes\mathbb{V}_{(a+1)}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{(a)}

\displaystyle e^{i}_{(a)}\boxtimes e_{i}^{(a+1)}

\displaystyle\mapsto x_{ij}^{(a)}

\displaystyle e^{i}_{(a)}\boxtimes e_{i}^{(a+1)}

\displaystyle\mapsto x_{ij}^{(a)}

\displaystyle e_{i}^{(a)}\boxtimes e^{j}_{(a+1)}

\displaystyle\mapsto\partial_{ji}^{(a)}

Proposition 4.2

(
[
Jor14
]
)
.

\mathcal{O}_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
are
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
-equivariant algebras.

Proof.

First consider the subalgebras
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}^{(a)}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{(a)}
.
The kernels of the algebra homomorphisms
\Pi_{\mathcal{O}}^{(a)}
and
\Pi_{\mathcal{D}}^{(a)}
correspond to the relations (
4.2
), (
4.6
), and (
4.8
).
These relations, in turn, can be expressed as images of
\mathcal{U}_{(a)}\otimes\mathcal{U}_{(a+1)}
-module homomorphisms into the respective tensor algebras (cf. 3.3.2 of
[
Jor14
]
).
It follows that
\Pi_{\mathcal{O}}^{(a)}
and
\Pi_{\mathcal{D}}^{(a)}
are in fact
\mathcal{U}_{(a)}\otimes\mathcal{U}_{(a+1)}
-module homomorphisms; thus,
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}^{(a)}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{(a)}
are
\mathcal{U}_{(a)}\otimes\mathcal{U}_{(a+1)}
-equivariant.

The remaining relations involve different
(a)
-indices, and they merely impose that
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
are
cyclically-ordered

braided
tensor products of algebras
\{\mathcal{O}_{\ell}^{(a)}\}
and
\{\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{(a)}\}
, respectively.
Namely, in the case
\ell\geq 3
, an element
x^{(a-1)}
with index
a-1
and an element
y^{(a)}
with index
a
both share an action of
\mathcal{U}^{(a)}
.
If we let
\widetilde{\mathbf{R}}^{(a)}={}_{s}r^{(a)}\otimes r^{(a)}_{s}
denote the universal
R
-matrix of
\mathcal{U}^{(a)}
, then we have imposed:

(4.11)

\displaystyle x^{(a-1)}y^{(a)}

\displaystyle=\left(r_{s}^{(a)}\cdot y^{(a)}\right)\left({{}_{s}}r^{(a)}\cdot x^{(a-1)}\right),

\displaystyle y^{(a)}x^{(a-1)}

\displaystyle=\left(\iota_{\mathcal{U}}({{}_{s}}r^{(a)})\cdot x^{(a-1)}\right)\left(r_{s}^{(a)}\cdot y^{(a)}\right)

(the cyclicity of the order comes from viewing
a\in\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}
).
For
\ell=2
, we have instead:

(4.12)

\displaystyle x^{(1)}y^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\left(\big[\iota_{\mathcal{U}}({}_{t}r^{(1)})\otimes r_{s}^{(2)}\big]\cdot y^{(2)}\right)\left(\big[r_{t}^{(1)}\otimes{}_{s}r^{(2)}]\cdot x^{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle y^{(2)}x^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\left(\big[r_{s}^{(1)}\otimes\iota_{\mathcal{U}}({}_{t}r^{(2)})\big]\cdot x^{(1)}\right)\left(\big[r_{t}^{(1)}\otimes{}_{s}r^{(2)}]\cdot y^{(2)}\right)

Because these relations can be written in terms of the braiding and inverse braiding, respectively,
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
are indeed
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
-equivariant.
∎

4.1.4.
FRT quantum determinants

Each
\mathcal{O}^{(a)}_{\ell}
is isomorphic to the
FRT algebra
(cf. Chapter 9 of
[
KS97
]
).
This algebra also has a notion of quantum determinant:

(4.13)

\displaystyle\textstyle\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{(a)})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{w\in\Sigma_{n}}(-\mathsf{q})^{l(w)}x_{w(1)1}^{(a)}\cdots x_{w(n)n}^{(a)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in\Sigma_{n}}(-\mathsf{q})^{l(w)}x_{1w(1)}^{(a)}\cdots x_{nw(n)}^{(a)}

Here, we will review the properties of
\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{(a)})
.
One of the distinguishing features of
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}^{(a)}
is that it possesses natural maps

\displaystyle\varphi_{L}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{V}_{(a)}^{*}}\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{\mathbb{V}}:\mathbb{V}_{(a)}^{*}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\ell}^{(a)}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{(a)}^{*}

\displaystyle\varphi_{R}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{coev}_{\mathbb{V}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{V}_{(a+1)}}:\mathbb{V}_{(a+1)}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{(a+1)}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\ell}^{(a)}

(these are in fact comodules with respect to a natural bialgebra structure on
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}^{(a)}
, which we will not use).
Ignoring the distinction between
\mathcal{U}_{(a)}
and
\mathcal{U}_{(a+1)}
, both maps are
\mathcal{U}
-module homomorphism.
However, due to the trivial action on the image of
\mathrm{coev}_{\mathbb{V}}
, we have that
\varphi_{L}
is a
\mathcal{U}_{(a)}
-module homomorphism when we have
\mathcal{U}_{(a)}
act trivially on the target
\mathbb{V}_{(a)}^{*}
tensorand.
Likewise,
\varphi_{R}
is a
\mathcal{U}_{(a+1)}
-morphism when we ignore the
\mathcal{U}_{(a+1)}
-action on the target
\mathbb{V}_{(a+1)}
-tensorand.

Because
\mathbf{R}
satisfies the Hecke relation (
3.7
), we can define the quantum exterior algebras
\wedge_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}
and
\wedge_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}^{*}
by performing Hecke antisymmetrizations; these are
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant algebras.
The characters
\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}}
and
\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}^{-1}}
are their respective top degree summands.
A key feature of
\varphi_{L}
and
\varphi_{R}
is that they extend to
\mathcal{U}_{(a)}
- and
\mathcal{U}_{(a+1)}
-equivariant algebra homomorphisms

\displaystyle\varphi_{L}^{\wedge}:

\displaystyle\wedge_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}_{(a)}^{*}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\ell}^{(a)}\otimes\wedge_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}_{(a)}

\displaystyle\varphi_{R}^{\wedge}:

\displaystyle\wedge_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}_{(a+1)}\rightarrow\wedge_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}_{(a+1)}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\ell}^{(a)}

respectively.
Here, as with before, when discussing equivariance, we only consider the
\mathcal{U}_{(a)}
- and
\mathcal{U}_{(a+1)}
-actions on the
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}^{(a)}
tensorand in the target.
Let
\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}^{-1}}^{(a)}
and
\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}}^{(a+1)}
denote the characters viewed as
\mathcal{U}_{(a)}
- and
\mathcal{U}_{(a+1)}
-modules, respectively.

Proposition 4.3
.

The FRT quantum determinant
\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{(a)})
transforms under the
\mathcal{U}_{(a)}\otimes\mathcal{U}_{(a+1)}
-action as a nonzero element of
\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}^{-1}}^{(a)}\boxtimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}}^{(a+1)}
.

Proof.

By Proposition 9.7 of
[
KS97
]
, one can choose basis elements

\displaystyle y\in\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}^{-1}}\subset\wedge_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}^{*}

\displaystyle y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}}\subset\wedge_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}

such that

\displaystyle\varphi_{L}^{\wedge}(y)

\displaystyle=\textstyle\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{(a)})\otimes y

\displaystyle\varphi_{R}^{\wedge}(y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\textstyle y^{\prime}\otimes\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{(a)})

The result follows from equivariance.
∎

Corollary 4.4
.

The FRT quantum determinants
\{\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{(a)})\}_{a\in\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}}
generate an Ore set in
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}
.

Proof.

By Proposition 9.9 of
[
KS97
]
,
\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{(a)})
is central in
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}^{(a)}
.
Its commutation with other superscript indices is given by the
R
-matrix.
However, by Proposition
4.3
and (
3.6
), it is easy to see that
\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{(a)})
at worst
\mathsf{q}
-commutes.
∎

4.1.5.
PBW basis

Let
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
denote
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{q}^{\pm 1}]
-subalgebra generated by the entries of
\{\mathbf{X}^{(a)},\mathbf{D}^{(a)}\}_{a\in\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}}
.
We define a
standard monomial
of
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{\mathbb{Z}}
to be a product

(4.14)

x_{i_{1}j_{1}}^{(a_{1})}\cdots x_{i_{M}j_{M}}^{(a_{M})}\partial_{k_{1}l_{1}}^{(b_{1})}\cdots\partial_{k_{N}l_{N}}^{(b_{N})}

where:

•

the sequence of triples of indices
(a_{1},i_{1},j_{1}),\ldots,(a_{M},i_{M},j_{M})
is in increasing lexicographic order;

•

the sequence of triples of indices
(b_{1},k_{1},l_{1}),\ldots,(b_{N},k_{N},l_{N})
is in increasing lexicographic order.

Here, we view the superscript indices
\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{M},b_{1},\ldots,b_{M}\}
as integers
1,\ldots,\ell
, and so we want these indices to be
ascending
.

Theorem 4.5

(
[
Jor14
]
)
.

The standard monomials form a basis of
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Thus,
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
is a flat deformation of the coordinate ring of
T^{*}(\mathrm{Mat}_{n\times n}^{\oplus\ell})
.

4.2.
The case
\mathbf{\ell=0}

For the quiver (
4.1
) with
\ell=1
, the algebra constructed by Jordan
[
Jor14
]
is related to
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
.
We will thus call this the
\ell=0
case.
The resulting algebra is Varagnolo–Vasserot’s algebra of quantum differential operators on
GL_{n}

[
VV10
]
, which had also appeared prior in work of Alekseev–Schomerus
[
AS96
]
in their quantizations of character varieties.

4.2.1.
Definition of
\mathcal{D}_{0}

The algebra
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{+}
has generators

\{a_{ij},b_{ij}\,|\,1\leq i,j\leq n\}

Placing them in matrices

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}\otimes a_{ij},

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}\otimes b_{ij}

the relations are:

(4.15)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{1}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{A}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{1}\mathbf{R};

(4.16)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{B}_{1}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{B}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{B}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{B}_{1}\mathbf{R};

(4.17)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{B}_{1}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{A}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{B}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1};

The first two relations yield surjections from the reflection equation algebra
\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}
onto each of the subalgebras generated by the entries of
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
.
Thus, we can make sense of the quantum determinants
\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{A})
and
\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{B})
.
By Proposition 1.8.3(b) of
[
VV10
]
, the quantum determinants generate an Ore set of
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{+}
.

Definition 4.6
.

\mathcal{D}_{0}
is the localization of
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{+}
at the multiplicative set generated by
\{\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{A}),\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{B})\}
.
We define the
fourth quadrant subalgebra

\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}\subset\mathcal{D}_{0}
to be the subalgebra generated by the entries of
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}^{-1}
.

4.2.2.
PBW and function representation

Denote the entries of
\mathbf{B}^{-1}
by
\{b^{\prime}_{ij}\}
.
Let
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV,\mathbb{Z}}}\subset\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}
be the
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{q}^{\pm 1}]
-subalgebra generated by the generators
\{a_{ij},b^{\prime}_{ij}\}_{i,j=1}^{n}
.
Similar to
4.1.5
, standard monomial for
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV},\mathbb{Z}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}
is a product

a_{i_{1}j_{1}}\cdots a_{i_{M}j_{M}}b_{k_{1}l_{1}}^{\prime}\cdots b_{k_{N}l_{N}}^{\prime}

where the

•

the sequence of pairs of indices
(i_{1},j_{1}),\ldots(i_{M},j_{M})
is in lexicographic order;

•

the sequence of pairs of indices
(k_{1},l_{1}),\ldots(k_{N},l_{N})
is in
reverse
lexicographic order.

Proposition 4.7

(
[
Jor14
,
VV10
]
)
.

We have the following:

(1)

The standard monomials form a basis of
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV},\mathbb{Z}}
.
Thus,
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}
is a flat deformation of the coordinate ring of
T^{*}(\mathrm{Mat}_{n\times n})
.

(2)

\mathcal{D}_{0}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}_{G}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{G}
as a
\mathcal{U}
-module.
Here, the first tensorand corresponds to the subalgebra generated by the entries of
\mathbf{A}
while the second tensorand corresponds to that of
\mathbf{B}
.

We denote by
\mathcal{O}_{G}^{\mathbf{A}},\mathcal{O}_{G}^{\mathbf{B}}\subset\mathcal{D}_{0}
the subalgebras generated by the entries of
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
, respectively, so

\mathcal{D}_{0}\cong\mathcal{O}_{G}^{\mathbf{A}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{G}^{\mathbf{B}}

Consequently, we can define a function representation on

\mathcal{O}_{G}^{\mathbf{A}}\cong\mathcal{D}_{0}\big/\mathcal{D}_{0}\left(\ker\epsilon_{\mathcal{O}_{G}^{\mathbf{B}}}\right)

Here,
\epsilon_{\mathcal{O}_{G}^{\mathbf{B}}}
is the counit of
\mathcal{O}_{G}^{\mathbf{B}}
(cf.
3.2.1
).

Proposition 4.8

(
[
VV10
]
)
.

For
v^{*}\otimes v\in V^{*}\otimes V\subset\mathcal{O}^{\mathbf{A}}_{G}
, viewed as an element of the function representation, and
b\in\mathcal{O}_{G}^{\mathbf{B}}
,

b\cdot(v^{*}\otimes v)=\left(\kappa(b)\bullet v^{*}\right)\otimes v

Here,
\bullet
denotes the
\mathcal{U}
-action on
V^{*}
.

4.2.3.
Homomorphism into
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}

For a sequence of noncommuting elements
x_{1},\ldots,x_{M}
, let

\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=1}^{M}}x_{a}:=x_{1}\cdots x_{M}

For
\ell>1
, consider the following two matrices of elements of
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{\circ}

\displaystyle:=\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=1}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}^{(a)}=\mathbf{X}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}^{(2)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(\ell)},

\displaystyle{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{X}^{(1)}\mathbf{D}^{(1)}

where
\mathbf{I}:=\sum_{i}E_{ii}\otimes 1
is the identity matrix.

Lemma 4.9
.

The map

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbf{X}^{\circ},

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}^{-1}

\displaystyle\mapsto{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}

defines an algebra homomorphism
\Phi^{\ell}:\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
.
If we endow
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}
with the
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
action whereby
\mathcal{U}_{(1)}
acts via the usual
\mathcal{U}
-action and
\mathcal{U}_{(a)}
acts trivially for
a\not=1
, then
\Phi^{\ell}
is
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
-equivariant.

Proof.

We need to check that
\mathbf{X}^{\circ}
and
{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}
satisfy the relations defining
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}
.
For (
4.15
), let us first consider the case
\ell\geq 3
.
We begin by moving
\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(1)}
all the way to the left:

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}^{\circ}_{1}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}^{\circ}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(2)}\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=3}^{\ell-1}}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\right)\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(\ell)}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(1)}}_{(\ref{XXad})}\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=2}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(2)}\underbrace{\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=3}^{\ell-1}}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\right)\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(1)}}_{(\ref{XXcom})}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(\ell)}\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=2}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(1)}\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(2)}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(1)}}_{(\ref{XXad})}\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=3}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\right)\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=2}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(1)}}_{(\ref{Xaa})}\mathbf{R}_{21}\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=2}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\right)\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=2}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}\right)

(4.18)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(1)}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}_{21}\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=2}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\right)\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=2}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}\right)

After this, moves are identical: iterate (
4.4
), then an application of (
4.3
), (
4.2
), and (
4.3
) again.
We illustrate this for the first step following (
4.18
):

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(1)}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(2)}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(3)}\underbrace{\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=4}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\right)\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(2)}}_{(\ref{XXcom})}\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=3}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(1)}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(2)}\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(3)}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(2)}}_{(\ref{XXad})}\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=4}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\right)\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=3}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(1)}\mathbf{R}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(2)}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(2)}}_{(\ref{Xaa})}\mathbf{R}_{21}\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=3}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\right)\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=3}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(1)}\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(1)}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(2)}}_{(\ref{XXad})}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(2)}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}_{21}\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=3}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\right)\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=3}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(2)}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(2)}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{R}_{21}\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=3}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\right)\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=3}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}\right)

The closing step entails commuting
\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(\ell)}
to the left:

\displaystyle=\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=1}^{\ell-1}}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}\right)\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=1}^{\ell-1}}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\right)\mathbf{R}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(\ell)}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(\ell)}}_{(\ref{Xaa})}

\displaystyle=\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=1}^{\ell-1}}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}\right)\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=1}^{\ell-2}}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\right)\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(\ell-1)}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(\ell)}}_{(\ref{XXad})}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(\ell)}\mathbf{R}

\displaystyle=\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=1}^{\ell-1}}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}\right)\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(1)}\underbrace{\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=2}^{\ell-2}}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\right)\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(\ell)}}_{(\ref{XXcom})}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(\ell-1)}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(\ell)}\mathbf{R}

\displaystyle=\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=1}^{\ell-1}}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}\right)\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(\ell)}}_{(\ref{XXad})}\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=2}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\right)\mathbf{R}

\displaystyle=\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=1}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}\right)\mathbf{R}_{21}\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=1}^{\ell}}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\right)\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{\circ}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{\circ}\mathbf{R}

For
\ell=2
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{\circ}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(1)}\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(2)}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(1)}}_{(\ref{XXad2})}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(1)}}_{(\ref{Xaa})}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(2)}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(2)}}_{(\ref{Xaa})}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(1)}\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(1)}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(2)}}_{(\ref{XXad2})}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(2)}\mathbf{R}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(2)}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(2)}\mathbf{R}=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{\circ}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{\circ}\mathbf{R}

The remaining relations rely on results presented in
5.1.1
; these results do not rely on the present material.
Relation (
4.16
) was proved by Jordan
[
Jor14
]
(cf. Proposition
5.1
(2) below).
For (
4.17
), we have:

\displaystyle{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\circ}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{2}}_{(\ref{YL1})}\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=2}^{\ell-1}}\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{2}\right)\mathbf{X}^{(\ell)}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{2}{}^{L}\underbrace{\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{1}\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=2}^{\ell-1}}\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{2}\right)}_{(\ref{YL2})}\mathbf{X}^{(\ell)}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{2}\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=2}^{\ell-1}}\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{2}\right)\underbrace{{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{1}\mathbf{X}^{(\ell)}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}}_{(\ref{YL3})}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{2}\left(\overset{\curvearrowright}{\prod_{a=2}^{\ell-1}}\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{2}\right)\mathbf{X}^{(\ell)}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}^{\circ}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{1}\qed

4.2.4.
Localization in
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}

By Lemma
4.9
, we can make sense of the element

\textstyle\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ}):=\Phi^{\ell}\left(\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{A})\right)\in\mathcal{D}_{\ell}

Lemma 4.10
.

The quantum determinant
\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})
generates an Ore set in
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
.

Proof.

First, let us consider the commutation relations between
\mathbf{X}^{\circ}
and
\{\mathbf{X}^{(a)}\}
.
Using (
4.2
)-(
4.4
), it is easy to see that

(4.19)

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{1}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{\circ}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{1}

(4.20)

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{1}^{\circ}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(\ell)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(\ell)}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{\circ}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}^{\circ}_{2}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(a)}\hbox{ for }a\not=1,\ell

Equations (
4.19
) and (
4.20
) can be written as follows.
Let
\bullet
denote the action of
\mathcal{U}
on
\mathbb{V}
or
\mathbb{V}^{*}
, and let
\bowtie
denote the action of
\mathcal{U}
on any subspace
V^{*}\otimes V\subset\mathcal{O}_{G}
via the coproduct
\Delta_{\mathcal{U}}
.
We will recycle notation from
4.1.3
: for
e^{i}_{(1)}\boxtimes e_{j}^{(1)}=x_{ij}^{(1)}
,
e^{i}_{(\ell)}\boxtimes e_{j}^{(\ell)}=x_{ij}^{(\ell)}
, and
x^{\circ}_{kl}:=\Phi^{\ell}(a_{kl})
,

\displaystyle\left(e^{i}_{(1)}\boxtimes e_{j}^{(1)}\right)x^{\circ}_{kl}

\displaystyle=\left(\iota_{\mathcal{U}}({}_{s}r)\bowtie x^{\circ}_{kl}\right)\left(r_{s}\bullet e^{i}_{(1)}\boxtimes e_{j}^{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle x^{\circ}_{kl}\left(e^{i}_{(\ell)}\boxtimes e_{j}^{(\ell)}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(e^{i}_{(\ell)}\boxtimes r_{s}\bullet e_{j}^{(\ell)}\right)\left(\iota_{\mathcal{U}}(r_{s})\bowtie x^{\circ}_{kl}\right)

By (
3.4
) and the fact that
\bowtie
is an algebra action, we have that for any product
x^{\circ}
in the entries of
\mathbf{X}^{\circ}
,

\displaystyle\left(e^{i}_{(1)}\boxtimes e_{j}^{(1)}\right)x^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\left(\iota_{\mathcal{U}}({}_{s}r)\bowtie x^{\circ}\right)\left(r_{s}\bullet e^{i}_{(1)}\boxtimes e_{j}^{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle x^{\circ}\left(e^{i}_{(\ell)}\boxtimes e_{j}^{(\ell)}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(e^{i}_{(\ell)}\boxtimes r_{s}\bullet e_{j}^{(\ell)}\right)\left(\iota_{\mathcal{U}}(r_{s})\bowtie x^{\circ}\right)

Because
\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})
is
\mathcal{U}
-invariant, we conclude that it commutes with
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}
.

For
\{\mathbf{D}^{(a)}\}
, we can show similar relations.
Let us consider the easiest case of
a\not=1,\ell
:

(4.21)

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(a)}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(1)}\cdots\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a-1)}\left(\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}\right)_{*j}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{R}\left(\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a+1)}\right)_{j*}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a+2)}\cdots\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(\ell)}\mathbf{D}^{(a)}_{1}+\hbox{ terms in }\mathcal{O}_{\ell}

Here, the subscripts
*j
and
j*
emphasize that we are considering the matrix units
E_{*j}
and
E_{j*}
, where
*
is whatever makes the matrix products work.
Moreover, we use an Einstein notation and thus there is an implicit summation over
j
.
Thus, all we are saying is that the
j
-indices must match.
We can rewrite (
4.21
) using
\bar{\kappa}
:

(4.22)

\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(a)}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{\circ}=\bar{\kappa}(\mathrm{coev}_{\mathbb{V}}(1))\big|_{\mathbb{V}^{*}}\mathbf{X}^{\circ}_{2}\mathbf{D}^{(a)}_{1}+\hbox{ terms in }\mathcal{O}_{\ell}

Because
\bar{\kappa}(\mathrm{coev}_{\mathbb{V}}(1))\in\mathcal{U}
is central, it acts on
\mathbb{V}^{*}
by a constant (which is easy to see is nonzero).
Similarly, we have

(4.23)

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{\circ}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\kappa(\mathrm{coev}_{\mathbb{V}}(1))\big|_{\mathbb{V}}\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(1)}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}^{\circ}\mathbf{R}+\hbox{ terms in }\mathcal{O}_{\ell}

(4.24)

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(\ell)}\mathbf{X}^{\circ}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}^{\circ}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{D}_{1}^{(\ell)}+\hbox{ terms in }\mathcal{O}_{\ell}

We can endow
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
with the structure of a filtered algebra by setting

\displaystyle\deg(x_{ij})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\deg(\partial_{ij})=1

Through similar reasoning as in the first paragraph, we have that in the associated graded,
\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})
commutes up to scalar with
\partial_{ij}^{(a)}
. We have cancelability because
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
is an integral domain, and for the Ore conditions, we can solve the problem at each step in the filtration.
At filtration degree 0, the problem is trivial because
\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})
is central in
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}
.
∎

Corollary 4.11
.

The matrices
\mathbf{X}^{(a)}
are invertible in
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}\left[\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})^{-1}\right]
.

Proof.

Recall the FRT quantum determinants
\{\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{(a)})\}\in\mathcal{O}_{\ell}
from
4.1.4
.
By Corollary
4.4
, they generate an Ore set in
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}
.
Proposition 9.8 of
[
KS97
]
) implies that
\mathbf{X}^{(a)}
has a left and right inverse in
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}\left[\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{(a)})^{-1}\right]
.
Let

\textstyle\mathcal{O}_{\ell}^{\star}:=\mathcal{O}_{\ell}\left[\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})^{-1},\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{(1)})^{-1},\ldots,\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{(\ell)})^{-1}\right]

A consequence of Theorem
4.5
is that
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}
is an integral domain.
Thus,
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}[\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})^{-1}]
includes into
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}^{\star}
.
We first have that

\mathbf{X}^{(2)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(\ell)}\left(\mathbf{X}^{\circ}\right)^{-1}

is a
right
inverse of
\mathbf{X}^{(1)}
.
Since
\mathbf{X}^{(1)}
has a left-right inverse over
\mathcal{O}_{\ell}^{\star}
, it follows that the expression above is also a left inverse.
Similarly,

\left(\mathbf{X}^{\circ}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}

is a left and hence left-right inverse to
\mathbf{X}^{(2)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(\ell)}
.
Proceeding by induction, if we have an inverse to
