\mathbf{X}^{(a)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(\ell)}
, then

\mathbf{X}^{(a+1)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(\ell)}\left(\mathbf{X}^{(a)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(\ell)}\right)^{-1}

is a right inverse of
\mathbf{X}^{(a)}
.
By similar arguments, this must be a left-right inverse.
∎

4.3.
The case
\mathbf{\ell=1}

We will introduce a new algebra of quantum differential operators that is closer in spirit to the construction of Crawley-Boevey–Shaw
[
CBS06
]
for the quiver (
4.1
) for
\ell=1
.

4.3.1.
Definition of
\mathcal{D}_{1}

The algebra
\mathcal{D}_{1}
has generators

\left\{x_{ij},\partial_{ij}\,\middle|\,1\leq i,j\leq n\right\}

which we place into the matrices

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}\otimes x_{ij},

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}\otimes\partial_{ij}

The relations are:

(4.25)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R};

(4.26)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{D}_{2}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21};

(4.27)

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{D}_{1}+(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\mathbf{R}\mathbf{\Omega}

This presentation describes
\mathcal{D}_{1}
as a quotient of the tensor algebra of
\mathbb{V^{*}}\otimes\mathbb{V}\oplus\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{V}^{*}
.
Let us denote the first summand by
\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{X}}
and the second by
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}^{*}
and use similar notation for the bases, e.g.
\{e^{i}_{\mathbf{X}}\}\subset\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}
.
The quotient map is then:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}\left(\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{X}}\oplus\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{1}

\displaystyle e^{i}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes e_{j}^{\mathbf{X}}

\displaystyle\mapsto x_{ij}

\displaystyle\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(e^{i}_{\mathbf{D}}\otimes e_{j}^{\mathbf{D}})

\displaystyle\mapsto\partial_{ij}

Note that we are identifying
\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}\cong\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}\otimes\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{D}}
using
\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}
.

Proposition 4.12
.

\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant algebra.

Proof.

Relations (
4.25
)-(
4.27
) are, respectively, the images of the maps

\displaystyle\beta_{\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{X}},\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}}^{-1}\left(\beta_{\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{X}},\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}}-\beta_{\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{X}},\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{X}}}\right):\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{X}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{X}}

\displaystyle\beta_{\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{D}},\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}}\left(\beta_{\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{D}},\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{D}}}^{-1}-\beta_{\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}},\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}}^{-1}\right):\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{D}}\otimes\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{D}}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\beta_{\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{X}}^{*},\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}}\beta_{\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}},\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{X}}}^{-1}\vskip 3.61371pt-\beta_{\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{X}},\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{D}}}^{-1}\beta_{\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{D}},\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}}\\
-(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\mathrm{ev}_{\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{V}}\beta_{\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{D}},\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}}\end{array}\right):\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{D}}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{X}}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\left(\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}\otimes\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{X}}\right)\\
\oplus\left(\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}\right)\oplus\mathbb{1}\end{array}\right)

followed by
\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}
for any
\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{D}}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}
tensorand.
Thus, the relations describe a
\mathcal{U}
-submodule of the tensor algebra and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant.
∎

4.3.2.
PBW for
\mathcal{D}_{1}

The notion of standard monomial for
\mathcal{D}_{1}
will be slightly different from that of
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
.
Namely, we define a standard monomial to be

\partial_{k_{1}l_{1}}\cdots\partial_{k_{N}l_{N}}x_{i_{1}j_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{M}j_{M}}

where:

•

the sequence of pairs
(i_{1},j_{1}),\ldots,(i_{M},j_{M})
are in increasing lexicographic order

•

and the sequence of pairs
(k_{1},l_{1}),\ldots,(k_{N},l_{N})
are in
decreasing
lexicographic order.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be the
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{q}^{\pm 1}]
-subalgebra generated by
\{x_{ij},\partial_{ij}\}
.

Proposition 4.13
.

The standard monomials are a basis of
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Consequently,
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is a flat deformation of the coordinate ring of
T^{*}(\mathrm{Mat}_{n\times n})
.

Proof.

Observe that the subalgebra generated by the coordinates of
\mathbf{X}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}
and the subalgebra generated by the coordinates of
\mathbf{D}
is isomorphic to
\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}\,)\subset\mathcal{O}_{G}
.
It is known that the analogous standard monomials are linearly independent in
\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}

[
KS97
]
, and the relations show that any monomial in
\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}
can be straightened into a
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{q}^{\pm 1}]
-linear combination of the standard ones (cf.
[
Jor14
]
Example 4.10).
Thus, we get the desired statement for monomials in
\{x_{ij}\}
, and the analogous statement for monomials in the
\{\partial_{ij}\}
holds after applying the algebra anti-automorphism
\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}
.
To upgrade this to
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, it suffices to show that:

(1)

x_{kl}\partial_{ij}
lies in the
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{q}^{\pm 1}]
-span of standard monomials;

(2)

\mathcal{D}_{1}\cong\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}\,)\otimes\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}\,
.

For the first statement, let us unpack (
4.27
):

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{q}^{\delta_{i,k}-\delta_{i,l}}x_{kl}\partial_{ij}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{q}^{-\delta_{j,k}+\delta_{j,l}}\partial_{ij}x_{kl}-(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\bigg(\mathsf{q}^{\delta_{i,j}}\delta_{i,l}\delta_{j,k}+(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\theta(j-l)\delta_{i,j}\delta_{k,l}\bigg)

(4.28)

\displaystyle\quad-(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\bigg(\underbrace{\theta(k-i)x_{il}\partial_{kj}}_{(\mathrm{a})}+\underbrace{\delta_{i,l}\sum_{m>i}x_{km}\partial_{mj}}_{(\mathrm{b})}\bigg)-(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})^{2}\underbrace{\theta(k-i)\delta_{k,l}\sum_{m>k}x_{im}\partial_{mj}}_{(\mathrm{c})}

\displaystyle\quad+(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\bigg(\theta(l-j)\partial_{il}x_{kj}-\delta_{j,k}\sum_{m<j}\partial_{im}x_{ml}\bigg)-(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})^{2}\theta(l-j)\delta_{k,l}\sum_{m<l}\partial_{im}x_{mj}

Here,
\theta(x):\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the Heaviside theta function:

\theta(x)=\begin{cases}1&x>0\\
0&x\leq 0\end{cases}

Only the terms in the second line (
4.28
) are not standard monomials, and our goal is to show that these terms disappear after multiple iterations of the relation.
First consider a term from (b).
Clearly, it cannot simultaneously satisfy the conditions for (c).
If it simultaneously satisfies the conditions for (a), then the result of (a) will be of the form
x_{ii}\partial_{kj}
with
k>i
, which does not satisfy any of the conditions for (a), (b), or (c).
This shows that iterating (a) will terminate once
i=n
.
Next, note that a term from (a) can yield at most a term in (b) after applying the relation, which terminates upon iteration.
Finally, a term from (c) will yield a term in (b) and thus will terminate as well.

For the second statement, the proof is similar to that of Theorem 5.3 from
[
Jor14
]
.
Let us recycle notation from
4.3.1
.
Namely, we view:

•

\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}
as a quotient of the tensor algebra
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{X}}:=\mathcal{T}(\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{X}})
by the ideal
I_{\mathbf{X}}
generated by the relation (
4.25
);

•

\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}\,)
as a quotient of the tensor algebra
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{D}}:=\mathcal{T}(\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{D}}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}})
by the ideal
I_{\mathbf{D}}
generated by the relation
(\ref{D1rel2})
;

•

and
\mathcal{D}_{1}
as a quotient of the tensor algebra
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{D}\mathbf{X}}:=\mathcal{T}(\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{D}}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{D}}\oplus\mathbb{V}^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{X}})
by the ideal generated by
I_{\mathbf{X}}
,
I_{\mathbf{D}}
, and the ideal
I_{\mathbf{D}\mathbf{X}}
generated by (
4.27
).

As in
loc. cit.
, it suffices to show that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{X}}I_{\mathbf{D}}

\displaystyle\subset I_{\mathbf{D}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{X}}+I_{\mathbf{D}\mathbf{X}},

\displaystyle I_{\mathbf{X}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{D}}

\displaystyle\subset\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{D}}I_{\mathbf{X}}+I_{\mathbf{D}\mathbf{X}}

We will only show the second containment.
Just like in
loc. cit.
, this is equivalent to showing that the entries of

\left(\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{X}_{2}-\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{12}\right)\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{R}_{23}\mathbf{D}_{3}

lie in
I_{\mathbf{X}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{D}}+I_{\mathbf{D}\mathbf{X}}
without using (
4.25
) or (
4.26
).
To that end:

\displaystyle\quad\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{12}\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{13}}_{\mathrm{commute}}\mathbf{R}_{23}\mathbf{D}_{3}-\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{R}_{23}}_{(\ref{QYBE})}\mathbf{D}_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{R}_{13}\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{23}\mathbf{D}_{3}}_{(\ref{D1rel3})}-\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{23}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{D}_{3}}_{\mathrm{commute}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{R}_{32}^{-1}}_{(\ref{QYBE})}\mathbf{D}_{3}\mathbf{R}_{23}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{23}-\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{R}_{23}\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{D}_{3}}_{(\ref{D1rel3})}\mathbf{R}_{12}

\displaystyle\quad-(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{\Omega}_{23}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{21}\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{32}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{D}_{3}}_{\mathrm{commute}}\mathbf{R}_{23}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{23}-\mathbf{X}_{2}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{R}_{23}\mathbf{R}_{31}^{-1}}_{(\ref{QYBE})}\mathbf{D}_{3}\mathbf{R}_{13}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{R}_{12}

\displaystyle\quad+(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\bigg(\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{R}_{23}\mathbf{\Omega}_{13}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{12}}_{(\ref{HeckeCond})}-\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{21}}_{(\ref{HeckeCond})}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{\Omega}_{23}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{R}_{32}^{-1}\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{D}_{3}}_{(\ref{D1rel3})}\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{R}_{23}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{23}-\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{31}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{23}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{D}_{3}}_{\mathrm{commute}}\mathbf{R}_{13}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{R}_{12}

\displaystyle\quad+(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\bigg(\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{R}_{23}\underbrace{\mathbf{\Omega}_{13}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}}_{\mathrm{commute}}-\mathbf{R}_{12}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{\Omega}_{23}\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad+(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})^{2}\bigg(\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{R}_{23}\mathbf{\Omega}_{13}\mathbf{\Omega}_{12}}_{\mathrm{commute}}-\mathbf{\Omega}_{12}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{\Omega}_{23}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{R}_{32}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{31}^{-1}}_{(\ref{QYBE})}\mathbf{D}_{3}\mathbf{R}_{13}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{R}_{23}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{23}-\mathbf{R}_{31}^{-1}\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{23}\mathbf{D}_{3}}_{(\ref{D1rel3})}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{R}_{13}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{R}_{12}

\displaystyle\quad+(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\bigg(\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{\Omega}_{13}-\mathbf{R}_{12}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{\Omega}_{23}-\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{R}_{32}^{-1}\underbrace{\mathbf{\Omega}_{13}\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{R}_{23}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{23}}_{\mathrm{commute}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{31}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{32}^{-1}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{D}_{3}}_{\mathrm{commute}}\mathbf{R}_{13}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{R}_{23}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{23}-\mathbf{R}_{31}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{32}^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{3}\mathbf{R}_{23}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{23}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{R}_{13}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{R}_{12}

\displaystyle\quad+(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\bigg(\mathbf{R}_{31}^{-1}\underbrace{\mathbf{\Omega}_{23}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{R}_{13}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{R}_{12}}_{\mathrm{commute}}-\mathbf{R}_{12}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{\Omega}_{23}\bigg)

(4.29)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{31}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{32}^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{3}\bigg(\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{R}_{13}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{R}_{12}\mathbf{R}_{23}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{23}-\mathbf{R}_{23}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{23}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{R}_{13}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{13}\mathbf{R}_{12}\bigg)

The expression in the parentheses in the final line (
4.29
) is the original relation (
4.25
) with the generating matrices
\{\mathbf{X}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{2}\}
conjugated by the
R
-matrix.
Its entries are thus in
I_{\mathbf{X}}
, and therefore the entries of the entire line (
4.29
) lie in
I_{\mathbf{X}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathbf{D}}
.
∎

4.3.3.
Interpretation of the cross-relation

We can rewrite (
4.27
) in two different ways:

(4.30)

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}+(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\mathbf{\Omega}

(4.31)

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{D}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}\mathbf{D}_{2}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}-(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\mathbf{\Omega}

Let us view the matrix
\mathbf{X}
as a map
\mathbb{V}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{V}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{1}
, and likewise, let us view
\mathbf{D}
as a map
\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(\mathbb{V}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{V})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{1}
.
If we let
m_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}
denote the multiplication in
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, then upon moving the
R
-matrices from the left to the right, equations (
4.30
) and (
4.31
) can be drawn as follows:

We will translate this back to algebra.
Let
\bullet
denote the action of
\mathcal{U}
on
\mathbb{V}
or
\mathbb{V}^{*}
and let
\bowtie
denote the action of
\mathcal{U}
on
\mathcal{O}_{G}\supset\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}\,,\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}\,)
.
For the rest of this paragraph, we will conflate
\mathcal{O}_{G}
with
\kappa(\mathcal{O}_{G})
and Sweedler notation will be for the coproduct
\Delta_{\mathcal{U}}
.
For
v^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes v_{\mathbf{X}}\in\mathbb{V}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{V}=\mathrm{im}(\mathbf{X})
,
v_{\mathbf{D}}\otimes v^{*}_{\mathbf{D}}\in\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{V}^{*}=\mathrm{im}(\mathbf{D})
,
m=m_{kl}\in\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}
, and
\underline{m}=\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(m_{kl})\in\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}\,)
.

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}(\underline{m})(v^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes v_{\mathbf{X}})

\displaystyle=\left[\underline{m}_{(1)}\bullet v^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\iota_{\mathcal{U}}({}_{s}r)\bullet v_{\mathbf{X}}\right]\mathbf{D}(r_{s}\bowtie\underline{m}_{(2)})+\mathrm{constant}

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}(m)(v_{\mathbf{D}}\otimes v^{*}_{\mathbf{D}})

\displaystyle=\left[r_{s}\bullet v_{\mathbf{D}}\otimes({}_{s}r\bowtie m)_{(1)}\bullet v^{*}_{\mathbf{D}}\right]\mathbf{X}\left(({}_{s}r\bowtie m)_{(2)}\right)+\mathrm{constant}

Now let us extend the notation
\mathbf{X}:\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}\,\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathbf{D}:\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}\,)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{1}
.
Because of (
3.2
) and (
3.3
), the formulas above interact well with the product in the respective domains of
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{D}
: for
m^{\prime}=m_{k^{\prime}l^{\prime}}
and
\underline{m}^{\prime}=\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(m_{k^{\prime}l^{\prime}})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}(\underline{m}^{\prime}\underline{m})(v^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes v_{\mathbf{X}})

\displaystyle=\left[\underline{m}^{\prime}_{(1)}\underline{m}_{(1)}\bullet v^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\iota_{\mathcal{U}}({}_{t}r)\iota_{\mathcal{U}}({}_{s}r)\bullet v_{\mathbf{X}}\right]\mathbf{D}\left((r_{t}\bowtie\underline{m}^{\prime}_{(2)})(r_{s}\bowtie\underline{m}_{(2)})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\hbox{ terms in }\{\partial_{ij}\}

\displaystyle=\left[(\underline{m}^{\prime}\underline{m})_{(1)}\bullet v^{*}_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\iota_{\mathcal{U}}({}_{s}r)\bullet v_{\mathbf{X}}\right]\mathbf{D}\left(r_{s}\bowtie(\underline{m}^{\prime}\underline{m})_{(2)}\right)+\hbox{ terms in }\{\partial_{ij}\}

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}(m^{\prime}m)(v_{\mathbf{D}}\otimes v^{*}_{\mathbf{D}})

\displaystyle=\left[r_{t}r_{s}\bullet v_{\mathbf{D}}\otimes({}_{t}r\bowtie m^{\prime})_{(1)}({}_{s}r\bowtie m)_{(1)}\bullet v^{*}_{\mathbf{D}}\right]\mathbf{X}\left(({}_{t}r\bowtie m^{\prime})_{(2)}({}_{s}r\bowtie m)_{(2)}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\hbox{ terms in }\{x_{ij}\}

\displaystyle=\left[r_{s}\bullet v_{\mathbf{D}}\otimes({}_{s}r\bowtie m^{\prime}m)_{(1)}\bullet v^{*}_{\mathbf{D}}\right]\mathbf{X}\left(({}_{s}r\bowtie m^{\prime}m)_{(2)}\right)+\hbox{ terms in }\{x_{ij}\}

and likewise for iterated products.
Because
\kappa(\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{M}))=\mathsf{q}^{-2\omega_{n}}
is grouplike, we can conclude:

\displaystyle\textstyle\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{D})x_{ij}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{q}^{2}x_{ij}+\hbox{ terms in }\{\partial_{kl}\}

(4.32)

\displaystyle\textstyle\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X})\partial_{ij}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{q}^{-2}\partial_{ij}+\hbox{ terms in }\{x_{kl}\}

Proposition 4.14
.

The elements
\{\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{D}),\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X})\}
generate an Ore set in
\mathcal{D}_{1}
.

Proof.

We will only prove the case of
\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X})
.
A consequence of Proposition
4.13
is that
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is an integral domain, so cancellability is trivial.
We can endow
\mathcal{D}_{1}
with a filtration such that

\displaystyle\deg(x_{ij})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\deg(\partial_{ij})

\displaystyle=1

Equation (
4.32
) then states that
\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X})

\mathsf{q}^{2}
-commutes with elements of
\{\partial_{ij}\}
in the associated graded.
The proof proceeds as in that of Lemma
4.10
.
∎

4.3.4.
Homomorphism into
\mathcal{D}_{0}

We discovered the relations (
4.25
)-(
4.27
) by emulating the moves from 3.1 of
[
BEF20
]
and proving the following:

Lemma 4.15
.

For
\mathsf{Z}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, the map

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbf{A},

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbf{A}^{-1}\left(\mathsf{Z}^{-1}\mathbf{B}^{-1}-\mathbf{I}\right)

defines a
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant algebra homomorphism
\Psi_{\mathsf{Z}}^{1}:\mathcal{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{0}
.

Proof.

The
\mathcal{U}
-equivariance statement follows from the homomorphism statement because the matrix maps are
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant and both
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{0}
are
\mathcal{U}
-module algebras.
To show that
\Psi_{\mathsf{Z}}^{1}
is an algebra homomorphism, we only need to check (
4.26
) and (
4.27
).
For (
4.26
), let us first unwind both sides of the relation:

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{-1}\Psi_{\mathsf{Z}}^{1}(\mathbf{D}_{1})\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\Psi_{\mathsf{Z}}^{1}(\mathbf{D}_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Z}^{-2}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{-1}_{2}\mathbf{B}_{2}^{-1}}_{(1)}+\underbrace{\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}}_{(2)}

\displaystyle\quad-\mathsf{Z}^{-1}\bigg(\underbrace{\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{-1}_{1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}\mathbf{A}^{-1}_{2}\mathbf{B}_{2}^{-1}+\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}}_{(3)}\bigg)

\displaystyle\Psi^{1}(\mathbf{D}_{2})\mathbf{R}^{-1}\Psi^{1}(\mathbf{D}_{1})\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Z}^{-2}\underbrace{\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}}_{(1)}+\underbrace{\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}}_{(2)}

\displaystyle\quad-\mathsf{Z}^{-1}\bigg(\underbrace{\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}+\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}}_{(3)}\bigg)

We will show that parts labeled (1)-(4) in both expressions are equal.

(1)

This one is quite direct:

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\underbrace{\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{-1}_{2}}_{(\ref{DAB})}\mathbf{B}_{2}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}}_{(\ref{DAA})}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{2}^{-1}}_{(\ref{DBB})}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}}_{(\ref{DAB})}\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}

(2)

This follows from (
4.15
).

(3)

We use the Hecke relations (
3.7
) to adjust some of the
R
-matrices at the ends:

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{-1}_{1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}\mathbf{A}^{-1}_{2}\mathbf{B}_{2}^{-1}+\underbrace{\mathbf{R}^{-1}}_{(\ref{HeckeCond})}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}

\displaystyle\overset{?}{=}\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}}_{(\ref{HeckeCond})}+\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{-1}_{1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}\mathbf{A}^{-1}_{2}\mathbf{B}_{2}^{-1}+\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}

\displaystyle\overset{?}{=}\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}

\displaystyle-(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\underbrace{\mathbf{\Omega}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}}_{\mathrm{commute}}

\displaystyle\quad-(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{\Omega}

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{-1}_{1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}\mathbf{A}^{-1}_{2}\mathbf{B}_{2}^{-1}+\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}

\displaystyle\overset{?}{=}\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}

We then have:

\displaystyle\quad\underbrace{\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}^{-1}_{1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}\mathbf{A}^{-1}_{2}}_{(\ref{DAA})}\mathbf{B}_{2}^{-1}+\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\underbrace{\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}}_{(\ref{DAB})}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}\mathbf{B}_{2}^{-1}}_{(\ref{DAB})}+\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}}_{(\ref{DAA})}\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{A}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}

Finally, for (
4.27
), we have:

\displaystyle\Psi_{\mathsf{Z}}^{1}(\mathbf{D}_{1})\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\Psi_{\mathsf{Z}}^{1}(\mathbf{X}_{2})\mathbf{R}_{21}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Z}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\underbrace{\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}}_{(\ref{DAB})}-\underbrace{\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}}_{(\ref{DAA})}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Z}^{-1}\underbrace{\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}}_{(\ref{DAA})}\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}-\mathbf{R}\mathbf{A}_{2}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}^{-1}}_{(\ref{HeckeCond})}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Z}^{-1}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{B}_{1}^{-1}-\left(\mathbf{R}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}-(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\mathbf{R}\mathbf{A}_{2}\mathbf{\Omega}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}\Psi_{\mathsf{Z}}^{1}(\mathbf{X}_{2})\mathbf{R}_{21}\Psi_{\mathsf{Z}}^{1}(\mathbf{D}_{1})+(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\mathbf{R}\mathbf{\Omega}\qed

4.4.
Quantum Weyl algebra

Finally, we address the arrow connecting to the square vertex in (
4.1
).
The algebra assigned to this arrow is the
quantum Weyl algebra
, first defined in
[
GZ95
]
.

4.4.1.
Definition of
\mathcal{W}
and PBW

The rank
n
quantum Weyl algebra
\mathcal{W}
has generators

\{x_{i},\partial_{i}\,|\,1\leq i\leq n\}

and relations

(4.33)

\displaystyle\begin{split}x_{i}x_{j}&=\mathsf{q}x_{j}x_{i}\hbox{ for }i>j\\
\partial_{i}\partial_{j}&=\mathsf{q}^{-1}\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\hbox{ for }i>j\\
\partial_{i}x_{j}&=\mathsf{q}x_{j}\partial_{i}\hbox{ for }i\not=j\\
\partial_{i}x_{i}&=1+\mathsf{q}^{2}x_{i}\partial_{i}+(\mathsf{q}^{2}-1)\sum_{j<i}x_{j}\partial_{j}\end{split}

We can put these generators into vectors:

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}&\cdots&x_{n}\end{pmatrix}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{i}\otimes x_{i},

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}

\displaystyle:=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\partial_{n}\end{pmatrix}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}\otimes\partial_{i}

With this notation, the relations (
4.33
) can be expressed using the
R
-matrix:

(4.34)

\displaystyle\mathsf{q}\mathbf{x}_{1}\mathbf{x}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}_{2}\mathbf{x}_{1}\mathbf{R}

\displaystyle\mathsf{q}\mathbf{d}_{1}\mathbf{d}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{d}_{2}\mathbf{d}_{1}

\displaystyle\mathsf{q}^{-1}\mathbf{d}_{2}\mathbf{x}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{x}_{1}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{d}_{2}+\mathsf{q}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{i}\otimes e_{i}\otimes 1

Let

\widetilde{\partial}_{i}:=(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\partial_{i}

and let
\mathcal{W}^{\mathbb{Z}}
to be the
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{q}^{\pm 1}]
-subalgebra generated by
\{x_{i},\widetilde{\partial}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
.
Define a standard monomial for
\mathcal{W}^{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\mathcal{W}
to be

x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{M}}\widetilde{\partial}_{j_{1}}\cdots\widetilde{\partial}_{j_{N}}

such that the sequences
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{M})
and
(j_{1},\ldots,j_{N})
are in nondecreasing order.

Proposition 4.16

(
[
GZ95
,
Jor14
]
)
.

The standard monomials form a
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{q}^{\pm 1}]
-basis of
\mathcal{W}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Thus,
\mathcal{W}
is a flat deformation of
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\mathbb{C}^{n})
.

4.4.2.
Quantum symmetric algebra

Because
\mathbf{R}
satisfies the Hecke relation (
3.7
), we can construct a
\mathsf{q}
-symmetrizer and apply it to
\mathbb{V}^{\otimes m}
nd
(\mathbb{V}^{*})^{\otimes m}
to obtain the
m
th quantum symmetric powers

S_{\mathsf{q}}^{m}\mathbb{V}
and
S_{\mathsf{q}}^{m}\mathbb{V}^{*}
, respectively.
Moreover, we obtain
quantum symmetric algebras
:

\displaystyle S_{\mathsf{q}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}:=\bigoplus_{m=0}^{\infty}S_{\mathsf{q}}^{m}\mathbb{V},

\displaystyle S_{\mathsf{q}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}^{*}:=\bigoplus_{m=0}^{\infty}S_{\mathsf{q}}^{m}\mathbb{V}^{*}

These algebras are
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant.

In what follows, let
\mathcal{W}^{\mathbf{x}},\mathcal{W}^{\mathbf{d}}\subset\mathcal{W}
be the subalgebras generated by
\{x_{i}\}
and
\{\partial_{i}\}
, respectively.

Theorem 4.17

(
[
GZ95
]
)
.

We have the following:

(1)

The map
e_{i}\mapsto x_{i}
induces an isomorphism of algebras
S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}\cong\mathcal{W}^{\mathbf{x}}
.

(2)

The map
e^{i}\mapsto\partial_{i}
induces an isomorphism of algebras
S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}^{*}\cong\mathcal{W}^{\mathbf{d}}
.

(3)

\mathcal{W}
has a structure of a
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant algebra that resticts to those of
\mathcal{W}^{\mathbf{x}}
and
\mathcal{W}^{\mathbf{d}}
obtained from (1) and (2).
As a
\mathcal{U}
-module,
\mathcal{W}\cong\mathcal{W}^{\mathbf{x}}\otimes\mathcal{W}^{\mathbf{d}}\cong S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}\otimes S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}^{*}
.

Part (3) allows us to define a
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant action of
\mathcal{W}
on
S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}
.
Namely, let
I^{\mathbf{d}}\subset\mathcal{W}
be the left ideal

I^{\mathbf{d}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{W}\partial_{i}

We then have that the
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant
\mathcal{W}
-module
\mathcal{W}/I^{\mathbf{d}}
is isomorphic to
S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}
as a
\mathcal{U}
-module.

Finally, let us go into more detail on the
\mathcal{W}
-action on
S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}
.
The ordered monomials

x_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{k_{n}}

give a basis of
S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}
, and
\mathcal{W}^{\mathbf{x}}
acts by multiplication followed by reordering.
For
\mathcal{W}^{\mathbf{d}}
, we have (cf.
[
KS97
]
):

\partial_{i}\cdot x_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{k_{n}}=(\mathsf{q}x)^{k_{1}}\cdots(\mathsf{q}x_{i-1})^{k_{i-1}}[k_{i}]_{\mathsf{q}^{2}}x_{i}^{k_{i}-1}x_{i+1}^{k_{i+1}}\cdots x_{n}^{k_{n}}

4.4.3.
Difference operators

We will reinterpret the
\mathcal{U}
- and
\mathcal{W}
-module structures on
S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}
in terms of
\mathsf{q}
-difference operators acting on polynomials.
Namely, we translate over these structures over to the polynomial ring
\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[\mathbf{z}_{n}]:=\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}]
via the map

x_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{k_{n}}\mapsto z_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots z_{n}^{k_{n}}

Let
\tau_{z_{i},\mathsf{q}}
be the
\mathsf{q}
-shift operator

\tau_{z_{i},\mathsf{q}}z_{j}=\mathsf{q}^{\delta_{i,j}}z_{j}

and for
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, we use monomial notation:

\displaystyle z^{\lambda}

\displaystyle:=z_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\cdots z_{n}^{\lambda_{n}},

\displaystyle\tau_{\mathsf{q}}^{\lambda}

\displaystyle:=\tau_{z_{1},\mathsf{q}}^{\lambda_{1}}\cdots\tau_{z_{n},\mathsf{q}}^{\lambda_{n}}

Consider the following rings of
\mathsf{q}
-difference operators:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\hbox{\it iff}\,_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{z}_{n})

\displaystyle:=\left\{\sum_{\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}a_{\lambda,\mu}z^{\lambda}\tau_{\mathsf{q}}^{\mu}\,\middle|\,\begin{array}[]{l}a_{\lambda,\mu}\in\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})\\
\hbox{and only finitely many }a_{\lambda,\mu}\not=0\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\hbox{\it iff}\,_{\mathsf{q}}^{+}(\mathbf{z}_{n})

\displaystyle:=\left\{D\in\mathcal{D}\hbox{\it iff}\,_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{z}_{n})\,\middle|\,\begin{array}[]{l}Df\in\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[\mathbf{z}_{n}]\\
\hbox{for all }f\in\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[\mathbf{z}_{n}]\end{array}\right\}

The actions of
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{W}
on
\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[\mathbf{z}_{n}]
come from a homomorphism
\mathfrak{qdiff}:\mathcal{W}\rtimes\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}\hbox{\it iff}\,_{\mathsf{q}}^{+}(\mathbf{z}_{n})
given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{qdiff}(\mathsf{q}^{\epsilon_{i}})

\displaystyle=\tau_{z_{i},\mathsf{q}}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{qdiff}(x_{i})

\displaystyle=z_{i}\tau_{z_{1},\mathsf{q}}\cdots\tau_{z_{i-1},\mathsf{q}}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{qdiff}(E_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{z_{i}}{z_{i+1}}\left(\frac{\tau_{z_{i+1},\mathsf{q}}-\tau_{z_{i+1},\mathsf{q}}^{-1}}{\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1}}\right)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{qdiff}(\partial_{i})

\displaystyle=z_{i}^{-1}\tau_{z_{1},\mathsf{q}}\cdots\tau_{z_{i-1},\mathsf{q}}\left(\frac{\tau_{z_{i},\mathsf{q}}^{2}-1}{\mathsf{q}^{2}-1}\right)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{qdiff}(F_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{z_{i+1}}{z_{i}}\left(\frac{\tau_{z_{i},\mathsf{q}}-\tau_{z_{i},\mathsf{q}}^{-1}}{\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1}}\right)

5.
Harish-Chandra isomorphism

5.1.
Moment maps

Let
H
be a Hopf algebra, let
A
be an
H
-module algebra, and let
\bullet
denote the
H
action on
A
.
A
(quantum) moment map
(in the sense of
[
VV10
]
) is a ring homomorphism
\mu:H\rightarrow A
such that for
h\in H
and
a\in A
:

(5.1)

\mu(h)a=\left(h_{(1)}\bullet a\right)\mu(h_{(2)})

More generally, this notion makes sense if
H^{\prime}\subset H
is a left coideal subalgebra and
\mu
is only defined on
H^{\prime}
.
For what follows, we will be interested in the case
H=\mathcal{U}
,
H^{\prime}=\kappa(\mathcal{O}_{G})
, and
A
is one of the algebras of quantum differential operators from Section
4
above.
When discussing the domain of a moment map, we will abuse notation and conflate
\mathcal{O}_{G}
with its image in
\mathcal{U}
via
\kappa
.

For what will appear below,
A
will always be a locally-finite
\mathcal{U}
-module algebra and
\mu:\mathcal{O}_{G}\rightarrow A
will always be
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant.
In this case, the moment map equation (
5.1
) on
V^{*}\otimes V\subset\mathcal{O}_{G}
looks like (cf.
[
GJS19
]
):

(5.2)

where
m_{A}
is the multiplication in
A
.

5.1.1.
Moment maps for
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
for
\ell>1

For
\ell>1
and
a\in\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}
, consider the matrices of elements:

\displaystyle{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(a)}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{X}^{(a)}\mathbf{D}^{(a)},

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{D}^{(a)}\mathbf{X}^{(a)}

Let
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{(a)}
be the subalgebra generated by the coordinates of
\mathbf{X}^{(a)}
and
\mathbf{D}^{(a)}
; note that the coordinates of
{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(a)}
and
{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}
lie in
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{(a)}
.
Next, recall the notation we introduced in
4.1.3
.
Using this notation,
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{(a)}
is a
\mathcal{U}_{(a)}\otimes\mathcal{U}_{(a+1)}
-module, where

\displaystyle x_{ij}

\displaystyle=e^{i}_{(a)}\boxtimes e_{j}^{(a+1)}\in\mathbb{V}^{*}_{(a)}\boxtimes\mathbb{V}_{(a+1)},

\displaystyle\partial_{ij}

\displaystyle=e_{j}^{(a)}\boxtimes e^{j}_{(a+1)}\in\mathbb{V}_{(a)}\boxtimes\mathbb{V}^{*}_{(a+1)}

Proposition 5.1

(
[
Jor14
]
)
.

{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}
and
{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(a)}
satisfy the following:

(1)

The map
\mathbf{M}\mapsto{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}
defines a
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant ring homomorphism
\mu_{R}^{(a)}:\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}\,\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{(a)}
.

(2)

The map
\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mapsto{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(a)}
defines a
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant ring homomorphism
\mu_{L}^{(a)}:\iota_{G}(\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}\,)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{(a)}
.

(3)

The elements
\mu_{R}^{(a)}(\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{M}))
and
\mu_{L}^{(a)}(\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{M})^{-1})
generate Ore sets of
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
.
Let
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{(a),\star}
denote the localization of
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{(a)}
.
The extension
\mu_{R}^{(a)}:\mathcal{O}_{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{(a),\star}
is a moment map for the
\mathcal{U}_{(a+1)}
-action, and the extension
\mu_{L}^{(a)}:\mathcal{O}_{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{(a),\star}
is a moment map for the
\mathcal{U}_{(a)}
-action.

Let
{}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}:=\big({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(a)}\big)^{-1}
and let
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{\star}
denote the localization of
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
at the determinants from part (3) above.
Translating (
5.2
) shows that part (3) is equivalent to the identities:

(5.3)

\displaystyle{}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{2}{}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}

(5.4)

\displaystyle{}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{D}^{(a)}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{D}^{(a)}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}{}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{R}

(5.5)

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{R}

(5.6)

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{D}^{(a)}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{D}^{(a)}_{2}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}

Using the relations for
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
, we can deduce that
{}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}
satisfies:

(5.7)

\displaystyle{}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a-1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a-1)}\mathbf{R}_{21}{}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}

(5.8)

\displaystyle{}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(b)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(b)}{}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\hbox{ for }b\not=a,a-1

(5.9)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{21}{}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(a-1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(a-1)}{}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}

(5.10)

\displaystyle{}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(b)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(b)}{}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\hbox{ for }b\not=a,a-1

On the other hand,
{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}
satisfies:

(5.11)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{-1}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a+1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a+1)}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}

(5.12)

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(b)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(b)}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\hbox{ for }b\not=a,a+1

(5.13)

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(a+1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(a+1)}\mathbf{R}^{-1}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{R}

(5.14)

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(b)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(b)}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\hbox{ for }b\not=a,a+1

Proposition 5.2

(
[
Jor14
,
GJS19
]
)
.

The map
\mu_{\mathcal{D}_{\ell}}^{(a)}:\mathcal{O}_{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{\star}
given by

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{D}_{\ell}}^{(a)}(\mathbf{M})

\displaystyle={}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a-1)}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{X}^{(a)}\mathbf{D}^{(a)}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{D}^{(a-1)}\mathbf{X}^{(a-1)}\right)

is a moment map for the
\mathcal{U}_{(a)}
-action.
Consequently,

\mu_{\mathcal{D}_{\ell}}(\mathbf{M}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{M}_{\ell})=\mu_{\mathcal{D}_{\ell}}^{(1)}(\mathbf{M}_{1})\cdots\mu_{\mathcal{D}_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}(\mathbf{M}_{\ell})

is a moment map for the
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
-action.

5.1.2.
Moment maps for
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{W}

Recall that in the
\mathcal{U}
-module structure for
\mathcal{D}_{0}
, we view the entries of the generating matrices
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{B}
as elements of
\mathbb{V}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{V}
.

Proposition 5.3

(
[
Jor14
,
VV10
]
)
.

The assignment

\mathbf{M}\mapsto\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{B}^{-1}\mathbf{A}

defines a moment map
\mu_{\mathcal{D}_{0}}:\mathcal{O}_{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{0}
.

Proposition 5.4

(
[
Jor14
]
)
.

The assignment

\mathbf{M}\mapsto\mathsf{q}^{-2}\left(\mathbf{I}+(\mathsf{q}^{2}-1)\mathbf{d}\mathbf{x}\right)

defines a ring homomorphism
\mu_{\mathcal{W}}:\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}\,\rightarrow\mathcal{W}
.
The element
\mu_{\mathcal{W}}(\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{M}))
generates an Ore set in
\mathcal{W}
.
Let
\mathcal{W}^{\star}
be the localization.
The extension
\mu_{\mathcal{W}}:\mathcal{O}_{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{W}^{\star}
is a moment map.

5.1.3.
Moment map for
\mathcal{D}_{1}

As elements of a
\mathcal{U}
-module, the entries of
\mathbf{X}
are elements of
\mathbb{V}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{V}
and the entries of
\mathbf{D}
are elements of
\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(\mathbb{V}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{V})\cong\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{V}^{*}
.
Like in the case
\ell>1
, we will start with approximations to a moment map for the
\mathcal{U}
-action on each of the tensorands.
Let:

\displaystyle{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{X}\mathbf{D},

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{D}\mathbf{X}

Lemma 5.5
.

The matrices
{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}
and
{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}
satisfy:

(5.15)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{-1}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{2}

\displaystyle={}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{2}\mathbf{R}^{-1}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}

(5.16)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{21}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}_{1}\mathbf{R}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}_{2}

\displaystyle={}^{R}\mathbf{Y}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}_{1}\mathbf{R}

(5.17)

\displaystyle{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}

(5.18)

\displaystyle{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}\mathbf{D}_{2}\mathbf{R}^{-1}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}

(5.19)

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}_{1}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}_{1}\mathbf{R}

(5.20)

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}_{1}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{D}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}\mathbf{D}_{2}\mathbf{R}^{-1}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}_{1}\mathbf{R}

Proof.

We will only prove (
5.15
) and (
5.17
).
For (
5.15
), we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{-1}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}+\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}+\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}+\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}

\displaystyle{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{2}\mathbf{R}^{-1}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}+\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}+\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}+\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}

All we need to show is:

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}+\underbrace{\mathbf{R}^{-1}}_{(\ref{HeckeCond})}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}

\displaystyle\overset{?}{=}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}+\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{1}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}}_{(\ref{HeckeCond})}

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}+\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}

\displaystyle\overset{?}{=}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}+\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}

We start from the left:

\displaystyle\quad\mathbf{R}^{-1}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}}_{(\ref{HeckeCond})}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}+\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}\underbrace{\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}}_{(\ref{D1rel3})}\mathbf{D}_{2}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}}_{(\ref{D1rel1})}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{2}}_{(\ref{D1rel2})}+\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}+(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\bigg(\mathbf{R}_{21}\underbrace{\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{\Omega}}_{\mathrm{commute}}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{2}-\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{\Omega}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{D}_{2}\mathbf{R}^{-1}}_{(\ref{D1rel3})}\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}+(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\bigg(\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{21}-\mathbf{R}^{-1}}_{(\ref{HeckeCond})}\bigg)\mathbf{\Omega}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}-(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\underbrace{\mathbf{\Omega}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{D}_{1}}_{\mathrm{commute}}\mathbf{R}+(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})^{2}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}-(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}\mathbf{\Omega}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}}_{(\ref{HeckeCond})}+(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})^{2}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}-(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}\underbrace{\mathbf{\Omega}}_{(\ref{HeckeCond})}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{D}_{2}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}

For (
5.17
), we start from the right:

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathbf{R}}_{(\ref{HeckeCond})}\mathbf{X}_{2}+\underbrace{\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}_{1}}_{(\ref{D1rel1})}\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}+\mathbf{X}_{1}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}}_{(\ref{D1rel3})}+(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\mathbf{\Omega}\mathbf{X}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}+\mathbf{X}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}

\displaystyle={}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}\qed

Corollary 5.6
.

The homomorphism
\Psi^{1}:\mathcal{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{0}
is injective.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.14
,
\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X})
generates an Ore set in
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and thus there is an extension:

\textstyle\Psi_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}:\mathcal{D}_{1}[\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X})^{-1}]\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{0}

The image of
\Psi_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}
lies in
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}[\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{A})^{-1}]
.
By (
5.15
) and (
5.17
), the assignment

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbf{X},

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}^{-1}

\displaystyle\mapsto{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}

induces a map

\textstyle\Pi:\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}[\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{A})^{-1}]\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{1}[\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{\mathbf{X}})^{-1}]

Observe that
\Psi_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}
is a section of
\Pi
and is hence injective.
Since
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is an intgral domain, it includes into its localization.
∎

Equation (
5.15
) implies that the entries of
{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}
generate a copy of
\iota_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}\,)
.
Hence, we can make sense of
\det_{\mathsf{q}}({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}})
.

Corollary 5.7
.

The element
\det_{\mathsf{q}}({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}})
generates an Ore set of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
.
Let
\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\star}
denote the localization at
\det_{\mathsf{q}}({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}})
and let
{}^{L}\mathbf{Y}:={}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{-1}
.
The assigment

\mathbf{M}\mapsto{}^{L}\mathbf{Y}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{X}\mathbf{D}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{D}\mathbf{X}\right)

defines a moment map
\mu_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}:\mathcal{O}_{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\star}
.

Proof.

We have
\Psi^{1}({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}})=\mathbf{B}^{-1}
, which generates an Ore set of
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}[\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{A})^{-1}]
.
Since
\Psi^{1}
is injective, we have that
\det_{\mathsf{q}}({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}})
generates an Ore set in
\mathcal{D}_{1}
.
We will abuse notation and denote the extension
\Psi^{1}:\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\star}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{0}

Next, observe that

\Psi^{1}\left({}^{L}\mathbf{Y}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}\right)=\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{B}^{-1}\mathbf{A}

Again, by the injectivity of
\Psi^{1}
, we can conclude that
\mu_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}:\mathcal{O}_{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{1}^{\star}
is a ring homomorphism and a moment map.
∎

5.2.
Quantum Hamiltonian reduction

Fix a ground ring
R
and recall the setup at the start of
5.1
: we have a Hopf algebra
H
, a left coideal subalgebra
H^{\prime}\subset H
, an
H
-module algebra
A
, and a moment map
\mu:H^{\prime}\rightarrow A
.
If
H^{\prime}
is closed under the adjoint action of
H
, then we can define a character of
H^{\prime}
to be an
R
-algebra homomorphism
\chi:H^{\prime}\rightarrow R
that is invariant with respect to this adjoint action.
For such a character
\chi
, let

I_{\chi}:=A\big(\mu(\ker\chi)\big)

We can perform
quantum Hamiltonian reduction
:

A\sslash_{\chi}H^{\prime}:=\left[A\big/I_{\chi}\right]^{H}

Proposition 5.8

(
[
GJS19
,
VV10
]
)
.

The quantum Hamiltonian reduction
A\sslash_{\chi}H^{\prime}
is an
R
-algebra.

In our setup of
H=\mathcal{U}
and
H^{\prime}=\kappa(\mathcal{O}_{G})\cong\mathcal{O}_{G}
, we do indeed have that
\mathcal{O}_{G}
is closed under the adjoint action of
\mathcal{U}
.
Recall from
3.1.3
that for
\mathsf{C}\in\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})^{\times}
, we have a character
\chi_{\mathsf{C}}
.
From (
3.6
), it is easy to see that the restriction of
\chi_{\mathsf{C}}
to
\mathcal{O}_{G}
is given by

\chi_{\mathsf{C}}(\mathbf{M})=\mathsf{C}^{-2}\mathbf{I}

Later on, we will base change to
\mathbb{L}_{\mathsf{q}}:=\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[\mathsf{t}^{\pm 1}]
, and the same holds for
\mathsf{C}\in\mathbb{L}_{\mathsf{q}}^{\times}
.

5.2.1.
Quantized multiplicative quiver varieties

For two locally finite
\mathcal{U}
-module algebras
A
and
B
, we define their
braided tensor product

A\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,B
to be the
\mathcal{U}
-module
A\otimes B
with the product

(a_{1}\otimes b_{1})(a_{2}\otimes b_{2})=a_{1}(r_{s}\cdot a_{2})\otimes({}_{s}r\cdot b_{1})b_{2}

The extra braiding makes
A\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,B
into a
\mathcal{U}
-module algebra.
The following was proved in
[
GJS19
]
:

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
A
and
B
be two locally finite
\mathcal{U}
-module algebras with moment maps
\mu_{A}:\mathcal{O}_{G}\rightarrow A
and
\mu_{B}:\mathcal{O}_{G}\rightarrow B
.
The matrix product

\displaystyle\mu_{A\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,B}

\displaystyle:=(\mu_{A}\otimes\mu_{B})\circ\Delta_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}

\displaystyle\mu_{A\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,B}(m_{ij})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\mu_{A}(m_{ik})\mu_{B}(m_{kj})

defines a moment map
\mu_{A\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,B}:\mathcal{O}_{G}\rightarrow A\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,B
.

We now set

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\ell}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{D}_{\ell}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\mathcal{W},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\ell}^{\star}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{\star}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\mathcal{W}^{\star}\hbox{ for }\ell\geq 1

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{D}_{0}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\mathcal{W},

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\star}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{D}_{0}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\mathcal{W}^{\star}

We will view
\mathcal{W}
as a
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
-module where only
\mathcal{U}_{(1)}
acts nontrivially.
For
\ell\geq 1
, we have by Proposition
5.9
that the maps

(5.21)

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{M}_{\ell}}^{(a)}(\mathbf{M})

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{X}^{(a)}\mathbf{D}^{(a)}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{D}^{(a-1)}\mathbf{X}^{(a-1)}\right)&a\not=1\\
\mathsf{q}^{-2}\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{X}^{(1)}\mathbf{D}^{(1)}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{D}^{(\ell)}\mathbf{X}^{(\ell)}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}+(\mathsf{q}^{2}-1)\mathbf{d}\mathbf{x}\right)&a=1\end{cases}

are moment maps for the
\mathcal{U}_{(a)}
-action on
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}^{\star}
.
These can be combined into a moment map for the
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
-action:

\mu_{\mathcal{M}_{\ell}}(\mathbf{M}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{M}_{\ell}):=\mu_{\mathcal{M}_{\ell}}^{(1)}(\mathbf{M}_{1})\cdots\mu_{\mathcal{M}_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}(\mathbf{M}_{\ell})

For
\mathsf{C}=(\mathsf{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{C}_{\ell})\in(\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})^{\times})^{\ell}
, we have the following character of
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
:

\chi_{\mathsf{C}}:=\chi_{\mathsf{C}_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\chi_{\mathsf{C}_{\ell}}

Finally, for
\ell=0
, the moment map for
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\star}
is:

\mu_{\mathcal{M}_{0}}(\mathbf{M}):=\mathsf{q}^{-2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{B}^{-1}\mathbf{A}\left(\mathbf{I}+(\mathsf{q}^{2}-1)\mathbf{d}\mathbf{x}\right)

Now, we will incorporate the parameters
\mathsf{Z}=(\mathsf{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{Z}_{\ell})\in(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}}
from the cyclotomic DAHA.
Namely, after picking a choice of square root for each
\mathsf{Z}_{a}
, we consider character parameters of the form

(5.22)

\mathsf{C}_{a}=\left(\frac{\mathsf{Z}_{a-1}}{\mathsf{Z}_{a}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathsf{K}_{a}

for some
\mathsf{K}_{a}\in\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})^{\times}
or later
\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[\mathsf{t}^{\pm 1}]^{\times}
.
Let
\mathsf{K}=(\mathsf{K}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{K}_{\ell})
and denote by
\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}
the parameter vector
\mathsf{C}
given by (
5.22
).

Definition 5.10
.

For
\ell\geq 1
,
\mathsf{Z}\in(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{\ell}
, and
\mathsf{K}\in(\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})^{\times})^{\ell}
, let us abbreviate
I_{\mathsf{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}}:=I_{\chi_{\mathsf{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}}}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\ell}^{\star}
.
The
quantized multiplicative quiver variety
is the quantum Hamiltonian reduction

\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{K}):=\left[\mathcal{M}_{\ell}^{\star}\big/I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}\right]^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}

Similarly, for
\ell=0
and
\mathsf{K}\in\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})^{\times}
, we abbreviate
I_{\mathsf{K}}:=I_{\chi_{\mathsf{K}}}\subset\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\star}
and define

\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mathsf{K}):=\left[\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\star}\big/I_{\mathsf{K}}\right]^{\mathcal{U}}

Proposition 5.11
.

\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{K})
is generated by the image of the natural map
\pi:(\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{\star})^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{K})
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma 5.7 in
[
Wen23
]
.
∎

5.2.2.
Classical degeneration and filtrations

Recall that for
\ell\geq 1
, we have defined notations of standard monomials for
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
, and we also have such a notion for
\mathcal{W}
.
Let us define a standard monomial for
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
to be a product

M_{\mathcal{D}}M_{\mathcal{W}}

where
M_{\mathcal{D}}
is a standard monomial for
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
and
M_{\mathcal{W}}
is a standard monomial for
\mathcal{W}
.
We let
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}^{\mathbb{Z}}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
to be the
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{q}^{\pm 1}]
-subalgebra generated by

\left\{x_{ij},\partial_{ij}\right\}_{i,j=1}^{n}\cup\left\{x_{i},\widetilde{\partial_{i}}\right\}_{i=1}^{n}

Proposition 5.12

(
[
Jor14
]
)
.

The standard monomials form a basis of
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Thus,
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
is a flat deformation of the coordinate ring of
T^{*}(\mathrm{Mat}_{n\times n}^{\oplus\ell}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n})
.

For any subspace
V\subset\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
, we set

\displaystyle V_{\mathbb{Z}}

\displaystyle:=V\cap\mathcal{M}_{\ell}^{\mathbb{Z}},

\displaystyle V\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}

\displaystyle:=V_{\mathbb{Z}}\bigg/(\mathsf{q}-1)

Because monomials span
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
, we evidently have

V=\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})\otimes V_{\mathbb{Z}}

Moreover, for finite-dimensional
V
, because
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is a free
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{q}^{\pm 1}]
-module and
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{q}^{\pm 1}]
is a PID, we have

\dim_{\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})}V=\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{q}^{\pm 1}]}V_{\mathbb{Z}}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}V\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}

For a subquotient
W/V
of
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
with
W,V\subset\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
, we define

W/V\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}:=W\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}\bigg/V\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}

Note that for finite dimensional
W
and
V
,

(5.23)

\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})}W/V=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}W/V\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}

Next, let us consider invariants.
We will use
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}^{(a)}
to denote the
a
th summand of
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}^{\oplus\ell}
.
For

(\mathbf{Z}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{Z}^{(\ell)},\mathbf{z},\mathbf{W}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{W}^{(\ell)},\mathbf{w})\in\left(\mathrm{Mat}_{n\times n}^{\oplus\ell}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n}\right)^{\oplus 2}\cong T^{*}\left(\mathrm{Mat}_{n\times n}^{\oplus\ell}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n}\right)

and
g\in\mathfrak{gl}_{n}^{(a)}
, we set

(5.24)

g\cdot(\mathbf{Z}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{Z}^{(\ell)},\mathbf{z})=\begin{cases}(\mathbf{Z}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{Z}^{(a-2)},-\mathbf{Z}^{(a-1)}g,g\mathbf{Z}^{(a)},\mathbf{Z}^{(a+1)},\ldots,\mathbf{Z}^{(\ell)},\mathbf{z})&a\not=1\\
(g\mathbf{Z}^{(1)},\mathbf{Z}^{(2)}\ldots,\mathbf{Z}^{(\ell-1)},-\mathbf{Z}^{(\ell)}g,-g^{T}\mathbf{z})&a=1\end{cases}

and take the induced action on
T^{*}(\mathrm{Mat}_{n\times n}^{\oplus\ell}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n})
.
It is evident that
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is closed under the
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
-action.
If we identify

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{(a)}\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}

\displaystyle=\hbox{ coordinate to }\mathbf{Z}^{(a)}

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}^{(a)}\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}

\displaystyle=\hbox{ coordinate to }\mathbf{W}^{(a)}

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}

\displaystyle=\hbox{ coordinate to }\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}

\displaystyle=\hbox{ coordinate to }\mathbf{w}

then the actions of
E_{i},F_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{(a)}
become those of the matrix units
E_{i,i+1},E_{i+1,i}\in\mathfrak{gl}_{n}^{(a)}
on the coordinate ring upon taking
\mathsf{q}=1
.
Thus, for a
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
-submodule
V\subset\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
,
V\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}
is a
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}^{\oplus\ell}
-module and we have

V^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}\subset\left(V\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}\right)^{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}^{\oplus\ell}}

We will prove the opposite containment in
5.2.3
below.
For
\ell=0
, we can define similar notions using the integral form
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Finally, we can define a filtration
F_{k}^{\mathcal{D}}
of
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
wherein

\displaystyle\deg(x_{ij})=\deg(\partial_{ij})

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\deg(x_{i})=\deg(\partial_{i})

\displaystyle=0

We endow all subspaces and quotients of
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
with the induced filtration.
Let

I^{+}_{\mathsf{C}}:=I_{\mathsf{C}}\cap\mathcal{M}_{\ell}

Observe that in
\mathrm{gr}_{F^{\mathcal{D}}}\left(\mathcal{M}_{\ell}\big/I^{+}_{\mathsf{C}}\right)
, we have the relations

(5.25)

\mathsf{Z}_{a}\mathbf{X}^{(a)}\mathbf{D}^{(a)}=\begin{cases}\mathsf{K}_{a}^{2}\mathsf{Z}_{a-1}\mathbf{D}^{(a-1)}\mathbf{X}^{(a-1)}&a\not=1\\
\mathsf{K}_{1}^{2}\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}\left(\mathbf{D}^{(\ell)}\mathbf{X}^{(\ell)}+\mathbf{D}^{(\ell)}\mathbf{X}^{(\ell)}\mathbf{\widetilde{d}}\mathbf{x}\right)&a=1\end{cases}

where
\mathbf{\widetilde{d}}=(\mathsf{q}-\mathsf{q}^{-1})\mathbf{d}
.
We can also define a filtration
F_{l}^{\mathcal{W}}
of
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
wherein

\displaystyle\deg(x_{ij})=\deg(\partial_{ij})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\deg(x_{i})=\deg(\partial_{i})=1

Let us set
F_{k,l}:=F_{k}^{\mathcal{D}}\cap F_{l}^{\mathcal{W}}
.
Then each
F_{k,l}
is a finite-dimensional
\mathcal{U}
-submodule of
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
.

5.2.3.
Quantum trace elements

Recall that if we view
\mathbb{V}_{(a)}
as a copy of
\mathbb{V}
placed at the
a
th round vertex in the quiver (
4.1
), then we view
\mathbf{X}^{(a)}
as the coordinates of a matrix
\mathbf{Z}^{(a)}:\mathbb{V}_{(a+1)}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{(a)}
.
Likewise, we view
\mathbf{D}^{(a)}
as the coordinates of a matrix
\mathbf{W}^{(a)}:\mathbb{V}_{(a)}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{(a+1)}
,
\mathbf{x}
as the coordinates of a covector
\mathbf{z}:\mathbb{V}_{(1)}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})
, and
\mathbf{d}
as the coordinates of a vector
\mathbf{w}:\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{(1)}
.
We call a matrix product
\mathbf{\Pi}
of
\{\mathbf{X}^{(a)},\mathbf{D}^{(a)},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{d}\}_{a\in\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}}
a
cyclic word
it describes a composition of maps that starts and ends at the same round vertex in (
4.1
).
For example, the following elements are cyclic words:

(5.26)

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{\circ}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{X}^{(1)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(\ell)},

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}^{\circ}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{D}^{(\ell)}\cdots\mathbf{D}^{(1)}

For a cyclic word
\mathbf{\Pi}
, we define its
quantum trace
to be

\mathrm{tr}_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{\Pi}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{q}^{-n+2i}\mathbf{\Pi}_{ii}

Proposition 5.13
.

Quantum traces over cyclic words are
\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}
-invariant elements in
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}^{\mathbb{Z}}
that generate
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}
.

Proof.

The proof of invariance is similar to that of Proposition 5.4 in
[
Wen23
]
.
Clearly,
\mathrm{tr}_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{\Pi})\in\mathcal{M}_{\ell}^{\mathbb{Z}}
and becomes the usual trace at
\mathsf{q}=1
.
To prove generation, it suffices to consider each finite-dimensional piece
F_{k,l}\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
.
By a theorem of Le Bruyn–Procesi
[
LBP90
]
,
F_{k,l}\mathcal{M}_{\ell}\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}
is generated by classical traces of cyclic words.
We then get the desired result for
F_{k,l}\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
by applying Nakayama’s lemma.
∎

Corollary 5.14
.

For any
\mathcal{U}
-submodule
V\subset\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
,

V^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}=\left(V\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}\right)^{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}^{\oplus\ell}}

Proof.

Proved similarly to Lemma 5.5 of
[
Wen23
]
.
∎

5.2.4.
Nakajima quiver varieties

For special values of the parameter
\mathsf{C}
, we can relate the
\mathsf{q}=1
degeneration to the Nakajima quiver variety for (
4.1
)
[
Nak98
]
.
We begin by recalling its definition.
Let
(\mathbf{G}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{G}^{(\ell)},\mathbf{j},\mathbf{H}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{H}^{(\ell)},\mathbf{i})
be the coordinates of another copy of
\mathbb{M}_{\ell}:=T^{*}\left(\mathrm{Mat}^{\oplus\ell}_{n\times n}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{n}\right)
, equipped with the same
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}^{\oplus\ell}
-action as (
5.24
).
Let
I_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{M}_{\ell}]
be the ideal of relations

(5.27)

\mathbf{G}^{(a)}\mathbf{H}^{(a)}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{H}^{(a-1)}\mathbf{G}^{(a-1)}&a\not=1\\
\mathbf{H}^{(\ell)}\mathbf{G}^{(\ell)}+\mathbf{i}\mathbf{j}^{T}&a=1\end{cases}

The
Nakajima quiver variety
is then

\mathfrak{M}_{\ell}:=\mathrm{Spec}\left(\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{M}_{\ell}]\big/I_{\ell}\right)^{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}^{\oplus\ell}}

We can also define a version without the square vertex: let
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}_{\ell}:=T^{*}\left(\mathrm{Mat}_{n\times n}^{\oplus\ell}\right)
and let
I^{\prime}_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{M}_{\ell}^{\prime}]
be the ideal of relations

\mathbf{G}^{(a)}\mathbf{H}^{(a)}=\mathbf{H}^{(a-1)}\mathbf{G}^{(a-1)}\hbox{ for all }a

Define

\mathfrak{M}_{\ell}^{\prime}:=\mathrm{Spec}\left(\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{M}^{\prime}]\big/I_{\ell}^{\prime}\right)^{\mathfrak{gl}_{n}^{\oplus\ell}}

There is an obvious map
p:\mathfrak{M}_{\ell}\rightarrow\mathfrak{M}_{\ell}^{\prime}
given by projecting away the
(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{i})
coordinates.
Finally, recall the ring
\mathcal{R}_{\ell}
from (
2.6
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\ell}^{\Sigma_{n}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n},w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}]^{\Sigma_{n}\wr\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z}}

Proposition 5.15
.

We have

\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{\ell}]\cong\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{\ell}^{\prime}]\cong\mathcal{R}_{\ell}^{\Sigma_{n}}

The first isomorphism is given by
p^{*}
and the second is given by restricting to the locus

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}^{(a)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{S},

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}^{(a)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{T}\hbox{ {\rm for all} }a

where
\mathbf{T}
and
\mathbf{S}
are diagonal matrices with coordinates
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
and
(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n})
, respectively.

Proof.

The isomorphism
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{\ell}]\cong\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}_{\ell}]
is Lemma 2.3 of
[
CB02
]
.
The second isomorphism
\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}_{\ell}]\cong\mathcal{R}_{\ell}^{\Sigma_{n}}
is the main result of
[
Gan06
]
.
∎

\mathcal{R}_{\ell}^{\Sigma_{n}}
inherits a grading where

\deg(z_{i})=\deg(w_{i})=1

For any graded vector space
V
, we denote by
\dim^{k}V
the dimension of its
k
th graded piece.
Finally, recall the natural map
\pi:(\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{\star})^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{K})
from Proposition
5.11
.

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
\mathsf{K}
be such that
\mathsf{K}_{a}\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}=1
for all
a
.

(1)

We have an isomorphism of rings:

\mathrm{gr}_{F^{\mathcal{D}}}\left(\left(\mathcal{M}_{\ell}\big/I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}^{+}\right)^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}\bigg|_{\mathsf{q}=1}\right)\cong\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{\ell}]

(2)

For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\dim^{k}_{\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})}\mathrm{gr}_{F^{\mathcal{D}}}\left(\left(\mathcal{M}_{\ell}\big/I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}^{+}\right)^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}\right)\leq\dim^{k}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{R}_{\ell}^{\Sigma_{n}}

(3)

\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{K})
is generated by the quantum traces

(5.28)

\mathrm{tr}_{\mathsf{q}}\left((\mathbf{X}^{\circ})^{k_{1}}({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)})^{k_{2}}(\mathbf{D}^{\circ})^{k_{3}}\right)

where
\mathbf{X}^{\circ}
and
\mathbf{D}^{\circ}
are defined in (
5.26
).

Proof.

Comparing (
5.25
) and (
5.27
), we have that

(5.29)

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}^{(a)}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbf{X}^{(a)},

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}^{(a)}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathsf{Z}_{a}\mathbf{D}^{(a)},

\displaystyle\mathbf{j}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbf{x},

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbf{D}^{(\ell)}\mathbf{X}^{(\ell)}\tilde{\mathbf{d}}

defines a map

f:\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{\ell}]\rightarrow\mathrm{gr}_{F^{\mathcal{D}}}\left(\mathcal{M}_{\ell}^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}\big/(I_{\mathsf{C}}^{+})^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}\right)

(here, we are implicitly using Corollary
5.14
).
Its image contains
\mathrm{gr}_{F^{\mathcal{D}}}\left(\pi\left(\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}\right)\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}\right)
, and thus by Proposition
5.11
, it is surjective.
Part (2) follows from
Proposition
5.15
.

For injectivity in part (1), we note that Proposition
5.15
implies that
\mathfrak{M}_{\ell}
is reduced and irreducible.
Thus, its coordinate ring is an integral domain and includes into its localization at
\{\det(\mathbf{G}^{(\ell)}),\det(\mathbf{H}^{(\ell)})\}
.
We can then define a map

g:\mathrm{gr}_{F^{\mathcal{D}}}\left(\mathcal{M}_{\ell}^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}\big/(I_{\mathsf{C}}^{+})^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}\right)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{\ell}][\det(\mathbf{G}^{(\ell)})^{-1},\det(\mathbf{H}^{(\ell)})^{-1}]

by

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{(a)}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbf{G}^{(a)},

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}^{(a)}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathsf{Z}_{a}^{-1}\mathbf{H}^{(a)},

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbf{j},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{d}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathsf{Z}_{a}\left(\mathbf{H}^{(\ell)}\mathbf{G}^{(\ell)}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{i}

Clearly,
f
is a section of
g
and thus
f
is injective.

For part (3), recall the generator
p_{a,k,b}
(
2.9
) of
\mathcal{R}_{\ell}^{\Sigma_{n}}
.
It is easy to see that composition of the isomorphisms from Proposition
5.15
together with (
5.29
) sends
p_{k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}}
to the associated graded of the quantum trace (
5.28
) at
\mathsf{q}=1
.
By applying Nakayama’s lemma to each
F_{k}^{\mathcal{D}}\pi\left(\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}\right)
, which is finite-dimensional, we conclude that the given quantum traces generate
\pi\left(\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}\right)
.
Finally, we apply Proposition
5.11
to carry the generation statement over to
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{K})
.
∎

Now consider
\ell=0
.
Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,\mathcal{W}

\displaystyle I_{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathrm{IV}}

\displaystyle:=I_{\mathsf{K}}\cap\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}

The following is proved similarly to Lemma
5.16
(1):

Proposition 5.17
.

For
\mathsf{K}
with
\mathsf{K}\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}=1
, the map

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbf{A},

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbf{B}^{-1},

\displaystyle\mathbf{j}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbf{x},

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbf{B}^{-1}\mathbf{A}\tilde{\mathbf{d}}

gives an isomorphism

\left(\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}\big/I_{\mathsf{K}}^{\mathrm{IV}}\right)^{\mathcal{U}}\bigg|_{\mathsf{q}=1}\cong\mathbb{C}[\mathfrak{M}_{1}]

If we endow
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}
with the grading where

\displaystyle\deg(a_{ij})

\displaystyle=\deg(b_{ij})=1,

\displaystyle\deg(x_{i})

\displaystyle=\deg(\partial_{i})=0

then composing this isomorphism with that of Proposition
5.15
gives a graded isomorphism with
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\Sigma_{n}}
.

5.3.
From
\ell
to 0

Recall that
I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}^{+}:=I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}\cap\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
.
We begin by proving some identities in
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}/I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}^{+}
for
\ell>1
.
The ideas presented already appeared in the proof of Lemma 5.2 of
[
BEF20
]
.
In the quantum setting, we have to reckon with the fact that
I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}
is only a
left
ideal.
These identities will allow us to map
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{K})
into a quotient of
\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mathsf{K}_{1})
for special values of
\mathsf{K}
.
For
\ell=1
, we already have the homomorphism
\Psi^{1}:\mathcal{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{0}
.

5.3.1.
Small corn-on-the-cob

We start with the following:

Lemma 5.18
.

For
\ell>1
and
2\leq a\leq\ell
, we have the following identity in
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}/I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}^{+}
:

(5.30)

{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(a-1)}\mathbf{X}^{(a)}=\mathsf{q}^{-2n(a-1)}\left(\mathsf{Z}_{a}\big/\mathsf{Z}_{1}\right)\mathsf{K}_{2}^{-2}\cdots\mathsf{K}_{a}^{-2}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(a)}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}

Proof.

First, we have

\displaystyle{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(a)}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{X}^{(1)}\mathbf{D}^{(1)}\right)\mathbf{X}^{(1)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(a)}

(5.31)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}^{(1)}\underbrace{{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}^{(2)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(a)}}

Let us look closer into the underbraced part of (
5.31
) without the matrix product between
{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}
and
\mathbf{X}^{(2)}
:

\displaystyle\underbrace{{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}_{1}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}^{(2)}_{2}}_{(\ref{YR3})}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(2)}\underbrace{{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}_{1}\mathbf{X}^{(3)}_{2}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{2}}_{(\ref{YR4})}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(2)}\cdots\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}\underbrace{{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}_{1}}_{(\ref{MuEll})}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathsf{Z}_{2}\big/\mathsf{Z}_{1}\right)\mathsf{K}_{2}^{-2}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(2)}\underbrace{\cdots\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(2)}_{1}}_{(\ref{YL4})}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathsf{Z}_{2}\big/\mathsf{Z}_{1}\right)\mathsf{K}_{2}^{-2}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(2)}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(2)}_{1}}_{(\ref{YL1})}\mathbf{X}^{(3)}_{2}\cdots\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathsf{Z}_{2}\big/\mathsf{Z}_{1}\right)\mathsf{K}_{2}^{-2}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(2)}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(2)}\cdots\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}

Incorporating the matrix product, we have

(5.32)

\left({}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}_{1}\right)_{*j}\mathbf{R}\left(\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(2)}\right)_{j*}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(3)}\cdots\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}=\left(\mathsf{Z}_{2}\big/\mathsf{Z}_{1}\right)\mathsf{K}_{2}^{-2}\left({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{1}^{(2)}\right)_{*j}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\left(\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(2)}\right)_{j*}\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(3)}\cdots\mathbf{X}_{2}^{(a)}

Here, as in the proof of Lemma
4.10
, the subscripts
*j
and
j*
are the emphasize the matching indices for the matrix product.

Equation (
5.32
) can be depicted graphically as follows.
Let us view
{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}
,
\mathbf{X}^{(b)}
, and
{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(2)}
as maps
\mathbb{V}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{V}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
, and denote by
m_{\mathcal{D}_{\ell}}
the product in
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
.
We then have:

(5.33)

Using (
3.8
) to compute the effect of the ribbon twists, (
5.31
) becomes:

\mathbf{X}^{(1)}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}^{(2)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(a)}=\mathsf{q}^{-2n}\left(\mathsf{Z}_{2}\big/\mathsf{Z}_{1}\right)\mathsf{K}_{2}^{-2}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(2)}\mathbf{X}^{(2)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(a)}

We then continue as before but now starting with superscript index 2.
∎

5.3.2.
Small corn-on-the-cob and determinants

We emphasize that the identity (
5.30
) is a consequence of the
left
ideal
I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}^{+}
.
Recall by Lemma
4.10
that
\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})
generates an Ore set in
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
.
It also generates an Ore set in
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
because its commutation with
\mathcal{W}
is via the
R
-matrix.
Let
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{\circ}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\circ}_{\ell}/I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}^{+}
denote the respective localizations at
\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})
.
By Corollary
4.11
, we can make sense of

\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a)}:=\left(\mathbf{X}^{(a)}\right)^{-1}

in
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{\circ}
, and thus by
left
multiplication, the identity (
5.30
) yields

(5.34)

\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a)}\cdots\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(1)}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(a)}=\mathsf{q}^{-2n(a-1)}\left(\mathsf{Z}_{a}\big/\mathsf{Z}_{1}\right)\mathsf{K}_{2}^{-2}\cdots\mathsf{K}_{a}^{-2}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}

Again, because
I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}^{+}
is a left ideal, (
5.34
) does not
a priori
allow us to identify corresponding products of entries of the two matrices.
Let
{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1,a)}
denote the matrix on the left hand side of (
5.34
).

Lemma 5.19
.

In
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{\circ}
, we have the identities:

(5.35)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{21}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1,a)}_{1}\mathbf{R}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1,a)}_{2}

\displaystyle={}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1,a)}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1,a)}_{1}\mathbf{R}

(5.36)

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{21}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{R}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1,a)}_{1}

\displaystyle={}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1,a)}_{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{R}

Proof.

These are straightforward applications of (
4.2
)-(
4.4
), (
5.7
)-(
5.8
), and (
5.11
)-(
5.12
).
For (
5.35
), we have

\displaystyle\quad\mathbf{R}_{21}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1,a)}_{1}\mathbf{R}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1,a)}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{21}\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a)}_{1}\cdots\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(1)}_{1}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{1}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{1}\cdots\underbrace{\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{R}\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a)}_{2}}_{(\ref{Xaa})}\cdots\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(1)}_{2}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{2}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{2}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{21}\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a)}_{1}\cdots\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(1)}_{1}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{1}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{1}\cdots\underbrace{\mathbf{X}^{(a-1)}_{1}\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a)}_{2}}_{(\ref{XXad})}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{1}\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a-1)}_{2}}_{(\ref{XXad})}\cdots\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(1)}_{2}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{2}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{2}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{21}\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a)}_{1}\underbrace{\cdots\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(1)}_{1}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{1}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(a-2)}_{1}\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a)}_{2}}_{(\ref{XXcom})+(\ref{YL4})}\mathbf{X}^{(a-1)}_{1}\mathbf{R}\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a-1)}_{2}\underbrace{\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{1}\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a-2)}_{2}\cdots\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(1)}_{2}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{2}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{2}\cdots}_{(\ref{XXcom})+(\ref{YL4})}\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{21}\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a)}_{1}\underbrace{\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a-1)}_{1}\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a)}_{2}}_{(\ref{XXad})}\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a-2)}\cdots\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(1)}_{1}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{1}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(a-1)}_{1}

\displaystyle\quad\times\mathbf{R}\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a-1)}_{2}\cdots\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(1)}_{2}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{2}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{2}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(a-2)}_{2}\underbrace{\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{X}^{(a-1)}_{2}}_{(\ref{XXad})}\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{2}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{21}\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a)}_{1}\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a)}_{2}\mathbf{R}^{-1}}_{(\ref{Xaa})}\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a-1)}_{1}\cdots\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(1)}_{1}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{1}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(a-1)}_{1}

\displaystyle\quad\times\mathbf{R}\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a-1)}_{2}\cdots\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(1)}_{2}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{2}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{2}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(a-1)}_{2}\underbrace{\mathbf{R}_{21}\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{2}}_{(\ref{Xaa})}

\displaystyle=\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a)}_{2}\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a)}_{1}\cdots\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(1)}_{1}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{1}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(a-1)}_{1}\mathbf{R}\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(a-1)}_{2}\cdots\underline{\mathbf{X}}^{(1)}_{2}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{2}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}_{2}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{2}\mathbf{X}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{R}

The commutation of the remaining matrices works
mutatis mutandis
.
Equation (
5.36
) is much easier to prove, and we leave it as an exercise.
∎

Corollary 5.20
.

In
\mathcal{M}^{\circ}_{\ell}/I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}^{+}
, for any
k\geq 0
, we have

{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{1}^{(1,a)}\cdots{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{k}^{(1,a)}=\mathsf{q}^{-2kn(a-1)}\left(\mathsf{Z}_{a}\big/\mathsf{Z}_{1}\right)^{k}\left(\mathsf{K}_{2}^{-2}\cdots\mathsf{K}_{a}^{-2}\right)^{k}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\cdots{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{k}

Proof.

This is a consequence of (
5.35
) and (
5.36
) being of the same form.
Graphically depicting (
5.36
), we can move
{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}
to the right, apply relation (
5.34
)
{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}
, and then by (
5.35
