) apply the reverse graphical move to place
{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}
in the original location.
For
k=2
, this looks like:

Here, we have omitted the extra scalars from (
5.31
).
∎

Corollary 5.21
.

For
\ell>1
and any
a
,
\det_{\mathsf{q}}({}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)})^{-1}
and
\det_{\mathsf{q}}({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(a)})^{-1}
lie in the localization

\textstyle\mathcal{M}_{\ell}^{\circ}\big/I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}^{+}\left[\det_{\mathsf{q}}({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)})^{-1}\right]=\mathcal{M}_{\ell}/I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}^{+}\left[\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})^{-1},\det_{\mathsf{q}}({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)})^{-1}\right]

Proof.

Because

\Delta_{\mathcal{O}_{G}}(\mathrm{qcoev}_{\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}}}(1))=\mathrm{qcoev}_{\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}}}(1)\otimes\mathrm{qcoev}_{\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}}}(1)

it follows that in
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}\big/I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}^{+}
, we have

\textstyle\det_{\mathsf{q}}({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(a)})=\det_{\mathsf{q}}({}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a-1)})

Thus, it suffices to only consider
\det_{\mathsf{q}}({}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)})^{-1}
.
By (
5.35
), the entries of
{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1,a)}
generate a copy of
\mathcal{R}\hbox{\it ef}\,
, and thus it makes sense of
\det_{\mathsf{q}}({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1,a)})
.
Moreover, by Corollary
5.20
, we have

\textstyle\det_{\mathsf{q}}({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1,a)})=C\det_{\mathsf{q}}({}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)})

in
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}^{\circ}/I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}^{+}
for some nonzero scalar
C\in\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})
.
The result follows from the observation that we have
\det_{\mathsf{q}}({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1,a)})^{-1}
upon localizing at
\det_{\mathsf{q}}({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)})
.
∎

5.3.3.
Big corn-on-the-cob

Next, we consider a quantum analogue of Lemma 5.2 of
[
BEF20
]
.

Lemma 5.22
.

For
\ell>1
, the following equality holds in
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}\big/I_{\mathsf{C}}^{+}
:

\mathbf{X}^{\circ}\mathbf{D}^{\circ}=\left({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}-\mathbf{I}\right)\left(\mathsf{q}^{2n}\left(\mathsf{Z}_{1}\big/\mathsf{Z}_{2}\right)\mathsf{K}_{2}^{2}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}-\mathbf{I}\right)\cdots\left(\mathsf{q}^{2n(\ell-1)}\left(\mathsf{Z}_{1}\big/\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}\right)\mathsf{K}_{2}^{2}\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\ell}^{2}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}-\mathbf{I}\right)

Proof.

First, we have

\mathbf{X}^{\circ}\mathbf{D}^{\circ}=\mathbf{X}^{(1)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(\ell-1)}\left({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(\ell)}-\mathbf{I}\right)\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-1)}\cdots\mathbf{D}^{(1)}

Let us consider the
{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(\ell)}
summand on its own.
Moreover, we will ignore the
\mathbf{X}
-matrices and the matrix product between
{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(\ell)}
and
\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-1)}
.
We make moves similar to the proof of Lemma
5.18
:

\displaystyle\underbrace{{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(\ell)}_{1}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-1)}_{2}}_{(\ref{YL5})}\cdots\mathbf{D}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-1)}_{2}\underbrace{{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(\ell)}_{1}\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-2)}\cdots\mathbf{D}^{(1)}}_{(\ref{YL6})}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-1)}_{2}\cdots\mathbf{D}^{(1)}\underbrace{{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(\ell)}_{1}}_{(\ref{MuEll})}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathsf{Z}_{\ell-1}\big/\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}\right)\mathsf{K}_{\ell}^{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-1)}_{2}\underbrace{\cdots\mathbf{D}^{(1)}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(\ell-1)}_{1}}_{(\ref{YR6})}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathsf{Z}_{\ell-1}\big/\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}\right)\mathsf{K}_{\ell}^{2}\mathbf{R}_{21}^{-1}\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-1)}_{2}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(\ell-1)}_{1}\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-1)}\cdots\mathbf{D}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathsf{Z}_{\ell-1}\big/\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}\right)\mathsf{K}_{\ell}^{2}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(\ell-1)}_{1}\mathbf{R}\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-1)}_{2}\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-1)}\cdots\mathbf{D}^{(1)}

As in the proof of Lemma
5.18
, when we incorporate the matrix product between
{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(\ell)}
and
\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-1)}
, we obtain an identity that is diagrammatically depicted like (
5.33
) except that the crossings on the right-hand-side are reversed.
Altogether, we get:

\displaystyle\quad\mathbf{X}^{(1)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(\ell-1)}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(\ell)}\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-1)}\cdots\mathbf{D}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{q}^{2n}\left(\mathsf{Z}_{\ell-1}\big/\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}\right)\mathsf{K}^{2}_{\ell}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(\ell-1)}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(\ell-1)}\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-1)}\cdots\mathbf{D}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{q}^{2n}\left(\mathsf{Z}_{\ell-1}\big/\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}\right)\mathsf{K}^{2}_{\ell}\mathbf{X}^{(1)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(\ell-1)}\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-1)}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(\ell-1)}\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-2)}\cdots\mathbf{D}^{(1)}

Proceeding in this manner, we obtain

\displaystyle\quad\mathbf{X}^{(1)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(\ell)}\mathbf{D}^{(\ell)}\cdots\mathbf{D}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}^{(1)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(\ell-1)}\left({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(\ell)}-\mathbf{I}\right)\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-1)}\cdots\mathbf{D}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}^{(1)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(\ell-1)}\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-1)}\cdots\mathbf{D}^{(1)}\left(\mathsf{q}^{2n(\ell-1)}\left(\mathsf{Z}_{1}\big/\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}\right)\mathsf{K}_{2}^{2}\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\ell}^{2}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}-\mathbf{I}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}^{(1)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(\ell-1)}\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-1)}\cdots\mathbf{D}^{(1)}\left(\mathsf{q}^{2n(\ell-1)}\left(\mathsf{Z}_{1}\big/\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}\right)\mathsf{K}_{2}^{2}\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\ell}^{2}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}-\mathbf{I}\right)

To proceed as above starting with
\mathbf{X}^{(\ell-1)}\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-1)}
, we need to commute
{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(a)}
for
a\not=1
over past the
{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}
on the right, change it into
{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a-1)}
, and then commute it back.
The following is easy to prove using (
5.3
)-(
5.4
) and (
5.7
)-(
5.10
):

\displaystyle{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(a)}_{1}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{2}

\displaystyle={}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{2}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(a)}_{1}\hbox{ for }a\not=1

\displaystyle{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{2}

\displaystyle={}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}_{2}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\hbox{ for all }a

Thus, there is no issue.
Continuing this process of commuting right, exchanging, and then commuting back, we obtain the desired identity.
∎

5.3.4.
Big corn-on-the-cob of arbitrary length

Let

\left(\mathsf{K}^{2}\mathsf{Z}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}-\mathbf{I}\right):=\left({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}-\mathbf{I}\right)\left(\mathsf{q}^{2n}\left(\mathsf{Z}_{1}\big/\mathsf{Z}_{2}\right)\mathsf{K}_{2}^{2}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}-\mathbf{I}\right)\cdots\left(\mathsf{q}^{2n(\ell-1)}\left(\mathsf{Z}_{1}\big/\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}\right)\mathsf{K}_{2}^{2}\cdots\mathsf{K}_{\ell}^{2}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}-\mathbf{I}\right)

i.e. the right-hand-side of (
5.22
).
Recall that we denote by
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}^{\circ}:=\mathcal{M}_{\ell}[\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})^{-1}]
.
Lemma
5.22
implies that in
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}^{\circ}\big/I_{\mathsf{C}}^{+}
, we have the identity

\mathbf{D}^{\circ}=\left(\mathbf{X}^{\circ}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathsf{K}^{2}\mathsf{Z}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}-\mathbf{I}\right)

We will need to derive a similar identity for products of the entries of
\mathbf{D}^{\circ}
.

Lemma 5.23
.

For
\ell>1
and
k\geq 1
, we have the following identity in
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}^{\circ}\big/I_{\mathsf{C}}^{+}
:

\mathbf{D}_{1}^{\circ}\cdots\mathbf{D}_{k}^{\circ}=\left(\mathbf{X}_{1}^{\circ}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathsf{K}^{2}\mathsf{Z}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{1}^{(1)}-\mathbf{I}_{1}\right)\cdots\left(\mathbf{X}_{k}^{\circ}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathsf{K}^{2}\mathsf{Z}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{k}^{(1)}-\mathbf{I}_{k}\right)

Proof.

We prove this by induction; the case
k=1
is Lemma
5.22
.
Consider

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{1}^{\circ}\mathbf{D}_{1}^{\circ}\cdots\mathbf{D}_{k}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}_{1}^{(1)}\cdots\mathbf{X}^{(\ell-1)}_{1}\left({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(\ell)}_{1}-\mathbf{I}_{1}\right)\mathbf{D}^{(\ell-1)}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{D}^{(1)}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{2}^{\circ}\cdots\mathbf{D}_{k}^{\circ}

We can try to make the same moves as in the proof of Lemma
5.22
: for
a\not=1
, we commute
{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(a)}
all the way to the right, exchange it for
{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a-1)}
, and then commute back.
However, there is a novelty once we encounter
\mathbf{D}^{(a)}_{j}\mathbf{D}^{(a-1)}_{j}
for
j>1
.
By (
5.4
), (
5.6
), (
5.7
), and (
5.11
), we have:

\displaystyle{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(a)}\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(a-1)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(a)}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(a-1)}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(a)}_{1}

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(a)}\mathbf{R}^{-1}\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{21}\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(a-1)}{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}

\displaystyle={}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(a)}\mathbf{D}_{2}^{(a-1)}

Thus, when performing our back and forth moves,
\mathbf{D}^{\circ}_{2}\cdots\mathbf{D}^{\circ}_{k}
remains unchanged.
We then obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{\circ}_{1}\mathbf{D}_{1}^{\circ}\mathbf{D}_{2}^{\circ}\cdots\mathbf{D}_{k}^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathsf{K}^{2}\mathsf{Z}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}_{1}^{(1)}-\mathbf{I}_{1}\right)\mathbf{D}_{2}^{\circ}\cdots\mathbf{D}_{k}^{\circ}

The result follows by
left
multiplying by
\left(\mathbf{X}^{\circ}_{1}\right)^{-1}
and applying induction to
\mathbf{D}^{\circ}_{2}\cdots\mathbf{D}_{k}^{\circ}
.
∎

5.3.5.
The map
\Psi^{\ell}_{\mathsf{Z}}

Recall that for
\ell=1
, by Lemma
4.15
, we have a
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant algebra homomorphism
\Psi^{1}_{\mathsf{Z}}:\mathcal{D}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{0}
.
By combining this with the identity map on
\mathcal{W}
, it is easy to see that
\Psi^{1}_{\mathsf{Z}}
induces a map
\mathcal{A}_{1}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{K})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mathsf{K})
.
Abusing notation, we will also denote this induced homomorphism by
\Psi^{1}_{\mathsf{Z}}
.

On the other hand, for
\ell>1
, we only have a
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant algebra homomorphism going the other direction
\Phi^{\ell}:\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
.
We can modify it to incorporate
\mathsf{Z}
: define
\Phi^{\ell}_{\mathsf{Z}}:\mathcal{D}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
by

(5.37)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\ell}_{\mathsf{Z}}(\mathbf{A})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{X}^{\circ},

\displaystyle\Phi^{\ell}_{\mathsf{Z}}(\mathbf{B}^{-1})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Z}_{1}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}

This rescaling will only play a cosmetic effect in
5.4.2
.
By Lemma
4.10
, we can localize at
\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})
and
\det_{\mathsf{q}}({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)})
to obtain a homomorphism

\textstyle\Phi_{\mathsf{Z}}^{\ell}:\mathcal{D}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\ell}^{\star}\left[\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})^{-1}\right]

Our aim here is to leverage this into a map going the opposite direction between the quantized multiplicative quiver varieties.

Lemma 5.24
.

For
\ell>1
, let

\mathsf{K}_{2}=\cdots=\mathsf{K}_{\ell}=\mathsf{q}^{-n}

We have the following:

(1)

For these parameters,
\Phi^{\ell}_{\mathsf{Z}}
induces a map
\Phi^{\ell}_{\mathsf{Z}}:\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mathsf{K}_{1})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{K})[\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})^{-1}]
.

(2)

Moreover, if
\mathsf{K}_{1}=\mathsf{q}^{k}
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, then the map from part (1) is an isomorphism.
We use
\Psi^{\ell}_{\mathsf{Z}}
to denote the natural map
\Psi^{\ell}_{\mathsf{Z}}:\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{K})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mathsf{q}^{k})
.

Proof.

First, we check that
\Phi^{\ell}_{\mathsf{Z}}
respects the moment map relations once we take the quotient
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}^{\star}[\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})^{-1}]
by
I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}
:

\Phi^{\ell}_{\mathsf{Z}}\left(\mathsf{q}^{-2}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{B}^{-1}\mathbf{A}\left(\mathbf{I}+(\mathsf{q}^{2}-1)\mathbf{d}\mathbf{x}\right)\right)=\mathsf{q}^{-2}{}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}\left(\mathbf{X}^{\circ}\right)^{-1}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}^{\circ}\left(\mathbf{I}+(\mathsf{q}^{2}-1)\mathbf{d}\mathbf{x}\right)

Because the commutation relations between elements of
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
and
\mathcal{W}
are the same regardless of the specific elements of the two algebras, we can proceed as in the proof of Lemma
5.18
.
Namely, we can commute
{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}^{\circ}
past
\mu_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathbf{M})
, apply (
5.30
), and then reverse the commutation.
We then obtain:

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{q}^{-2}{}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}\left(\mathbf{X}^{\circ}\right)^{-1}\underbrace{{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}\mathbf{X}^{\circ}}_{(\ref{Cob0})}\left(\mathbf{I}+(\mathsf{q}^{2}-1)\mathbf{d}\mathbf{x}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{q}^{-2}\left(\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}\big/\mathsf{Z}_{1}\right){}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}\left(\mathbf{X}^{\circ}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{X}^{\circ}\underbrace{{}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(\ell)}\left(\mathbf{I}+(\mathsf{q}^{2}-1)\mathbf{d}\mathbf{x}\right)}_{(\ref{MuEll})}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{K}_{1}^{-2}{}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}=\mathsf{K}_{1}^{-2}\mathbf{I}

Thus, we do indeed obtain a map
\Phi_{\mathsf{Z}}^{\ell}:\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mathsf{C}_{1})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{C})[\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})^{-1}]
.

For surjectivity at
\mathsf{K}_{1}=\mathsf{q}^{k}
, we first note that in this case,
\Phi_{\mathsf{Z}}^{\ell}
covers
\left[\mathcal{M}_{\ell}\big/I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}^{+}\right]^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}
due to Lemmas
5.16
and
5.23
.
Corollary
5.21
then implies that upon localizing at
\{\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ}),\det_{\mathsf{q}}({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)})\}
, the additional localizations at
\{\det_{\mathsf{q}}({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(a)}),\det_{\mathsf{q}}({}^{R}\mathbf{Y}^{(a)})\}
in the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{K})
are superfluous.
For injectivity, it suffices to consider the restriction of
\Phi^{\ell}_{\mathsf{Z}}
to
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}
because localization is exact:

\Phi^{\ell}_{\mathsf{Z}}:\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}:=\left(\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}\big/I_{\mathsf{K}_{1}}^{\mathrm{IV}}\right)^{\mathcal{U}}\rightarrow\left(\mathcal{M}_{\ell}\big/I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{K}}^{+}\right)^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}=:\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{+}

It is obvious that
\Phi^{\ell}_{\mathsf{Z}}
respects the integral forms.
Recall the grading on
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}
from Proposition
5.17
; by that proposition, each graded piece is finite-dimensional.
By (
5.23
), it then suffices to show that each graded piece of the kernel has dimension zero once
\mathsf{q}\mapsto 1
.
Combining the isomorphisms from Lemma
5.16
and Proposition
5.17
, we have the following commutative diagram:

\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}
\mathrm{gr}_{F^{\mathcal{D}}}\left(\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{+}\big|_{\mathsf{q}=1}\right)
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{\Sigma_{n}}
\mathcal{R}_{\ell}^{\Sigma_{n}}
\cong
\cong
\Phi^{\ell}_{\mathsf{Z}}
f

The induced map
f
can be described as follows.
Letting

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}]

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{n},w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}]^{(\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z})^{n}}

then
f
is the restriction to
\Sigma_{n}
-invariants of the homomorphism
\mathcal{R}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{R}_{\ell}
where

\displaystyle x_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto z_{i}^{\ell},

\displaystyle y_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto z_{i}w_{i}

This latter homomorphism is injective; thus,
f
and
\Phi^{\ell}_{\mathsf{Z}}
are injective as well.
∎

5.4.
Radial parts

For the rest of this section, we set the
\mathsf{K}
-parameter as

(5.38)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Q}^{k}

\displaystyle:=(\mathsf{q}^{k},\mathsf{q}^{-n},\ldots,\mathsf{q}^{-n})

\displaystyle\mathsf{T}

\displaystyle:=(\mathsf{q}^{-1}\mathsf{t},\mathsf{q}^{-n},\ldots,\mathsf{q}^{-n})

The relationship between
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{T})
to
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
is facilitated by Etingof–Kirillov’s realization of Macdonald polynomials via
intertwiners
.
Namely, we relate the latter to a function representation for
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{T})
.
To establish an isomorphism between the two algebras, we can use Lemma
5.16
(1) to reduce the problem to showing the image of
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{Q}^{k})
under the action on intertwiners contains the image of
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})
under its action on symmetric polynomials.

5.4.1.
Etingof-Kirillov theory at
\mathsf{t}=\mathsf{q}^{k}

Recall the quantum symmetric power
S_{\mathsf{q}}^{m}\mathbb{V}
from
4.4.2
.
For
k\geq 1
, let

U_{k}:=S_{\mathsf{q}}^{n(k-1)}\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}^{-(k-1)}}

We note that the zero weight space
U_{k}[0]
is one-dimensional; let us make a choice of spanning vector
u\in U_{k}[0]
.
For a representation
V\in\mathfrak{C}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, we denote by
I(V,U_{k})
the space of
U_{k}
-
intertwiners
:

I(V,U_{k}):=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{U}}(V,V\otimes U_{k})

For
\mu\in P
and
\Phi\in I(V,U_{k})
, we can take the
\mathsf{q}^{\mu}
-weighted trace:

\mathrm{tr}_{V}\left(\Phi(\mathsf{q}^{\mu}\cdot-)\right)\in U_{k}[0]

Using our fixed spanning vector
u\in U_{k}[0]
, we can identify this trace with an element of
\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})
.
Letting
\mu
vary, we obtain a
weighted trace function
.
We can identify a weighted trace function with an element of the ring of Laurent polynomials
\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm 1}]
via the map

x_{i}\mapsto\mathsf{q}^{\langle\epsilon_{i},-\rangle}

Finally, recall now the staircase partition
\delta=(n-1,n-2,\ldots,1,0)
.
We set

V_{\lambda}^{k}:=V_{\lambda+(k-1)\delta}

Theorem 5.25

(
[
EK94
]
)
.

Let
k\geq 1
.
We have the following:

(1)

For
\lambda\in P^{+}
, the space
I(V^{k}_{\lambda},U_{k})
is one-dimensional.
There exists a unique intertwiner
\Phi_{\lambda}^{k}\in I(V^{k}_{\lambda},U_{k})
such that for any choice of highest weight vector
v_{\lambda+k\delta}\in V^{k}_{\lambda}
,

\Phi_{\lambda}^{k}(v_{\lambda+k\delta})=v_{\lambda+k\delta}\otimes u+\hbox{ {\rm terms of lower weight in} }V^{k}_{\lambda}

(2)

Let
\varphi^{k}_{\lambda}
denote the weighted trace function of
\Phi_{\lambda}^{k}
.
Then we have

P_{\lambda}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})=\frac{\varphi_{\lambda}^{k}}{\varphi_{0}^{k}}

It follows from Theorem
5.25
(2) that for fixed
k
, a
U_{k}
-intertwiner is determined by its weighted trace function.
This gives a realization of Macdonald polynomials (and thus symmetric Laurent polynomials) that directly interacts with our rings of quantum differential operators.
Specifically, to a
U_{k}
-intertwiner
\Phi\in I(V,U_{k})
, we associate an invariant vector
{}_{\cup}\Phi\in(V^{*}\otimes V\otimes U_{k})^{\mathcal{U}}
using
\mathrm{qcoev}_{V}
:

{}_{\cup}\Phi:=(1\otimes\Phi)\circ\mathrm{qcoev}_{V}

This is clearly a vector space isomorphism.

Recall that
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and
\mathcal{W}
have their function representations on
\mathcal{O}_{G}
and
S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}
, respectively.
Thus,
\mathcal{M}_{0}
acts equivariantly on the
braided
tensor product of modules
\mathcal{O}_{G}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}
: here, elements of
\mathcal{W}
commute past elements of
\mathcal{O}_{G}
via the
R
-matrix.
Let us denote the representation map by
\mathfrak{R}
.
It follows that
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\mathcal{U}}
preserves the
\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}^{k-1}}
-isotypic component of
\mathcal{O}_{G}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}
, which is precisely
(\mathcal{O}_{G}\otimes U_{k})^{\mathcal{U}}
.

Theorem 5.26

(
[
EK94
,
VV10
]
)
.

For
k\geq 1
, we have the following:

(1)

The action of
\mathcal{M}_{0}^{\mathcal{U}}
on
(\mathcal{O}_{G}\otimes U_{k})^{\mathcal{U}}
descends to an action of
\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mathsf{q}^{k-1})
.

(2)

For
\mathrm{qcoev}_{V_{\mu}}^{\mathbf{A}}(1):=\mathrm{qcoev}_{V_{\mu}}(1)\in(\mathcal{O}_{G}^{\mathbf{A}})^{\mathcal{U}}
, the weighted trace function of
\mathrm{qcoev}_{V_{\mu}}^{\mathbf{A}}(1)\cdot{}_{\cup}\Phi_{\lambda}^{k}
is
s_{\mu}P_{\lambda}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})
, where
s_{\mu}
is the Schur function.

(3)

For
\mathrm{qcoev}_{V_{\mu}}^{\mathbf{B}}(1):=\mathrm{qcoev}_{V_{\mu}}(1)\in(\mathcal{O}_{G}^{\mathbf{B}})^{\mathcal{U}}
, we have

\mathrm{qcoev}_{V_{\mu}}^{\mathbf{B}}(1)\cdot{}_{\cup}\Phi_{\lambda}^{k}=\mathsf{q}^{-|\mu|(n-1)}s_{\mu}(\mathsf{q}^{2(\lambda_{1}+k(n-1))},\mathsf{q}^{2(\lambda_{2}+k(n-2))},\ldots,\mathsf{q}^{2\lambda_{n}}){}_{\cup}\Phi_{\lambda}^{k}

Setting
\mu=\omega_{i}
in part (3), we obtain scaled versions of the Macdonald operators.
Theorem
5.26
(1) gives an algebra homomorphism

\mathfrak{rad}^{0}_{\mathsf{q}^{k}}:\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mathsf{q}^{k-1})\rightarrow\mathrm{End}\left(\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(\mathsf{q})\right)

called the
quantum radial parts map
, which was first defined in
[
VV10
]
.
he following is one of the main results of
[
Wen23
]
:

Theorem 5.27
.

For
k>2n
,
\mathfrak{rad}^{0}_{\mathsf{q}^{k}}
is an isomorphism onto the image of
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})
under the polynomial representation map
\mathfrak{r}
.

5.4.2.
Harish-Chandra isomorphism at
\mathsf{t}=\mathsf{q}^{k}

For
k\geq 1
, Etingof–Kirillov theory allows us to define an action of
\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mathsf{q}^{k-1})
on Macdonald polynomials at
\mathsf{t}=\mathsf{q}^{k}
via its function representation.
By precomposing the action map with
\Psi_{\mathsf{Z}}^{\ell}:\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{Q}^{k-1})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mathsf{q}^{k-1})
, we thus have an action of
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{Q}^{k-1})
on Macdonald polynomials as well.
Thus, we can define a radial parts map at
\ell>0
:

\mathfrak{rad}^{\ell}_{\mathsf{q}^{k}}:\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{Q}^{k-1})\rightarrow\mathrm{End}\left(\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(\mathsf{q})\right)

Our main goal is to show that
\mathrm{im}(\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{q}^{k}}^{\ell})
contains
\mathfrak{r}\left(\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})\right)
.

For
k>2n\ell
, recall that by Lemma
2.10
,
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})
is generated by

(5.39)

\widetilde{S}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]\widetilde{S}\cup\widetilde{S}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[Y_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,Y^{\pm 1}_{n}]\widetilde{S}\cup\widetilde{S}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[D_{1},\ldots D_{n}]\widetilde{S}

Let
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{X^{\circ}})\subset\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
be the subalgebra generated by the entries of
\mathbf{X}^{\circ}
and let
\mathcal{O}_{G}({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)})\subset\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
be the sublagebra generated by the entries of
{}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)}
and
{}^{L}\mathbf{Y}^{(1)}
.
Additionally, let
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{-1})\subset\mathcal{M}_{0}
be the subalgebra generated by the entries of
\mathbf{A}^{-1}
.
From Theorem
5.26
, one can deduce

\displaystyle\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{q}^{k}}^{\ell}\left(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}\right)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{S}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]\widetilde{S}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{q}^{k}}^{\ell}\left(\mathcal{O}_{G}({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)})^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}\right)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{S}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}]\widetilde{S}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{q}^{k}}^{0}\left(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{-1})^{\mathcal{U}}\right)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{S}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[X_{1}^{-1},\ldots,X_{n}^{-1}]\widetilde{S}

Thus, the first two subalgebras in (
5.39
) lie in
\mathrm{im}(\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{q}^{k}}^{\ell})
.

To consider
\widetilde{S}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}]\widetilde{S}
, recall the element
\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}}
(
2.4
).
By Proposition
2.5
(1), we have

(5.40)

\widetilde{S}\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[X_{1}^{-1},\ldots,X_{n}^{-1}]\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}}^{-1}\widetilde{S}=\widetilde{S}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}]\widetilde{S}

By Proposition
2.5
(2), we can extend
\mathfrak{r}(\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}})
to an operator
\mathfrak{R}(\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}})
on
\mathcal{O}_{G}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}
by

\mathfrak{R}(\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}})\big|_{V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\lambda}\otimes S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}}=\left(\prod_{a=1}^{\ell}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\mathsf{q}^{-2(n-i)-1}\mathsf{Z}_{a}^{-1};\mathsf{q}^{2}\right)_{-\lambda_{i}}\right)

Notice that we have set
\mathsf{t}=\mathsf{q}^{k}
in (
2.5
) and incorporated the shift in highest weight
V^{k}_{\lambda}:=V_{\lambda+(k-1)\delta}
.
This extended operator is invertible.
The following is an equality analogous to (
5.40
) but for
\mathrm{im}(\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{q}^{k}}^{\ell})
:

Lemma 5.28
.

Let
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{D}^{\circ})\subset\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
be the subalgebra generated by the entries of
\mathbf{D}^{\circ}
.
We have

\mathfrak{R}(\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}})\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{q}^{k}}^{0}\left(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{A}^{-1})^{\mathcal{U}}\right)\mathfrak{R}(\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}})^{-1}=\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{q}^{k}}^{\ell}\left(\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{D}^{\circ})^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}\right)

Proof.

Since conjugation is a ring homomorphism, it suffices to show that

\mathfrak{R}(\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}})\mathfrak{R}(\mathbf{A}^{-1})\mathfrak{R}(\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}})^{-1}\overset{?}{=}(-1)^{\ell}\mathfrak{R}\left(\Psi_{\mathsf{Z}}^{\ell}(\mathbf{D}^{\circ})\right)

To that end, recall that the entries of
\mathbf{A}^{-1}
lie in
\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{V}^{*}\cong\mathbb{V}^{**}\otimes\mathbb{V}^{*}
.
When they are multiplied to an element of
V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\lambda}\otimes S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}
, the result lies in the direct sum of
V_{\mu}^{*}\otimes V_{\mu}\otimes S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}
where
\lambda-\mu=\epsilon_{i}
for some
i
.
For such a
\mu
, we have:

(5.41)

\mathfrak{R}(\gamma_{\mathbb{Z}})\big|_{V_{\mu}^{*}\otimes V_{\mu}\otimes S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}}\bigg/\mathfrak{R}(\gamma_{\mathbb{Z}})\big|_{V_{\lambda}^{*}\otimes V_{\lambda}\otimes S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}}=\prod_{a=1}^{\ell}\left(1-\mathsf{q}^{-2\lambda_{i}-2(n-i)-1}\mathsf{Z}_{a}^{-1}\right)

We can write the ratio of eigenvaues (
5.41
) in terms of the ribbon element
\nu
.
Specifically, let us define an operator
\mathfrak{R}(\nu)
on
\mathcal{O}_{G}\,\widetilde{\otimes}\,S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}
where for
v^{*}\otimes v\otimes x\in V^{*}\otimes V\otimes S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}
,

\mathfrak{R}(\nu)\cdot\left(v^{*}\otimes v\otimes x\right)=(\nu v^{*})\otimes v\otimes x

Using (
3.8
), we compute:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(\nu)\big|_{V^{*}_{\mu}\otimes V_{\mu}\otimes S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}}\bigg/\mathfrak{R}(\nu)\big|_{V^{*}_{\lambda}\otimes V_{\lambda}\otimes S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{q}^{-\langle\lambda-\epsilon_{i},\lambda-\epsilon_{i}+2\rho\rangle+\langle\lambda,\lambda+2\rho\rangle}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{q}^{2\lambda_{i}+n+1-2i}

In comparison with (
5.41
), we have:

\mathfrak{R}(\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}})\mathfrak{R}(\mathbf{A}^{-1})\mathfrak{R}(\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}})^{-1}=\bigg[\left(1-\mathsf{Z}_{1}^{-1}\mathsf{q}^{-n}\mathrm{ad}_{\mathfrak{R}(\nu)}^{-1}\right)\circ\cdots\circ\left(1-\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}^{-1}\mathsf{q}^{-n}\mathrm{ad}_{\mathfrak{R}(\nu)}^{-1}\right)\bigg]\left(\mathfrak{R}(\mathbf{A}^{-1})\right)

Finally, we apply Lemma A.8 from
[
Wen23
]
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}^{-1}_{\mathfrak{R}(\nu)}\left(\mathfrak{R}(\mathbf{A}^{-1})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{q}^{n}\mathfrak{R}\left(\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{B}^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ad}^{-1}_{\mathfrak{R}(\nu)}\left(\mathfrak{R}(\mathbf{B}^{-1})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{R}\left(\mathbf{B}^{-1}\right)

We thus have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}})\mathfrak{R}(\mathbf{A}^{-1})\mathfrak{R}(\gamma_{\mathsf{Z}})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{R}\left(\mathbf{A}^{-1}\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{Z}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{B}^{-1}\right)\cdots\left(\mathbf{I-\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{B}}^{-1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell}\mathfrak{R}\left(\Psi_{\mathsf{Z}}^{\ell}(\mathbf{D}^{\circ})\right)

by Lemma
5.22
(cf. (
5.37
)).
∎

Theorem 5.29
.

For
k>2n\ell
, the radial parts map induces an isomorphism:

\mathfrak{r}^{-1}\circ\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{q}^{k}}^{\ell}:\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{Q}^{k-1})\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})

Proof.

By Lemma
5.28
,
\mathrm{im}(\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{q}^{k}}^{\ell})
contains
\mathfrak{r}(\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k}))
.
Recall the notation from the proof of Lemma
5.24
:

\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{+}:=\left(\mathcal{M}_{\ell}\big/I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{Q}^{k-1}}^{+}\right)^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}

We have that
\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{q}^{k}}^{\ell}(\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{+})
contains
\mathfrak{r}(\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})^{-})
, and moreover, the filtration
F_{\mathcal{D}}
is sent to the filtration defined in Proposition
2.8
.
Taking the associated graded, Corollary
2.9
gives the opposite inequality to Lemma
5.16
(2), from which the theorem follows.
∎

5.4.3.
Etingof-Kirillov theory at generic
\mathsf{t}

Here, we review the necessary changes to
5.4.1
for considering the case of generic
\mathsf{t}
.
To discuss Etingof–Kirillov theory here, we will need to base change to
\mathbb{K}:=\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
.
We will formally denote
\mathsf{t}=\mathsf{q}^{\alpha}
, although a logarithm for
\mathsf{q}
is unnecessary and all the objects below can be defined in terms of
\mathsf{t}
.
Recall that at
\mathsf{t}=\mathsf{q}^{k}
, we had a vector space isomorphism:

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\pm}_{n}(\mathsf{q})

\displaystyle\cong\left(\mathcal{O}_{G}\otimes S_{\mathsf{q}}^{n(k-1)}\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{-(k-1)}\right)^{\mathcal{U}}

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}^{k})

\displaystyle\mapsto{}_{\cup}\Phi_{\lambda}^{k}\in(V_{\lambda+(k-1)\delta}^{*}\otimes V_{\lambda+(k-1)\delta}\otimes S_{\mathsf{q}}^{n(k-1)}\mathbb{V}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{-(k-1)})^{\mathcal{U}}

Each tensorand on the right-hand-side will need to be replaced.

First, recall the isomorphism from
4.4.3
between
S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}
and a
commutative
polynomial ring
\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[\mathbf{z}_{n}]
.
This allowed us to write the
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant
\mathcal{W}
-action on
S_{\mathsf{q}}\mathbb{V}
in terms of a homomorphism into a ring of
\mathsf{q}
-difference operators:

\mathfrak{qdiff}:\mathcal{W}\rtimes\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}\hbox{\it iff}\,^{+}_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{z}_{n})

The ring
\mathcal{D}\hbox{\it iff}\,^{+}_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{z}_{n})
acts on the
\mathbb{K}
-vector space
W_{\alpha}
spanned by the monomials

\left\{z_{1}^{m_{1}+\alpha-1}\cdots z_{n}^{m_{n}+\alpha-1}\,\middle|\,(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\right\}

(here, we can omit the
\alpha
and just impose that the shift operator
\tau_{z_{i},\mathsf{q}}
multiplies by
\mathsf{t}
).
Precomposing with
\mathfrak{qdiff}
, we obtain a
\mathcal{U}
-equivariant action of
\mathcal{W}
on
W_{\alpha}
.
We note that the zero weight space of
W_{\alpha}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}\mathsf{t}^{-1}}
is one-dimensional.
As in the
\mathsf{t}=\mathsf{q}^{k}
case, we pick a spanning vector
u
.

Next, the finite dimensional irreducible
\mathcal{U}
-module
V^{k}_{\lambda}:=V_{\lambda+k\delta}
will need to be replaced with the
Verma module

M^{\alpha}_{\lambda}:=M_{\lambda+(\alpha-1)\delta}
.
We note that we can define the highest weight of the Verma module in terms of
\mathsf{t}
rather than
\alpha
.
The structures in
3.1.3
can be applied to
M^{\alpha}_{\lambda}
except those involving the coevaluation because it is infinite-dimensional.
Nonetheless, we can still define a weighted trace over
M^{\alpha}_{\lambda}
, which will return a power series.

Theorem 5.30

(
[
EK94
]
)
.

For
\lambda\in P^{+}
, there exists a unique intertwiner

\Phi_{\lambda}^{\alpha}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{U}}(M_{\lambda}^{\alpha},M_{\lambda}^{\alpha}\otimes W_{\alpha}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}\mathsf{t}^{-1}})

such that for a highest weight vector
v^{\alpha}_{\lambda}\in M_{\lambda}^{\alpha}
,

\Phi_{\lambda}^{\alpha}(v^{\alpha}_{\lambda})=v^{\alpha}_{\lambda}\otimes u+\hbox{\rm lower order terms}

Let
\varphi_{\lambda}^{\alpha}
denote its weighted trace over
M_{\lambda}^{\alpha}
.
We then have

P_{\lambda}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})=\frac{\varphi^{\alpha}_{\lambda}}{\varphi^{\alpha}_{0}}

5.4.4.
Harish-Chandra isomorphism at generic
\mathsf{t}

The lack of coevaluation maps for
M_{\lambda}^{\alpha}
forces us to work with intertwiners rather than invariant vectors.
Consider the space

\displaystyle\mathrm{tHom}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\bigoplus_{M\hbox{ {\tiny is highest weight}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}\left(M,M\otimes W_{\alpha}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}\mathsf{t}^{-1}}\right)\bigg/

\displaystyle\left\langle(f\otimes 1)\circ\psi-\psi\circ f\,\middle|\,\begin{array}[]{l}\psi\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{K}}\left(M^{\prime},M\otimes W_{\alpha}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathsf{q}\mathsf{t}^{-1}}\right),\\
f\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{U}}(M,M^{\prime})\end{array}\right\rangle

The relations are analogous to the coend relation (
3.9
).
We also set
\mathrm{Int}^{+}_{\alpha}\subset\mathrm{tHom}_{\alpha}
to be the subspace spanned by the intertwiners
\{\Phi_{\lambda}^{\alpha}\}_{\lambda\in P^{+}}
.

Now, let us address the base change of quantum differential operators.
Let
\mathbb{L}_{\mathsf{q}}:=\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{q})[\mathsf{t}^{\pm 1}]
and recall the parameter
\mathsf{T}
(
5.38
).
We denote by

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{0,\mathbb{L}}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{M}_{0}\otimes\mathbb{L}_{\mathsf{q}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mathsf{q}^{-1}\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}

\displaystyle:=\left(\mathcal{M}_{0,\mathbb{L}}\big/I_{\mathsf{q}^{-1}\mathsf{t}}\right)^{\mathcal{U}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mathsf{q}^{-1}\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{K}}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mathsf{q}^{-1}\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}\otimes\mathbb{K}

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\star}_{\ell,\mathbb{L}}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{M}^{\star}_{\ell}\otimes\mathbb{L}_{\mathsf{q}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{T})_{\mathbb{L}}

\displaystyle:=\left(\mathcal{M}^{\star}_{\ell,\mathbb{L}}\big/I_{\mathsf{Z}\mathsf{T}}\right)^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{T})_{\mathbb{K}}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{T})_{\mathbb{L}}\otimes\mathbb{K}

Proposition 5.31

(
[
Wen23
]
)
.

There exists a subspace
\mathrm{tHom}_{\alpha}^{D}\subset\mathrm{tHom}_{\alpha}
on which
\mathcal{M}_{0,\mathbb{L}}
acts.
This subspace contains
\mathrm{Int}_{\alpha}^{+}
and the action of
\mathcal{M}_{0,\mathbb{L}}^{\mathcal{U}}
preserves
\mathrm{Int}_{\alpha}^{+}
.
Moreover, the action of
\mathcal{M}_{0,\mathbb{L}}^{\mathcal{U}}
on
\mathrm{Int}_{\alpha}^{+}
factors through
\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mathsf{q}^{-1}\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}
.

Identifying
\mathrm{Int}^{+}_{\alpha}
with
\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
by sending
\Phi_{\lambda}^{\alpha}
to
P_{\lambda}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
, we obtain the radial parts maps

\displaystyle\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{t}}^{0}:\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mathsf{q}^{-1}\mathsf{t})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathrm{End}\left(\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})\right)

Proposition 5.32

(
[
Wen23
]
)
.

The radial parts map satisfies the following:

(1)

the image of
\mathrm{qcoev}_{V_{\mu}}^{\mathbf{A}}(1):=\mathrm{qcoev}_{V_{\mu}}(1)\in\mathcal{O}_{G}^{\mathbf{A}}
is

\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{t}}^{0}(\mathrm{qcoev}_{V_{\mu}}^{\mathbf{A}}(1))=\mathfrak{r}\left(\widetilde{S}s_{\mu}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\widetilde{S}\right)

(2)

the image of
\mathrm{qcoev}_{V_{\mu}}^{\mathbf{B}}(1):=\mathrm{qcoev}_{V_{\mu}}(1)\in\mathcal{O}_{G}^{\mathbf{B}}
is

\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{t}}^{0}(\mathrm{qcoev}_{V_{\mu}}^{\mathbf{B}}(1))=t^{-|\mu|(n-1)}\mathfrak{r}\left(\widetilde{S}s_{\mu}(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n})\widetilde{S}\right)

To define a radial parts map for
\ell>1
, we need the following:

Proposition 5.33
.

The map from Lemma
5.24
(1)

\Phi_{\mathsf{Z}}^{\ell}:\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mathsf{q}^{-1},\mathsf{t})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{T})[\textstyle\det_{\mathsf{q}}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})^{-1}]

is injective.

Proof.

Borrowing notation from the proof of Lemma
5.24
, it suffices to show that the restricted map

\Phi_{\mathsf{Z}}^{\ell}:\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}(\mathsf{q}^{-1}\mathsf{t})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{+}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{T})

is injective.
Moreover, the map above is defined for the corresponding algebras defined over
\mathbb{L}_{\mathsf{q}}
, which we denote by

\Phi_{\mathsf{Z},\mathbb{L}}^{\ell}:\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}(\mathsf{q}^{-1}\mathsf{t})_{\mathbb{L}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{+}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{T})_{\mathbb{L}}

Endowing
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\mathrm{IV}}
with a grading from Proposition
5.17
, we only need to check injectivity on each finite-dimensional graded piece.
On the other hand, by Lemma
5.24
(2),
\Phi_{\mathsf{Z},\mathbb{L}}^{\ell}\big|_{\mathsf{t}=\mathsf{q}^{k}}
is injective for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
This implies that on each graded piece, the kernel of
\Phi_{\mathsf{Z},\mathbb{L}}^{\ell}
must be torsion and thus vanishes upon base change to
\mathbb{K}
.
∎

Thus, for
\ell>1
, we can define

\Psi_{\mathsf{Z}}^{\ell}:\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{T})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{0}(\mathsf{q}^{-1}\mathsf{t})

For all
\ell>0
, we define the radial parts map by

\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\ell}:=\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{t}}^{0}\circ\Psi_{\mathsf{Z}}^{\ell}

Theorem 5.34
.

The radial parts map induces an isomorphism

\mathfrak{r}^{-1}\circ\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\ell}:\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{T})\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})

Proof.

The proof that the
\mathrm{im}(\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\ell})
contains
\mathfrak{r}(\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t}))
is similar to the case of
\mathsf{t}=\mathsf{q}^{k}
.
To show that the latter is the entire image, it suffices to show that
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{T})
is generated by the three subalgebras

\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{X}^{\circ})^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}\cup\mathcal{O}_{G}({}^{L}\underline{\mathbf{Y}}^{(1)})^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}\cup\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{D}^{\circ})^{\mathcal{U}^{\otimes\ell}}

For that, it suffices to show generation for
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{+}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{T})
.
On each finite-dimesional filtered piece
F_{l_{1},l_{2}}
(cf.
5.2.2
) of
\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{+}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{T})_{\mathbb{L}}
, this is the case for
\mathsf{t}=\mathsf{q}^{k}
.
Generation over
\mathbb{K}
then follows from Nakayama’s Lemma.

For injectivity, consider the map

\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{t},\mathbb{L}}^{\ell}:\mathcal{A}_{\ell}^{+}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{T})_{\mathbb{L}}\rightarrow\mathrm{End}\left(\Lambda_{n}^{\pm}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})\right)

given by precomposing
\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\ell}
with the localization map.
On each filtered piece
F_{l_{1},l_{2}}
, this map projects away the torsion submodule and then applies
\mathfrak{rad}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\ell}
.
Moreover, this map is injective on
F_{l_{1},l_{2}}
at infinitely many evaluations
\mathsf{t}=\mathsf{q}^{k}
, and thus the kernel must be torsion.
∎

Appendix A
Shifted quantum toroidal algebras

In this appendix, we show that
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
is isomorphic to the image of a
shifted quantum toroidal algebra
under Tsymbaliuk’s
GKLO homomorphism

[
Tsy23
]
.
The latter is expected to be isomorphic to a
K
-
theoretic Coulomb branch

[
BFN18
,
FT19a
,
FT19b
]
, and thus we make marginal progress towards the conjecture in
[
BEF20
]
concerning these Coulomb branches.
Our main reference is
[
Tsy23
]
, although note that to be consistent with our DAHA conventions, we have squared the parameters
(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
.

A.1.
Definition of shifted quantum toroidal algebras

For a nonnegative integer
\ell
, the
shifted quantum toroidal algebra

U_{\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t}}^{\ell}(\ddot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{1})
is a
\mathbb{K}
-algebra with generators

\left\{e_{r},f_{r}\right\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}\cup\left\{\psi_{s}^{+}\right\}_{s\geq 0}\cup\left\{\psi_{s}^{-}\right\}_{s\leq\ell}\cup\left\{(\psi_{0}^{+})^{-1},(\psi_{\ell}^{-})^{-1}\right\}

To describe its relations, we place the generators into
currents
:

\displaystyle e(z)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}e_{r}z^{-r},

\displaystyle f(z)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}f_{r}z^{-r},

\displaystyle\psi^{+}(z)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{s\geq 0}\psi_{s}^{+}z^{-s},

\displaystyle\psi^{-}(z)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{s\leq\ell}\psi_{s}^{-}z^{-s}

The relations are then:

\displaystyle\left[\psi^{\epsilon}(z),\psi^{\epsilon^{\prime}}(w)\right]=0,\,\psi^{+}_{0}\left(\psi^{+}_{0}\right)^{-1}=\psi^{-}_{\ell}\left(\psi^{-}_{\ell}\right)^{-1}=1,

\displaystyle\left(z-\mathsf{q}^{2}w\right)\left(z-\mathsf{t}^{2}w\right)\left(z-\mathsf{q}^{-2}\mathsf{t}^{-2}w\right)e(z)e(w)=\left(\mathsf{q}^{2}z-w\right)\left(\mathsf{t}^{2}z-w\right)\left(\mathsf{q}^{-2}\mathsf{t}^{-2}z-w\right)e(w)e(z),

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{q}^{2}z-w\right)\left(\mathsf{t}^{2}z-w\right)\left(\mathsf{q}^{-2}\mathsf{t}^{-2}z-w\right)f(z)f(w)=\left(z-\mathsf{q}^{2}w\right)\left(z-\mathsf{t}^{2}w\right)\left(z-\mathsf{q}^{-2}\mathsf{t}^{-2}w\right)f(w)f(z),

(A.1)

\displaystyle\left(z-\mathsf{q}^{2}w\right)\left(z-\mathsf{t}^{2}w\right)\left(z-\mathsf{q}^{-2}\mathsf{t}^{-2}w\right)\psi^{\epsilon}(z)e(w)=\left(\mathsf{q}^{2}z-w\right)\left(\mathsf{t}^{2}z-w\right)\left(\mathsf{q}^{-2}\mathsf{t}^{-2}z-w\right)e(w)\psi^{\epsilon}(z),

(A.2)

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{q}^{2}z-w\right)\left(\mathsf{t}^{2}z-w\right)\left(\mathsf{q}^{-2}\mathsf{t}^{-2}z-w\right)\psi^{\epsilon}(z)f(w)=\left(z-\mathsf{q}^{2}w\right)\left(z-\mathsf{t}^{2}w\right)\left(z-\mathsf{q}^{-2}\mathsf{t}^{-2}w\right)f(w)\psi^{\epsilon}(z),

\displaystyle\left[e(z),f(w)\right]=\frac{\delta(z/w)}{\left(1-\mathsf{q}^{2}\right)\left(1-\mathsf{t}^{2}\right)\left(1-\mathsf{q}^{-2}\mathsf{t}^{-2}\right)}\left(\psi^{+}(z)-\psi^{-}(z)\right),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sym}_{z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}}\frac{z_{2}}{z_{3}}\left[e(z_{1}),\left[e(z_{2}),e(z_{3})\right]\right]=0,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sym}_{z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}}\frac{z_{2}}{z_{3}}\left[f(z_{1}),\left[f(z_{2}),f(z_{3})\right]\right]=0

where
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\in\{+,-\}
and
\delta(z)
is the usual delta-function

\delta(z)=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}z^{r}

Proposition A.1
.

For any pair of nonzero
\{\mathsf{c}_{+},\mathsf{c}_{-}\}\subset\mathbb{K}^{\times}
, quotient
U_{\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t}}^{\ell}(\ddot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{1})\big/\langle\psi_{0}^{+}=\mathsf{c}_{+},\psi_{\ell}^{-}=\mathsf{c}_{-}\rangle
is generated by

\{e_{0},f_{0}\}\cup\left\{\psi^{+}_{s}\right\}_{s\geq 0}\cup\left\{\psi^{-}_{s}\right\}_{s\leq\ell}\cup\left\{(\psi_{0}^{+})^{-1},(\psi_{\ell}^{+})^{-1}\right\}

Proof.

Unpacking (
A.1
) and (
A.2
), we have:

\displaystyle\left[\psi^{+}_{1},e_{r}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{c}_{+}\left(\mathsf{q}^{-2}+\mathsf{t}^{-2}+\mathsf{q}^{2}\mathsf{t}^{2}-\mathsf{q}^{2}-\mathsf{t}^{2}-\mathsf{q}^{-2}\mathsf{t}^{-2}\right)e_{r+1}

\displaystyle\left[\psi^{-}_{\ell-1},e_{r}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{c}_{-}\left(\mathsf{q}^{2}+\mathsf{t}^{2}+\mathsf{q}^{-2}\mathsf{t}^{-2}-\mathsf{q}^{-2}-\mathsf{t}^{-2}-\mathsf{q}^{2}\mathsf{t}^{2}\right)e_{r-1}

\displaystyle\left[\psi^{+}_{1},f_{r}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{c}_{+}\left(\mathsf{q}^{2}+\mathsf{t}^{2}+\mathsf{q}^{-2}\mathsf{t}^{-2}-\mathsf{q}^{-2}-\mathsf{t}^{-2}-\mathsf{q}^{2}\mathsf{t}^{2}\right)f_{r+1}

\displaystyle\left[\psi^{-}_{\ell-1},f_{r}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathsf{c}_{-}\left(\mathsf{q}^{-2}+\mathsf{t}^{-2}+\mathsf{q}^{2}\mathsf{t}^{2}-\mathsf{q}^{2}-\mathsf{t}^{2}-\mathsf{q}^{-2}\mathsf{t}^{-2}\right)f_{r-1}\qed

A.2.
GKLO homomorphism

Here, we will map
U_{\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t}}^{\ell}(\ddot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{1})
into the ring of
\mathsf{q}
-difference operators on the variables
\mathbf{x}_{n}:=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
.
Let
\tau_{x_{i},\mathsf{q}}
denote the
\mathsf{q}
-shift operator on
x_{i}
:

\tau_{x_{i},\mathsf{q}}(x_{j})=\mathsf{q}^{\delta_{i,j}}x_{j}

We denote by
\mathcal{D}\hbox{\it iff}\,_{\mathsf{q}}^{\circ}(\mathbf{x}_{n})
the ring of
\mathsf{q}
-difference operators with coefficients in
\mathbb{K}(\mathbf{x}_{n})
.
The following was proved by Tsymbaliuk:

Proposition A.2

(
[
Tsy23
]
)
.

For
\mathsf{Z}=(\mathsf{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{Z}_{\ell})\in(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{\ell}
, the assignment

(A.3)

\displaystyle e(z)

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{1}{\mathsf{q}^{-2}-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta\left(\frac{x_{i}}{z}\right)\prod_{a=1}^{\ell}\left(1-\frac{\mathsf{Z}_{a}}{\mathsf{q}x_{i}}\right)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n\\
j\not=i\end{subarray}}\frac{x_{i}-\mathsf{t}^{-2}x_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\tau_{x_{i},\mathsf{q}^{2}}^{-1}

(A.4)

\displaystyle f(z)

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{1}{1-\mathsf{q}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta\left(\frac{\mathsf{q}^{2}x_{i}}{z}\right)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n\\
j\not=i\end{subarray}}\frac{x_{i}-\mathsf{t}^{2}x_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\tau_{x_{i},\mathsf{q}^{2}}

(A.5)

\displaystyle\psi^{\pm}(z)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left[\prod_{a=1}^{\ell}\left(1-\frac{\mathsf{Z}_{a}}{\mathsf{q}z}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\left(z-\mathsf{t}^{-2}x_{i}\right)\left(z-\mathsf{q}^{2}\mathsf{t}^{2}x_{i}\right)}{\left(z-x_{i}\right)\left(z-\mathsf{q}^{2}x_{i}\right)}\right]^{\mp}

defines a homomorphism of
\mathbb{K}
algebras
\mathrm{T}_{\mathsf{Z}}:U_{\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t}}^{\ell}(\ddot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{1})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}\hbox{\it iff}\,_{\mathsf{q}}^{\circ}(\mathbf{x}_{n})
.
In (
A.5
), the
\mp
on the right hand side denotes taking the Laurent series expansion in
z^{\mp 1}
.

We will take a closer look at (
A.3
) and (
A.4
) later.
For now, we note that (
A.5
) shows that the currents
\psi^{\pm}(z)
generate operators given by multiplication with symmetric Laurent polynomials.
To see this, let us take logarithmic generators

\{b_{m}\,|\,m\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}\}

such that

\displaystyle\psi^{+}(z)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\sum_{m>1}b_{m}\frac{z^{-m}}{m}\right),

\displaystyle\psi^{-}(z)

\displaystyle=z^{-\ell}\exp\left(\sum_{m>1}b_{-m}\frac{z^{m}}{m}\right)

Formula (
A.5
) then yields that for
m>0
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{\mathsf{Z}}(b_{m})

\displaystyle=\left(1-\mathsf{t}^{-2m}\right)\left(1-\mathsf{q}^{2m}\mathsf{t}^{2m}\right)\left(x_{1}^{m}+\cdots+x_{n}^{m}\right)-\mathsf{q}^{-m}\left(\mathsf{Z}_{1}^{m}+\cdots+\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}^{m}\right)

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{\mathsf{Z}}(b_{-m})

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{\ell}\mathsf{Z}_{1}\cdots\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}}{\mathsf{q}^{\ell}}\bigg(\left(1-\mathsf{t}^{2m}\right)\left(1-\mathsf{q}^{-2m}\mathsf{t}^{-2m}\right)\left(x_{1}^{-m}+\cdots+x_{n}^{-m}\right)-\mathsf{q}^{m}\left(\mathsf{Z}_{1}^{-m}+\cdots+\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}^{-m}\right)\bigg)

These clearly generate multiplication by power sums.
Finally, we note that
\mathrm{T}_{\mathsf{Z}}(\psi_{0}^{+})
and
\mathrm{T}_{\mathsf{Z}}(\psi_{\ell}^{-})
are constants and thus Proposition
A.1
applies to the image of
\mathrm{T}_{\mathsf{Z}}
.

A.3.
Isomorphism with cyclotomic DAHA

Before comparing
U_{\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t}}^{\ell}(\ddot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{1})
and
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
, we will need some preparatory steps.

A.3.1.
Automorphisms

The following is in 3.7 of
[
Che05
]
:

Proposition A.3
.

The formulas below define a
\mathbb{K}
-algebra automorphism
\tau_{X}
of
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}^{0}_{n}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
:

\displaystyle\tau_{X}(T_{i})

\displaystyle=T_{i},

\displaystyle\tau_{X}(X_{i})

\displaystyle=X_{i},

\displaystyle\tau_{X}(Y_{1}\cdots Y_{i})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{q}^{-i}(Y_{1}\cdots Y_{i})(X_{1}\cdots X_{i})

Additionally, we have a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra anti-involution
\varepsilon
given by:

\displaystyle\varepsilon(T_{i})

\displaystyle=T_{i}^{-1},

\displaystyle\varepsilon(X_{i})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{t}^{n-1}Y_{i},

\displaystyle\varepsilon(\mathsf{t}^{n-1}Y_{i})

\displaystyle=X_{i},

\displaystyle\varepsilon(\mathsf{q})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{q}^{-1},

\displaystyle\varepsilon(\mathsf{t})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{t}^{-1}

Note that
\varepsilon(\widetilde{S})
is still a projector onto the (
\mathsf{t}
-deformed) trivial representation of the Hecke algebra, so
\varepsilon(\widetilde{S})=\widetilde{S}
.
Thus, both
\varepsilon
and
\tau_{X}
restrict to
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{0}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
.

The automorphism
\tau_{X}
is in some sense internal.
We can define the following
Gaussian
in a suitable completion of
\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
:

\displaystyle\gamma_{X}

\displaystyle:=\exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\log(X_{i})^{2}}{4\log(\mathsf{q})}\right),

Proposition A.4

(
[
Che05
,
DFK19
]
)
.

We have
\tau_{X}=\mathrm{ad}_{\gamma_{X}^{-1}}
.

Finally, we note that the image of the cyclotomic
\mathsf{q}
-Dunkl element
\varepsilon(D_{1})
can be written in terms of
\tau_{X}
and hence
\mathrm{ad}_{\gamma_{X}^{-1}}
:

(A.6)

\varepsilon(D_{1})=\bigg[\left(\mathrm{ad}_{\gamma_{X}}^{-1}-\mathsf{Z}_{1}\right)\circ\cdots\circ\left(\mathrm{ad}_{\gamma_{X}}^{-1}-\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}\right)\bigg]\left(\mathsf{t}^{1-n}Y_{1}^{-1}\right)

A.3.2.
\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
and GKLO

We need one more preparatory result.
The following is an analogue of Proposition
A.1
.

Proposition A.5
.

\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})
is generated by

\left\{\widetilde{S}X_{1}\widetilde{S},\,\widetilde{S}D_{1}\widetilde{S}\right\}\cup\widetilde{S}\mathbb{K}\left[Y_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,Y_{n}^{\pm 1}\right]\widetilde{S}

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma 5.2 of
[
FFJ+11
]
.
Here, instead we try to generate
P_{a,k,b}
from (
2.10
).
∎

Theorem A.6
.

For
\mathsf{Z}=(\mathsf{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{Z}_{\ell})\in(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{\ell}
, let
\mathsf{Z}^{-1}:=(\mathsf{Z}_{1}^{-1},\ldots,\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}^{-1})
.
The polynomial representation
\mathfrak{r}
gives an isomorphism:

\mathfrak{r}:\varepsilon\left(\mathcal{S}\ddot{\mathcal{H}}_{n}^{\ell}(\mathsf{Z},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t})\right)\rightarrow\mathrm{T}_{\mathsf{Z}^{-1}}\left(U_{\mathsf{q},\mathsf{t}}^{\ell}(\ddot{\mathfrak{gl}}_{1})\right)

Proof.

It is evident that

\mathfrak{r}\left(\widetilde{S}\mathbb{K}[X_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,X_{n}^{\pm 1}]\right)=\mathrm{T}_{\mathsf{Z}^{-1}}\left(\langle\psi_{r}^{+},\psi_{\ell-r}^{-}\,|\,r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\rangle\right)

Thus, by Propositions
A.1
and
A.5
, it suffices to show that

(A.7)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}\left(\widetilde{S}Y_{1}\widetilde{S}\right)

\displaystyle=C_{1}\mathrm{T}_{\mathsf{Z}^{-1}}(f_{0})

(A.8)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}\left(\widetilde{S}\varepsilon(D_{1})\widetilde{S}\right)

\displaystyle=C_{2}\mathrm{T}_{\mathsf{Z}^{-1}}(e_{0})

for some nonzero constants
C
and
D
.
The first equality (
A.7
) is well known due to the formulas for the first Macdonald operators (cf.
[
Mac15
]
):

\displaystyle\mathsf{t}^{n-1}\mathfrak{r}\left(\widetilde{S}(Y_{1}+\cdots+Y_{n})\widetilde{S}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n\\
j\not=i\end{subarray}}\frac{x_{i}-\mathsf{t}^{2}x_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\tau_{x_{i},\mathsf{q}^{2}}

(A.9)

\displaystyle\mathsf{t}^{1-n}\mathfrak{r}\left(\widetilde{S}(Y_{1}^{-1}+\cdots+Y_{n}^{-1})\widetilde{S}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n\\
j\not=i\end{subarray}}\frac{x_{i}-\mathsf{t}^{-2}x_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\tau_{x_{i},\mathsf{q}^{2}}^{-1}

The formula for
\mathrm{T}_{\mathsf{Z}^{-1}}(e_{0})
is not too far from the right-hand-side of (
A.9
).
By Lemma 2.7 from
[
DFK19
]
, we have

\tau_{x_{i},\mathsf{q}^{2}}\mathfrak{r}(\gamma_{X})=\mathsf{q}x_{i}\mathfrak{r}(\gamma_{X})\tau_{x_{i},\mathsf{q}^{2}}

It follows that
\mathrm{T}_{\mathsf{Z}^{-1}}(e_{0})
is a nonzero scalar multiple of

\bigg[\big(\mathrm{ad}_{\mathfrak{r}(\gamma_{X})}^{-1}-\mathsf{Z}_{1}\big)\circ\cdots\circ\big(\mathrm{ad}_{\mathfrak{r}(\gamma_{X})}^{-1}-\mathsf{Z}_{\ell}\big)\bigg]\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n\\
j\not=i\end{subarray}}\frac{x_{i}-\mathsf{t}^{-2}x_{j}}{x_{i}-x_{j}}\tau_{x_{i},\mathsf{q}^{2}}^{-1}\right)

Equality (
A.8
) then follows from (
A.6
).
∎
